# Sūrah Hūd (Chapter 11) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah of Prophetic Burden and Moral Uprightness**

Hūd is the eleventh chapter of the Qur'an, comprising 123 verses of late Makkan revelation. It takes its name from the Arabian prophet Hūd, sent to the towering, arrogant civilization of ʿĀd, whose dramatic confrontation occupies verses 50–60. In prophetic tradition, Sūrah Hūd carries an extraordinary emotional and spiritual gravity: when Abū Bakr remarked on the premature whitening of the Prophet's hair, the Messenger of Allah ﷺ replied: *“Sūrah Hūd and its sisters have aged me”* (Jāmiʿ al-Tirmidhī 3297). Exegetes note that this holy trembling was specifically induced by verse 112: *“So stand firm as you have been commanded, along with those who have turned to Allah with you, and do not transgress.”* This command demanded unswerving, lifelong uprightness (*istiqāmah*) without bending toward compromise, tyranny, or fatigue. Preceded by Yūnus and succeeded by Yūsuf, Hūd stands as a terrifyingly vivid courtroom of prophetic sacred history, where the covenantal relationship between prophets and their recalcitrant communities is laid bare with uncompromising moral clarity.

**The Year of Sorrow and the Stiffening of Deniers**

Revealed in the agonizing interval of the late Makkan mission following the lifting of the three-year clan boycott and the deaths of Khadījah and Abū Ṭālib (the Year of Sorrow, *ʿĀm al-Ḥuzn*), Hūd addressed a Prophet whose physical sanctuary had dissolved. The Quraysh were actively plotting either his assassination, imprisonment, or exile, while scoffing at the promise of divine resurrection and retribution. Sūrah Hūd speaks directly to this isolation: it consoles the Messenger ﷺ, warning him not to let his breast be constricted when the deniers mock that no physical treasure was dropped on him or no angel arrived in his company (11:12). Instead of offering comforting illusions, the sūrah presents an unblinking historical record: no prophet before him was received with rose petals; every herald of monotheism was branded an imposter, a madman, or a destabilizer by the entrenched economic and political elites of their day.

**The Architecture and Movement of the Discourse**

The structure of Sūrah Hūd is built around a majestic doctrinal frame enclosing six epic narrative suites. The prologue (1–24) opens with the *Alif-Lām-Rā* of the Decisive Book, presenting the universal call to monotheism, exposing the absurdity of those who wrap their chests to hide from God, celebrating the divine guarantee of sustenance for every crawling creature on earth (11:6), and challenging the skeptics to produce ten fabricated sūrahs like it (11:13). The second movement (25–49) presents the longest and most emotionally devastating account of Noah in the Qur'an: the building of the Ark under the mockery of his chieftains, the bursting forth of the subterranean ovens and torrential skies, and the heartbreaking dialogue between Noah and his disbelieving son, who seeks refuge on a mountain only to be swept away by the surging wave, prompting the divine rebuke that an unrighteous offspring is no true part of a prophet's spiritual household. The third movement (50–95) rolls out the successive destructions of ancient civilizations: Hūd and the catastrophic wind that shattered the giants of ʿĀd; Ṣāliḥ and the sacred she-camel hamstrung by the insolent elders of Thamūd; Abraham receiving the angelic messengers bound for Sodom; the terrifying twilight confrontation of Lot with the depraved mob surrounding his guests; Shuʿayb warning the merchants of Midian against fraud and economic injustice; and Moses confronting Pharaoh's tyranny. The fourth movement (96–123) extracts the universal moral laws of history: that God never destroys a town unjustly while its people are actively reforming (*muṣliḥūn*, 11:117), culminating in the thunderous injunction to stand firm (*fa-staqim*, 11:112), to establish the prayer at both ends of the day and in the watches of the night to erase evil deeds (11:114), and sealing the chapter with the serene assurance: *“And to Allah belongs the unseen of the heavens and the earth, and to Him the whole matter will be returned; so worship Him and rely upon Him.”*

**The Philosophy of Unbending Istiqāmah**

The central spiritual argument of Sūrah Hūd is that truth is not evaluated by consensus, nor is prophetic duty conditioned on immediate worldly success. Noah preached for nine and a half centuries and was mocked by his own family; Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot, and Shuʿayb were rejected by the vast majority of their contemporaries. Yet the sūrah declares that they were the true victors, while their persecutors became dry stubble and cursed memories. The believer is commanded not to incline toward the oppressors (*“and do not incline toward those who do wrong, lest the Fire touch you,”* 11:113), but to maintain absolute moral integrity (*istiqāmah*) regardless of the prevailing political or cultural weather.

---

## Sūrah Hūd 11:1

> Alif-Lãm-Ra. ˹This is˺ a Book whose verses are well perfected and then fully explained. ˹It is˺ from the One ˹Who is˺ All-Wise, All-Aware

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Letters That Refuse to Explain Themselves**

Three consonants stand alone before any sentence begins: alif, lām, rāʾ. They form no word, carry no grammatical case, and attach to nothing that follows them. Twenty-nine of the Qurʾān's 114 sūrahs open with letters of this kind, and those twenty-nine draw on only fourteen of the twenty-eight letters of the Arabic alphabet. No explanation of them comes from the Prophet himself, and the absence of one is doing work. An audience that measured a man's worth by his command of speech met, in the first breath of this recitation, the bare material of its own language laid out like unworked stone, and then met a book assembled from that material which it could not match. The same triad heads Yūnus (10:1), Yūsuf (12:1), Ibrāhīm (14:1) and al-Ḥijr (15:1); in al-Raʿd it carries an added mīm (13:1). Repeated across a family of sūrahs, the letters function as a signature rather than a stray ornament, marking a set of chapters that all begin by naming the Book before naming anything else.

**A Structure Set Firm, Then Furnished Room by Room**

Two verbs describe this Book, and their order is the argument. The verses are *uḥkimat* — made firm, finished, set in place like a building whose joints are true — and then they are *fuṣṣilat*, set out in detail, distinguished one from another and given particular application. Exegetes separate the two sharply. Firmness means the text does not contradict itself; detailed exposition means it descends into rulings, promises, threats and narratives that a person can act upon. A book may be coherent and useless. Another may be lavish in detail and self-defeating. This verse claims both properties at once and claims them in the order a builder works: frame first, furnishing after. The same double claim runs through the Qurʾān's account of itself, which calls it *"a Book whose verses are perfectly explained—a Quran in Arabic for people who know"* (41:3) and stakes its divine origin on the absence of internal conflict: *"Do they not then reflect on the Quran? Had it been from anyone other than Allah, they would have certainly found in it many inconsistencies"* (4:82). Firmness is therefore not a literary compliment. It is a claim that can be tested, and the rest of the sūrah tests it against the settled record of destroyed nations.

**Two Names That Bracket the Entire Chapter**

The verse closes on a pair of divine names, *ḥakīm* and *khabīr*, and the pairing is not decorative. *Ḥakīm* names one who places things where they belong and orders them toward an end. *Khabīr* names one whose knowledge reaches inside things, to the hidden motive behind the visible act. Wisdom without interior knowledge would be planning in the dark; interior knowledge without wisdom would be insight with no direction. Together the two names describe the authorship claimed for this Book: it is firm and it is detailed because its author both knows what He is doing and knows what we are doing. The sūrah returns to precisely this ground at its close, where the final line insists that *"your Lord is never unaware of what you do"* (11:123). The bracket matters because everything between the two ends is a procession of destroyed towns, and every one of them fell through a miscalculation about these same two attributes. Their opponents assumed a God too inattentive to notice their concealment and too idle to carry out a threat. Naming the two attributes first answers both assumptions before a single story is told.

**The Chapter That Turned the Prophet's Hair Grey**

A report connects this sūrah to a physical change in the Prophet. Abū Bakr remarked on the whiteness of his hair, and the answer named this chapter among its causes: Hūd, al-Wāqiʿah, al-Mursalāt, ʿAmma yatasāʾalūn and Idhā al-shamsu kuwwirat had turned it grey (Sunan al-Tirmidhī 3297, from Ibn ʿAbbās, called ḥasan gharīb by al-Tirmidhī; a companion form in al-Shamāʾil al-Muḥammadiyyah 42 records Abū Juḥayfa asking the same question and receiving the shorter answer, Hūd and her sisters). Qurtubī preserves Ibn ʿAbbās's judgement that no verse came down harder on the Prophet than the command to hold firm, *"So be steadfast as you are commanded"* (11:112), and the hair report is normally tied to that verse. What greys a man here is not fear of the next world by itself. Hūd stands out for the density with which it narrates destruction inside history: the flood, the blast that left Thamūd lifeless in their own houses, the stones marked and rained on Lūṭ's towns, the cry that flattened Midian. A messenger told to expect that treatment from his own townsmen could not read these pages as literature.

**What Firmness Demands of the Reader**

A text that has been set firm does not bend to suit whoever opens it, and that is where the opening line stops being a boast and becomes a demand. If the verses are placed and free of contradiction, then an apparent difficulty marks the limit of a reader's understanding rather than a flaw in the text, and the honest response is to keep reading instead of amending. The Qurʾān puts the same point in a harder form elsewhere: *"The Word of your Lord has been perfected in truth and justice. None can change His Words"* (6:115), and it presents itself as *"a confirmation of what came before, and an explanation of the Scripture"* (10:37). Firmness also fixes the terms on which the sūrah's argument will run. Its accounts of Nūḥ, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Ibrāhīm, Lūṭ, Shuʿayb and Mūsā are not offered as edifying legend that can be trimmed to taste; they are set down as a closed record with one moral running through all of it. That is why the sūrah can move from three isolated letters straight to the demand for exclusive worship without any bridge. A Book whose joints are true needs none.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:2

> ˹Tell them, O Prophet,˺ “Worship none but Allah. Surely I am a warner and deliverer of good news to you from Him

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Book Set Firm for the Sake of One Sentence**

The first verse described a Book whose verses are made firm and then set out in detail, and this is the first piece of that detail. The whole of it fits in four words: worship none but Allah. Everything the sūrah goes on to do — seven messengers, a procession of destroyed towns, a long account of the Day — works on that sentence, and nothing in the chapter stands outside it. The sequence repays attention because it reverses what a reader expects. A call to worship might reasonably be preceded by an argument for God's existence, a cosmology, or a proof of prophethood. Instead the command comes first and the grounds are supplied as the situation demands. That is the order of a messenger who has arrived in a town, not of a lecturer opening a course. The connection between the two verses is causal rather than merely sequential: the text was made firm and detailed so that this demand could be issued with full authority behind it. Perfection of the Book is in the service of the demand, and the demand is stated before any justification for it.

**Warning First, Good News Second**

The verse names two offices and their order here is unusual. Across the Qurʾān the pairing normally runs the other way: God sends His messenger *"as a deliverer of good news and a warner to all of humanity"* (34:28), and again *"as a witness, a deliverer of good news, and a warner"* (48:8). In those statements the good news leads. Here, and in the one other place where the Prophet states the office in his own voice — *"I am only a warner and deliverer of good news for those who believe"* (7:188) — the warning comes first. The difference tracks the audience. When God describes the mission in general terms, mercy is foregrounded. When a messenger stands in front of a specific town that has just been told to abandon its gods, the danger is the nearer fact. Mekka in this period was not asking for reassurance. It was being asked to surrender the religious economy of its own sanctuary, and a warning placed ahead of the good news is honest about the price of the question being put.

**Neither Office Can Be Dropped**

Joining the two functions protects the message from the two ways it is ordinarily ruined. A messenger who only warns becomes a figure of doom, and the hearer learns to treat him as an enemy rather than an adviser. A messenger who only reassures becomes a source of comfort without consequence, and the hearer concludes that nothing is required of him. The Qurʾān closes both escapes by binding the roles into one person, and it does this not only for this Prophet but for the office itself: *"We do not send the messengers except as deliverers of good news and warners"* (18:56). The reason for the pairing is given in another verse: they were sent *"so humanity should have no excuse before Allah after ˹the coming of˺ the messengers"* (4:165). An excuse is removed only when both halves arrive. A warning unheard leaves the hearer unaccountable; a promise unheard leaves him without hope. Together the two offices produce the single condition under which a person can be judged fairly — fully informed and not coerced.

**"From Him" Fixes the Direction the News Travels**

The last words of the verse do quiet but essential work. The warner and bringer of good news is so *from Him*: the news originates elsewhere and the messenger only carries it. This is the same limit the Prophet states in its plainest form, *"I have no power to benefit or protect myself, except by the Will of Allah"* (7:188). Nothing in the office makes him a source. He cannot manufacture the reward he promises, cannot withhold the punishment he announces, and cannot accelerate either; he can only report accurately, and that accuracy is what his hearers are entitled to test. The preposition also settles an argument Mekka kept pressing, which was whether the messenger could produce whatever sign was demanded of him. If the message comes *from Him*, then its evidence, its timing and its outcome all rest with the sender, and a demand for a treasure or for an accompanying angel is addressed to the wrong person. The office is a channel and not a treasury. That distinction will be restated bluntly later in this same sūrah, when the messenger is told in four words what he is.

**An Unclosed Quotation Left Hanging Over Three Verses**

One feature is easy to miss on a first reading: the quotation mark opens and does not close. The speech begun here runs on through the call to repentance and only reaches its period after the statement about the return to God. The consequence is that the demand for exclusive worship, the promise of provision, the threat of a formidable Day and the assertion of divine power arrive as one unbroken address rather than three separate pronouncements. Nothing in the opening clause can be lifted out and kept apart from what follows, because grammatically there is nothing to lift: the first sentence has no end. This is a small device with a real effect. It welds invitation, reward, threat and power into a single act of speech by a single speaker, so that a hearer who accepts any part of it must encounter all of it. The structure also mirrors the substance. A message that cannot be divided without losing sense is being delivered by a messenger who cannot be divided from his sender. That is also why the sūrah's later reproaches take the shape they do. When Mekka asks for a treasure or an angel, or accuses the recitation of being invented, it is trying to negotiate with a component. The grammar of these verses has already declined the negotiation, and the answer it gives is not an argument but a reminder of where the speech began.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:3

> And seek your Lord’s forgiveness and turn to Him in repentance. He will grant you a good provision for an appointed term and graciously reward the doers of good. But if you turn away, then I truly fear for you the torment of a formidable Day

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Movements, Not a Single One**

The verse gives two instructions in sequence: seek forgiveness, then turn. The order is not decorative. *Istighfār* is a request addressed outward — a person asks that a debt be cancelled. *Tawbah* is a movement of the body and the will — the same person turns around and walks the other way. A request without the turn is a plea that costs nothing; a turn without the request is self-improvement that never settles what is owed. Classical exegesis reads the pair against the specific situation of an idolatrous town: abandoning the idols is itself the seeking of forgiveness for the sin of setting partners beside God, while devoting oneself wholly to Him is the turning. That reading makes the two verbs concrete rather than merely devotional. It also explains why the demand follows the command to worship none but Allah in the previous verse. Worship is the destination; forgiveness and turning are the two steps by which a person actually leaves where he is standing. The Qurʾān joins them again at the very end of revelation: *"then glorify the praises of your Lord and seek His forgiveness, for certainly He is ever Accepting of Repentance"* (110:3).

**What "Good Provision" Really Promises**

The reward offered for repentance is not primarily otherworldly. *Mataʿan ḥasanan* — a good provision, a good enjoyment — belongs to this life, and the commentators take it as sustenance and blessing while a person is still alive. One reading pushes it further and treats the good provision as the quality of the life itself: the believer enjoys what he has because he hopes in God and is content with His decree, while the denier can enjoy only the objects themselves. The contrast is sharpened elsewhere in the Book, where the difference between believers and their opponents is put in terms of hope rather than possession: *"if you are suffering, they too are suffering. But you can hope to receive from Allah what they can never hope for"* (4:104). That reading makes the promise psychologically precise. Repentance does not necessarily increase what a person owns; it changes his relation to what he owns. Provision described as *good* is provision that can be used without being worshipped, and the qualifier is doing more work than the noun.

**An Appointed Term Is Still a Term**

The provision is granted *for an appointed term*, and the term is death. This clause is the hinge of the verse, because it prevents the promise from being read as a guarantee of permanent worldly success. Everything granted here is granted on a lease. The Qurʾān states the same limit in a verse about dying: *"No soul can ever die without Allah's Will at the destined time"* (3:145). A reader who misses the clause hears an offer of prosperity; a reader who catches it hears an offer of a good life inside a fixed span, followed by something else. That is precisely the structure the sūrah will illustrate with its destroyed towns, whose inhabitants were given time and provision and mistook both for a verdict in their favour. The term is also what makes the promise merciful rather than merely material. Because the span is bounded, what a person does inside it has weight; if the span were endless, nothing would need deciding.

**Grace Added on Top of the Wage**

After the provision comes a second, different reward: God will graciously give to the doers of good. The shift of register matters. The first gift was described as provision, something that meets a need; the second is described as grace, something given beyond what the act deserves. Exegetes take this as the reward of the Hereafter, apportioned to the quality of a person's obedience, and they read it as exceeding the deed rather than matching it. The Qurʾān's ordinary language of recompense makes the same point: *"For them are ranks in the sight of their Lord, and forgiveness and a generous provision"* (8:4). A strict wage would be intolerable, since no act of worship equals the blessings already received; grace is what makes the accounting survivable. Placing grace after repentance also answers a quiet objection. A person who has spent years in denial may assume that returning merely cancels the debt and leaves him at zero. The verse says otherwise: the return is followed by a gift, and the gift is not scaled to the years lost.

**A Fear Spoken in the First Person**

The turn in the last clause is abrupt and deliberate. After two promises in God's voice, the sentence switches: *I truly fear for you*. The one speaking is the messenger, and what he expresses is not a verdict but a dread. He has not been told that they will be destroyed; he has been told what destruction looks like, and he is saying what that knowledge does to him when he looks at his own townsmen. This is the emotional register of the whole prophetic corpus, and it is the reason the warnings cannot be read as threats issued from a distance. A man who feared nothing for his audience would be indifferent to them. The Qurʾān elsewhere describes the same posture in physical terms, depicting a messenger *"fearing for a people"* and grieving over them. What makes the formulation powerful here is that the fear is honest about its limits. He fears *for* them, not *from* them; the danger he names is the one they are walking into, not the one they pose to him.

**The Day Named While There Is Still Time**

The verse ends on *the torment of a formidable Day*, and the adjective is chosen with care. The Day is *ʿaẓīm* — enormous, heavy, immense — before it is described as painful. What makes it formidable is not only the punishment but its scale: a gathering in which every concealment fails and no proxy can be produced. Some exegetes, reading the verse against its Mekkan audience, took the formidable Day to include a nearer fulfilment, the day on which the town met catastrophic defeat at Badr, so that the warning carried both a historical and an ultimate referent. Whether or not that double reference is intended, the placement of the phrase is the point. It arrives while the hearers can still act. A threat delivered after the outcome would be information; delivered before it, it is an invitation wearing the shape of a warning. That is the whole logic of the verse compressed: two movements, two gifts, one term, and a named danger with time still remaining to avoid it.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:4

> To Allah is your return. And He is Most Capable of everything.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Four Words That Carry the Whole Case**

This is the shortest verse in the opening of the sūrah and it does the heaviest work. *To Allah is your return* — three words in Arabic, and the entire moral structure of the chapter rests on them. Nothing before this point has established a court, a judge, or an accounting. The command to worship, the call to repentance, the promise of provision, the fear of a formidable Day: each of these presupposes that the story does not end inside a lifetime. This verse supplies the presupposition. Without a return, the promise of reward is sentiment and the threat of punishment is rhetoric, since the wicked who die prosperous would have won. With a return, both become statements about a settled fact that has not yet arrived. The formulation is not peculiar to this sūrah; the Qurʾān repeats it as a refrain, from *"To Him is your return all together"* (10:4) to *"To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth. And to Allah is the final return"* (24:42). Its repetition is itself evidence of its function: it is the axiom on which every other claim in the Book depends.

**A Reversion to Ownership, Not a Journey**

The word translated as return does not describe travel. It describes something going back to where it already belongs. The same root appears in the words a believer says at a death: *"Surely to Allah we belong and to Him we will ˹all˺ return"* (2:156), where belonging comes first and returning second. That ordering is the meaning. A person is not an independent agent who travels to God at the end of his life; he is held property throughout it, and death is the moment the holding becomes visible. The commentators draw the consequence for the Day: what happens there is not the arrival of a stranger before an unfamiliar judge but the accounting of a tenant whose lease has run out. This reading also explains why the verse is placed at the end of a call to worship rather than at the end of a threat. Worship is the acknowledgement of ownership while the lease is still running. The return is that acknowledgement becoming unavoidable.

**Power Is Named Exactly Where Escape Would Be Imagined**

The second clause is a statement of capability, and its placement is strategic. *He is Most Capable of everything* follows immediately on the return, and it closes the two doors through which a hearer might try to leave the argument. The first door is avoidance: if the return can be dodged, hidden, delayed or bargained out of, the whole demand collapses. The second door is incompetence: perhaps the One who commands is willing but unable, in which case the threats are aspirations. Naming absolute capability after naming the return shuts both. The Qurʾān pairs the two ideas repeatedly and in the same order, as in *"To Him belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth. He gives life and causes death. And He is Most Capable of everything"* (57:2), and in the verse that ties capability to interior knowledge: *"Whether you reveal what is in your hearts or conceal it, Allah will call you to account for it... And Allah is Most Capable of everything"* (2:284). That last pairing matters here, because the very next verse of this sūrah is about people who fold their chests to hide what is inside them.

**Why the Address Closes on Capability and Not on Mercy**

A reader might expect the opening address of a sūrah to end on comfort. It ends on power, and the choice deserves a reason. Mercy without capability is a wish; a God who forgives because He cannot punish has not forgiven anything. The sūrah needs its readers to understand that the mercy promised two verses earlier is exercised by a being who could do otherwise. The same logic governs the divine names at the head of the chapter: *All-Wise, All-Aware* describe a knowledge that reaches inside a person, and this verse adds the capacity to act on what that knowledge finds. Nothing in the sūrah's long record of destroyed towns makes sense unless both are true. Every one of those peoples had been warned, had been given time, and had been granted provision; what ended them was not an accident but a decision executed by a power that could execute it. Ending the address on capability is therefore not a threat appended to an invitation. It is the statement that makes the invitation serious.

**One Speech, Four Verses, No Seam**

The closing quotation mark at the end of this verse ends a speech that began two verses before, and the seamlessness is part of the design. The hearer receives a single address containing a command, a description of the messenger's office, a call to repentance, two promises, one threat and two statements about God. Nothing can be detached from the rest without breaking the grammar. That has a practical consequence for how the address must be answered. A person cannot accept the promise of provision and decline the return, or take the comfort of forgiveness and refuse the worship, because the parts were never issued separately. The rhetorical shape matches the theological claim: the message is one thing, delivered by one messenger, from one sender, and it stands or falls as a whole. Mekka's actual response, as the following verses record it, was to try to divide it — to keep the moral prestige of the sanctuary while refusing the exclusive worship, or to demand a treasure as a condition of listening. The structure of these four verses makes that kind of selective hearing impossible from the outset. It also fixes the shape of the sūrah's remainder. Every narrative that follows will end in the same place — a return, an accounting, a decision already taken by a power able to carry it out — and a reader who has taken in these four verses will recognise that ending before it arrives.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:5

> Indeed, they enfold ˹what is in˺ their hearts, ˹trying˺ to hide it from Him! But even when they cover themselves with their clothes, He knows what they conceal and what they reveal. Surely He knows best what is ˹hidden˺ in the heart

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse Begins With an Astonished "Look"**

The verse opens with *alā*, an interjection that means something close to *look* or *do you not see*, and it uses the particle twice — once before the description of the folding and once before the statement about the garments. That doubling is rare, and it sets the tone of the whole verse. The speaker is not presenting information neutrally. He is pointing at something so absurd that it demands attention before it can be described. What is being pointed at is a set of human beings attempting to conceal their thoughts from the being who knows them. The interjection registers the comedy and the tragedy in the same breath. Nothing else in the opening of this sūrah sounds like this: verse 1 is a statement about a Book, verses 2 to 4 are an address, and then suddenly the camera turns on an audience caught in a private gesture. The effect is to move the argument from what God has said to what the hearers are doing while they listen.

**Folding the Chest Over Its Own Contents**

The central image is physical. *Yathnūna ṣudūrahum* — they fold, bend or double their chests. The commentators give the gesture two readings and both are worth keeping. The first is that a person curls inward, hunching the chest over the heart so that what is inside will not leak out through the face or the voice; the posture of a man sitting in a gathering, hearing something he hates, and trying not to show it. The second reading is transmitted from Ibn ʿAbbās and reported in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, who is said to have recited the word in a form meaning *their chests were folded over them*, and who connected the verse to people who disliked exposing themselves to the sky; the sense is a person covering and curling at the same time. The two readings converge on one fact: the concealment is enacted by the body. There is no purely mental hiding here. A thought that a man intends to keep secret produces a posture, and the posture is visible to anyone in the room. The Qurʾān's point is not that the hiding is secret but that it is theatrical.

**Concealment Aimed at the Only One It Cannot Work Against**

The object of the hiding is given as *minhu* — from Him. Not from the neighbours, not from the gathering, not from the Prophet: from God. That single preposition contains the whole indictment. Concealment from other people is ordinary social behaviour and often legitimate; a person is entitled to keep his thoughts from a hostile room. Concealment from God is a category error, because it treats the divine knowledge as located somewhere it can be blocked — behind a wall, outside a tent, on the other side of a curtain. The verse answers the error by escalating: they fold their chests, and He knows; they pull their garments over their heads, and He knows; the knowledge is stated twice, the second time in a form that reaches the interior of the heart itself. The Qurʾān makes the same point about its own knowledge of the unseen, extending it to objects far smaller than a thought: *"Not even a leaf falls without His knowledge, nor a grain in the darkness of the earth or anything—green or dry—but is ˹written˺ in a perfect Record"* (6:59). If a falling leaf is tracked, a folded chest is not a hiding place.

**The Garment Used as a Door**

The second gesture is *yastaghshūna thiyābahum* — they cover themselves with their clothes. Exegetes read this as more than bedding. The garment pulled over the head functions as a door: it shuts out light and, more to the point, sound, so that the recitation cannot be heard. The same combination of gestures appears in Nūḥ's complaint about his own people, and there the sequence is explicit: *"And whenever I invite them to be forgiven by You, they press their fingers into their ears, cover themselves with their clothes, persist ˹in denial˺, and act very arrogantly"* (71:7). Fingers in the ears and a garment over the head are the same act performed at two different scales, and both are refusals of hearing rather than refusals of belief. That distinction is important. These people are not recorded here as having considered the message and rejected it on evidence. They are recorded as having blocked the input. The verse therefore identifies a failure prior to argument, one that no amount of further evidence can repair, because the channel has been closed by the person who needs the evidence.

**A Named Man Behind a General Rule**

The verse is reported to have been revealed about a specific individual, al-Akhnas ibn Sharīq, a man noted for his eloquence whose manner of speaking pleased the Prophet while his heart held hostility. Other exegetes prefer the general reading and take the verse as describing the Makkan idolaters' attitude as a class, and still others restrict it to their concealed enmity toward the Prophet alone. The disagreement is not trivial, because it decides whether the verse is history or a permanent diagnosis. The stronger reading holds both: a named occasion gives the words their edge, and a general application gives them their reach. The occasion is exactly the situation that makes concealment dangerous. A fluent, well-liked man who speaks agreeably and intends harm is harder to guard against than an open enemy, and the person being deceived is the one who must listen to him. That is why the verse moves so quickly from the gesture to the divine knowledge: the only reliable defence against a concealed intention is a knowledge that does not depend on the concealer's face.

**What the Heart Carries Is the Thing That Is Known**

The verse closes by naming the object of divine knowledge precisely: He knows best *what is in the heart* — literally *dhāt al-ṣudūr*, the owner-possessor of the chests, the thing that the chest contains. The idiom is worth holding, because it does not say that God knows the chest. He knows its contents, and He knows them better than anyone, including the person carrying them. The phrase is a standing description across the Book, attached wherever divine knowledge is set against human pretence: *"Whether you speak secretly or openly—He surely knows best what is ˹hidden˺ in the heart"* (13:13), and *"Indeed, Allah is the Knower of the unseen of the heavens and the earth. He surely knows best what is ˹hidden˺ in the heart"* (35:38). Two consequences follow. The first is that no performance is safe. A well-delivered speech with a bad intention behind it is not evaluated on its delivery. The second is subtler and more uncomfortable: a person may not fully know his own motives, and the knowledge that judges him is more accurate than his self-assessment. The verse that follows turns from the heart to the creature that crawls, and the movement is not random. The same knowledge that tracks the fold of a chest tracks the provision of every living thing.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:6

> There is no moving creature on earth whose provision is not guaranteed by Allah. And He knows where it lives and where it is laid to rest. All is ˹written˺ in a perfect Record

**Expanded Commentary**

**Every Moving Thing Has an Address With God**

The verse opens with the widest possible scope: not one creature on earth moves whose provision is not with God. The Arabic *dābba* covers everything that crawls, walks or creeps, and the Qurʾān uses the word elsewhere to include birds and to make a larger claim about animal life: *"All living beings roaming the earth and winged birds soaring in the sky are communities like yourselves. We have left nothing out of the Record"* (6:38). The scope here is deliberately exhaustive, and the exhaustiveness is the argument. If provision is guaranteed for a creature no one has named, in a place no one has visited, then the question of a believer's sustenance is settled before it is asked. The verse is often read devotionally, as reassurance, and that reading is not wrong, but it misses the sharper edge. Provision guaranteed is also provision allotted: the amount, the timing and the route are fixed, and effort changes the route without changing the amount. The Qurʾān states the same fact about creatures that cannot plan at all: *"How many are the creatures that cannot secure their provisions! ˹It is˺ Allah ˹Who˺ provides for them and you ˹as well˺"* (29:60).

**"Upon Allah" Without Making Allah a Debtor**

The phrase translated as guaranteed is *ʿalā Allāh*, literally upon God, and it raised a problem that the commentators address directly. Read literally, an obligation resting upon God would seem to contradict the supremacy of His will: a being who owes something is constrained by the debt. Their resolution is that the obligation is one God has taken on Himself by promise, not one imposed on Him from outside. The distinction is precise and it matters. A self-assumed commitment binds because the promiser is truthful, not because the promiser is compelled. The Qurʾān elsewhere names God as the source of provision without qualification: *"Indeed, Allah ˹alone˺ is the Supreme Provider—Lord of all Power, Ever Mighty"* (51:58), where the title is joined to power precisely to keep the giving free. So the verse asserts a certainty on the creature's side and no compulsion on God's. A believer can rely on the provision absolutely; nobody can demand it as a debt. Both halves are needed, because a promise that could be broken is no comfort and a God who could be forced is not God.

**A Dwelling Place and a Resting Place**

Two locations are then named, and the exegetes read them as a pair of coordinates on a life. *Mustaqarr* is the dwelling, the place where a creature settles; *mustawdaʿ* is the deposit, the place where it is stored. The dominant reading makes the first the loins of the father and the second the womb of the mother, so that the verse traces a creature from before conception to its resting place after death. An alternative takes the dwelling as the womb and the deposit as the place a person eventually occupies in the world. The disagreement is instructive rather than merely technical, because the two readings bracket different spans: one covers the whole journey from origin to grave, the other the visible portion of a life. What both share is the claim that a creature is known at every station of its existence, before it arrives and after it leaves. That is why the two locations are mentioned in a verse about provision. Sustenance is not delivered at random; it reaches a specific creature at a specific address at a specific time, and the address is known.

**Provision Placed Directly After Concealment**

The position of this verse is part of its meaning. The verse before it describes people folding their chests to hide their thoughts from God, and this verse answers them without mentioning them. The same knowledge that finds a concealed intention finds the food of an insect. If nothing in the physical world escapes the record, then the attempt to hide a mental state is not merely futile but incoherent. There is also a practical connection that the sūrah exploits. The Mekkan objection to exclusive worship was in part economic: the sanctuary's idols were tied to pilgrimage, trade and social standing, and abandoning them looked like abandoning a livelihood. A verse that guarantees the provision of every crawling thing removes the ground from that calculation. Renouncing a source of income is frightening only if the income is the actual source. The verse says it is not. Provision comes from God through channels He assigns, and channels can be reassigned. The Qurʾān makes the same promise to those who leave something for His sake: *"and provide for them from sources they could never imagine"* (65:3).

**Nothing Falls Outside the Record**

The verse ends on a Book. *All is in a clear Record* — a kitāb mubīn, plain, manifest, leaving nothing ambiguous. Commentators identify this with the Preserved Tablet, the same reality described elsewhere: *"˹recorded˺ in a Preserved Tablet"* (85:22), while others take it simply as God's knowledge, which amounts to the same claim from a different angle. The same formula appears twice more in almost identical words, and both times it closes a statement about the reach of divine knowledge. In al-Anʿām it follows the falling leaf and the grain in the darkness of the earth (6:59). In Yūnus it follows an assertion that no deed goes unobserved: *"Not ˹even˺ an atom's weight is hidden from your Lord on earth or in heaven; nor anything smaller or larger than that, but is ˹written˺ in a perfect Record"* (10:61). The three occurrences together define what the Record is: not a diary of great events but an inventory of small ones. That is the note on which the sūrah's opening turns from doctrine to history. The verses that follow will describe creation, the delay of punishment and the demand for a miracle, and every one of those subjects is governed by the same fact — nothing is unrecorded, so nothing is unaccounted for.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:7

> He is the One Who created the heavens and the earth in six Days—and His Throne was upon the waters—in order to test which of you is best in deeds. And if you ˹O Prophet˺ say, “Surely you will ˹all˺ be raised up after death,” the disbelievers will certainly say, “That is nothing but pure magic!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Cosmology Brought In Only to Serve the Test**

The verse moves from the making of the universe to the resurrection of the dead in a single breath, and the speed of the move is the point. Creation is not introduced here as a subject to be admired. It is introduced as evidence, and the evidence is aimed at one conclusion: a being who assembled the heavens and the earth can reassemble a body. The sūrah does not pause to describe the process, because description is not what the argument needs. What it needs is the sheer scale of the first act, set beside the smallness of the second. This is why the six days are mentioned at all in a passage about resurrection. A person who doubts that his bones can be gathered has not really grasped what he is already standing on. The Qurʾān presses the same comparison directly elsewhere, asking those who deny the raising whether the one who made the greater cannot make the lesser. The cosmology is therefore instrumental. Strip it out and the verse's second half loses its ground.

**A Throne Over Water Before There Was Any Sky**

The clause *and His Throne was upon the waters* creates an immediate difficulty, and the commentators face it squarely. If the heavens and the earth were still being made, what was the water sitting in, and what was the throne resting on? Rāzī's resolution is that *upon* here does not describe a location in space, since there was no space yet to be located in. The phrase indicates something else: the manner in which the heavens were raised and held without any support beneath them. That reading has a parallel in the Qurʾān's own description: *"It is Allah Who has raised the heavens without pillars—as you can see"* (13:2). The image of water is also the oldest available image of unformed matter, and it appears in the first chapter of Genesis in the same position. A report in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim supplies the traditional frame for the phrase: ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ heard the Prophet say that Allah ordained the measures of creation fifty thousand years before He created the heavens and the earth, and His Throne was upon water (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2653b). The decree precedes the making.

**Six Days and No Seventh of Rest**

The six days recur across the Qurʾān — in al-Aʿrāf, Yūnus, al-Furqān, al-Sajdah, Qāf and al-Ḥadīd — and in most of those places the account continues with God establishing Himself on the Throne (7:54; 10:3; 25:59). Two features of the number matter. The first is that it is a number at all: creation is described as ordered, staged and completed, not as a single undifferentiated act. The second is what does not follow it. The biblical account ends with a day of rest; the Qurʾānic account explicitly refuses fatigue: *"Indeed, We created the heavens and the earth and everything in between in six Days, and We were not ˹even˺ touched with fatigue"* (50:38). The stages are for the creature's comprehension, not the Creator's convenience. That refusal of exhaustion carries directly into the argument about resurrection, since a being who tires might reasonably decline to begin the work a second time. The denial of fatigue removes that excuse before it can be made.

**The Purpose Clause Reorders the Whole Verse**

Everything before the phrase *in order to test* is preparation for it. God created the heavens and the earth — and the stated end is a trial. The clause is precise about what is being measured: *which of you is best in deeds*, not which of you is most numerous, most eloquent, or most fortunate. The formulation is repeated with a variation that sharpens it further, where death and life themselves are described as instruments of the same measurement: *"˹He is the One˺ Who created death and life in order to test which of you is best in deeds"* (67:2). Another verse puts the adornment of the earth in the same frame: *"We have indeed made whatever is on earth as an adornment for it, in order to test which of them is best in deeds"* (18:7). Three passages, one formula. The world is fitted out as an examination room, and the examination grades quality of action rather than volume of outcome. That is why a life that produces little but does it well outranks one that produces much carelessly.

**A Test That Cannot Exceed the One Tested**

The trial is real, and its fairness is guaranteed by a limit stated as a rule: *"Allah does not require of any soul more than what it can afford"* (2:286). Without that limit the verse would describe an arbitrary exposure; with it, the trial becomes a measurement rather than a hazard. The commentators draw a further consequence. Trials are not interruptions of a life but a structural part of it, and a trial met correctly strengthens the person who meets it — in character, in reliance, in the accuracy of his judgement about what matters. Read this way, the test is not merely survivable but useful, which is why the Qurʾān can call hardship a mercy in some places without softening its description of hardship. The two clauses of the verse hold each other in place. The cosmos is vast enough to make the test meaningful, and the limit on the demand makes it just. A reader who takes only one of the two gets either an oppressive universe or a trivial one.

**"Pure Magic" Is the Last Word of an Exhausted Opponent**

The verse ends by rehearsing the response: announce the resurrection and the answer is that this is nothing but pure magic. *Siḥr mubīn* — evident, manifest sorcery — is the standard dismissal across the Qurʾān, applied to Mūsā's signs, to ʿĪsā's, and to this recitation: *"And the disbelievers say of the truth when it has come to them, 'This is nothing but pure magic!'"* (34:43). The word choice deserves attention. Sorcery is what a person calls a sign he cannot explain and does not intend to examine. Calling something magic is not a refutation; it is a decision to stop refuting. The accusation also has a tactical use, since it explains away the evidence without engaging it and preserves the accuser's position in front of his audience. Notice the structure of the verse at this point. The claim about resurrection is attributed to the Prophet, and the dismissal to the disbelievers, and no third voice adjudicates between them in the verse itself. The verdict is left to what follows: the record of peoples who made the same dismissal and were then overtaken by the thing they had called magic.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:8

> And if We delay their punishment until an appointed time, they will definitely say, “What is holding it back?” Indeed, on the Day it overtakes them, it will not be averted from them, and they will be overwhelmed by what they used to ridicule

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Delay Read as a Withdrawal**

The verse describes a specific failure of reasoning, and it names the moment at which it happens. A punishment is promised, time passes, nothing occurs, and the hearer concludes that the promise was empty. The inference feels natural and is entirely wrong, because it treats the passage of time as information about the promise rather than as part of its terms. The verse is explicit that the delay is *until an appointed time* — the delay has a boundary set before it begins. A person who knows a date has been fixed cannot read the interval as a cancellation, yet that is exactly the reading being made. This is the mechanism the Qurʾān calls drawing-on: the extension is granted, the gifts continue, and the recipient mistakes the silence for consent. The verse records the mistake from the inside, in the words of the person making it, which is what gives it its force. We are not told that they reasoned badly; we hear the reasoning.

**"What Is Holding It Back?" Asked as a Proof**

The question is not a genuine enquiry. Nobody who actually wanted to know what was delaying a punishment would ask it in that tone. The commentators are unanimous that it was said in derision, and that its purpose was to demonstrate that the threat was false: if the punishment were real, it would have arrived by now. The question is thus an argument dressed as a question, and it has a public function. Asked in a gathering, it wins the room. The person asking appears confident and the person warned appears foolish, and the audience adjusts its allegiance accordingly. This is the social mechanics of mockery, and the Qurʾān treats it as a serious matter rather than as bad manners. The same demand for haste appears elsewhere in the sūrah's world, where a people challenge their messenger to bring on the very thing he is warning them about. The demand looks like courage and functions as a wager made with someone else's money.

**An Appointed Time Is Set Before the Delay Begins**

The phrase *until an appointed time* does the quiet work of the verse. *Ajal musammā* — a term named, specified, marked down — is the same expression used for the fixed span of a human life and for the fixed term of a community. Its presence here means the delay is not open-ended and therefore not evidence of anything. It also means the delay is itself part of the decree rather than a failure of it. The Qurʾān states the same principle in general terms: *"If Allah were to hasten evil for people as they wish to hasten good, they would have certainly been doomed"* (10:11). The delay is mercy operating on people who are reading it as indifference. That is the uncomfortable core of the verse. Nothing is being withheld out of weakness or indecision; something is being withheld out of forbearance, and the recipient is citing the forbearance as proof that there is nothing to forbear about. The appointed time guarantees that the interval closes.

Two practical consequences follow for anyone reading the verse rather than being described by it. The first is that patience under an unexplained delay is not passivity; it is the correct response to a schedule one has not been shown. The second is that a promise kept late is still kept. The sūrah will spend its middle chapters showing destroyed towns, and in every one of those accounts the delay had a length and the length had an end. Nobody in those towns was ever told the date, and that withholding is what made the interval feel like an acquittal.

**On the Day It Cannot Be Turned Away**

The second half of the verse removes the possibility of a late escape. *It will not be averted from them* — the verb is the one used for deflecting a blow or diverting a calamity, and the verse denies it. On that day the punishment is not merely certain but unstoppable, which forecloses the two responses that ordinarily accompany approaching danger: negotiation and flight. There is no one to negotiate with who has authority over the decree, and no direction in which to run that is outside it. The Qurʾān describes the same closure in the sūrahs of the Hereafter, where the disbelievers' request to be sent back is refused not because the request is unreasonable but because the time for requests has passed. What makes this clause severe is its position. It follows immediately on the mockery, so the reader carries the derisive question into the sentence about the inescapable day and hears both at once.

**Mockery Comes Back Around on the Mocker**

The verse closes with a reversal stated in one clause: they will be overwhelmed by what they used to ridicule. The thing they laughed at becomes the thing that envelops them, and the formula is used repeatedly in the Qurʾān as a standing law rather than a local description. *"˹Other˺ messengers had already been ridiculed before you ˹O Prophet˺, but those who mocked them were overtaken by what they used to ridicule"* (6:10) — the same sentence appears again at 21:41. Three other verses repeat the final clause with only the tense changed: *"and they were overwhelmed by what they used to ridicule"* (16:34; 39:48; 45:33). The repetition is doing argumentative work. Mockery is not treated as a free act with no consequence; it is treated as an investment in a particular outcome, and the outcome arrives on the mocker's side of the ledger. That is why this sūrah, which narrates town after town destroyed after warning, keeps returning to ridicule as the common note. Each of those peoples had a moment in which the warning was funny. This verse identifies the joke as the turning point.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:9

> If We give people a taste of Our mercy then take it away from them, they become utterly desperate, ungrateful

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Taste, Not a Serving**

The verse begins with a word chosen for its smallness. What is given is a *taste* of mercy — a mouthful, enough to know the flavour, not enough to constitute a meal. The Qurʾān uses this verb repeatedly for divine gifts, and the choice is deliberate rather than diminutive. A person who has tasted something has knowledge of it, and knowledge changes the loss. Losing a thing one has never known produces no grief; losing a thing one has tasted produces exactly the grief the verse describes. The image also fixes the scale of the human position. Every blessing available in a lifetime is a sample, and the sūrah's own promise in an earlier verse — a good provision for an appointed term — keeps that limit in view. Read against it, the withdrawal described here is not an injustice but the end of a sample. What the verse condemns is not the grief itself, which is natural, but the conclusion drawn from it. The grief becomes despair, and the despair becomes ingratitude, and that sequence is a moral event rather than an emotional accident.

**Despair as the First Response**

The first of the two descriptions is *yaʾūs* — despondent, cut off from hope. The word denotes not ordinary sadness but the collapse of expectation: a person who has stopped believing that anything better is coming. The commentators locate the cause precisely. The despair arises from a deficit of hope in God, a lack of reliance on Him, and an inability to wait. None of those is a statement about the size of the loss. They are statements about the person's orientation while the loss occurs. A person anchored in God treats a withdrawal as a change in circumstance; a person anchored in the thing itself treats it as a change in his worth. The Qurʾān names the same collapse in a shorter verse: *"When We grant people Our favours, they turn away, acting arrogantly. But when touched with evil, they lose all hope"* (17:83), and again: *"One never tires of praying for good. And if touched with evil, they become desperate and hopeless"* (41:49). The pairing is exact and it exposes the asymmetry. The same person is insatiable in asking and bottomless in despairing.

**Ungrateful Because There Was Nothing to Be Grateful To**

The second description, *kafūr*, is stronger than the English suggests. It is the intensive form of the root behind unbelief: not merely unthankful but actively covering over. The word describes a person who receives a benefit and then behaves as though no benefit had been given. That is the logical shape of the verse's complaint. When the mercy is withdrawn, the person's account of the past is rewritten: the years in which he was given become, in his memory, years of nothing, and the giver disappears from the record. Ingratitude here is not a lapse in manners but a revision of history. The Qurʾān treats it as the ordinary failure of unanchored people and describes its extreme form in a man whose posture is given an image: *"And there are some who worship Allah on the verge ˹of faith˺: if they are blessed with something good, they are content with it; but if they are afflicted with a trial, they relapse ˹into disbelief˺, losing this world and the Hereafter"* (22:11). Standing on a verge means that either wind can push you off.

**A Pattern, Not an Incident**

The verse is written in the conditional, and that generality is what makes it a diagnosis rather than a story. *If We give... then We take away... they become.* No town is named, no individual is singled out, and no period is specified. The Qurʾān repeats the same conditional structure often enough that it functions as a law of the soul: *"If We give people a taste of mercy, they become prideful ˹because˺ of it. But if they are afflicted with an evil for what their hands have done, they instantly fall into despair"* (30:36). Note what that parallel adds. The pride on receiving and the despair on losing are two faces of one error — the person has attached his estimate of himself to his circumstances, so both directions of change move him. The sūrah's own sequence makes the same point by putting the two conditions in adjacent verses, and the reader is meant to see a single character in both. Generality also makes the verse apply forward. Every reader is inside the conditional; nobody is exempt from having tasted something and lost it.

**What the Anchored Person Does Differently**

The verse describes a failure, and the correction is available in the surrounding verses and in the tradition. The commentators attach to this passage a saying the Prophet reported from his Lord: *I am as My slave thinks I am* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 7405). The meaning given to it here is exact: a believer should hold a good opinion of God's acts, including the act of withdrawal, and should expect that what has been taken may return in a better form. That expectation is not wishful thinking but a claim about who is doing the taking. Rāzī puts the practical version plainly — the blessings arrived by God's bounty, so their departure should not produce despair, since the same source remains. The difference between the two people in this verse is not the size of their loss. It is whether they regard the giver as still present. The verse that follows this one describes the opposite case, and between the two the sūrah draws the complete portrait of a person whose inner state is set entirely by his circumstances.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:10

> But if We give them a taste of prosperity after being touched with adversity, they say, “My ills are gone,” and become totally prideful and boastful

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence They Say Out Loud**

The verse reports speech, and the speech is short: *My ills are gone.* Three words carry the entire diagnosis. Not *the hardship has ended*, which would be a statement about circumstances, but *my ills*, which is a statement about a personal condition that has now been removed. The commentators take *al-sayyiʾāt* here as the afflictions themselves — the difficulties that had pressed on the person — and the significance lies in what the sentence leaves out. Nothing in it acknowledges a giver. The relief has arrived and the person describes it as a change in his own state, the way one might describe a fever breaking. This is the precise moment the verse is isolating: not the joy of recovery, which is unremarkable, but the shape of the sentence in which the joy is expressed. A person's grammar under relief reveals his theology more reliably than his grammar under prayer.

**A Claim About Cause, Made Without Evidence**

*My ills are gone* is not only a description; it is an account of causation. The person has concluded that the ills are gone, and the natural completion of that thought is *because of something I did*. The Qurʾān states the completion explicitly in a parallel verse: *"Then when We shower Our blessings upon them, they say, 'I have been granted all this only because of ˹my˺ knowledge.'"* — and then refuses it outright: *"Not at all! It is ˹no more than˺ a test. But most of them do not know"* (39:49). That verse supplies what this one leaves implicit. The claim of personal merit is the ordinary interpretation people place on their own recovery, and the Book names it as a mistake of fact, not merely of manners. The person has been given something and reads the gift as a wage. Read as a wage, the gift obliges him to nothing; read as a gift, it obliges him to gratitude. The whole moral difference between the two verses of this pair turns on which reading a person adopts in the first hour of his relief.

**Prideful, Then Boastful**

Two adjectives follow the speech, and they mark two directions of the same movement. *Fariḥ* describes an inward swelling — delight that has become self-satisfaction. *Fakhūr* describes its outward expression: the person who announces his excellence to others. The order is significant. The inward state comes first and produces the public behaviour, so the boasting is not a separate fault but the visible half of one fault. The Qurʾān treats this pair as the predictable consequence of mercy received without recognition: *"If We give people a taste of mercy, they become prideful ˹because˺ of it"* (30:36), and again *"when We let someone taste a mercy from Us, they become prideful ˹because˺ of it"* (42:48). The preposition in those verses — prideful *because of it* — identifies the mercy itself as the occasion of the pride. The gift becomes the evidence of superiority. That is the mechanism by which a blessing turns into a spiritual liability, and it explains why the Qurʾān so often pairs the description of divine generosity with a warning rather than a celebration.

**An Adversity That Taught Nothing**

The verse specifies the sequence: prosperity comes *after being touched with adversity*. That ordering is the sharpest part of the portrait. This person has already been through the hardship, which means he has already had the experience that ordinarily produces insight. He has been in a position where he could not help himself, and he has come out of it. The relief should have taught him both the fact of his dependence and the identity of the one he depended on. Instead it teaches him the opposite. The commentators put the failure precisely: he will not thank God for removing the difficulty and will not consider that the new blessings can be taken away in their turn. Both omissions are failures of memory. The first forgets the source of the rescue; the second forgets that the rescue was a rescue. A person who cannot hold both facts at once is permanently exposed to the next reversal, because nothing in his account of the world tells him that the current state is temporary.

**One Character in Two Verses**

The verse begins with *but*, and the contrast is with the case described immediately before it: a person given a taste of mercy, then deprived of it, who becomes desperate and ungrateful. The two verses are not two kinds of people. They are one person observed in two conditions, and the point of setting them side by side is that the underlying state never changes. Deprivation produces despair; restoration produces arrogance; and in neither case does the person arrive at a settled view of who is giving and who is receiving. The commentators summarise the deficiency in three terms — insufficient hope in God, insufficient reliance on Him, and impatience — and all three persist through both conditions. The sūrah then draws a line through the middle of the portrait with a single exception, and the exception is defined not by circumstances but by a disposition.

One further detail sharpens the whole picture. The mercy given is described as a taste, and the adversity as a touch. Both words minimise. What is given is a sample, and what is taken is a grazing blow, yet the reaction to each is total: complete despair in one direction, complete self-congratulation in the other. The disproportion between the size of the event and the size of the response is the actual subject of the verse. A person whose whole estimate of reality shifts on a small change in his fortune has made his fortune into his standard of truth. The sūrah's own vocabulary supplies the correction two verses earlier, where provision is promised for an appointed term. A term is a boundary, and a person who holds the boundary in mind neither despairs at a loss nor swells at a gain, because both arrive inside a span he knows will end.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:11

> except those who patiently endure and do good. It is they who will have forgiveness and a mighty reward

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Exception Defined by Two Conditions**

The verse opens with *except*, and the exception is unusually precise. It does not say *except the believers* or *except the righteous*; it names two things the excepted people do, and the two correspond exactly to the two conditions described in the verses before. Patience belongs to hardship; doing good belongs to ease. The person who holds both is the person who has not been moved by either. The commentators make the correspondence explicit: patience refers to times of difficulty, and righteous deeds to times of blessing. That pairing turns the exception into a test with two halves rather than a label. A person who endures well but does nothing with his recovery has satisfied one condition; a person who is generous and active in prosperity but collapses when it ends has satisfied the other. Neither is the person this verse names. The definition is functional, which is why it can be applied honestly — anyone can ask which of the two he actually performed.

**Patience as One Half of Faith**

The weight given to patience here is not local to this sūrah. A saying of ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd, preserved in the works of the early scholars and quoted by Ibn al-Qayyim, divides faith into two halves: one half patience, the other gratitude. The same combination appears in the Qurʾān itself, where the people capable of reading the signs are described in one breath: *"Surely in this are signs for whoever is steadfast, grateful"* (14:5). The pairing is structural rather than ornamental. Faith requires a person to do what has been commanded and to leave what has been forbidden, and both halves require endurance: doing takes it, and abstaining takes it more. The Qurʾān commands patience as a standing obligation and attaches a promise to it that no other virtue carries in the same form: *"O believers! Seek comfort in patience and prayer. Allah is truly with those who are patient"* (2:153). Company is the reward here, not a prize. That is what makes patience a condition of the exception in this verse rather than an admirable extra.

**Forgiveness Placed Before Reward**

Two things are promised, and their order matters. Forgiveness comes first, and the mighty reward comes second. A person carrying an unpaid debt cannot enjoy an incoming payment; the payment is consumed by the debt before it reaches him. Placing forgiveness first acknowledges that every human being arrives at the accounting owing something, and it removes the objection that would otherwise meet any promise of reward — namely, that the deeds offered are not clean enough to be paid for. The Qurʾān makes the same arrangement in its covenants: *"I will certainly forgive your sins and admit you into Gardens under which rivers flow"* (5:12), where the cancellation precedes the gift. For the person described in the two previous verses, the order is also the answer to his problem. His difficulty was not that he lacked blessings but that he could not hold them correctly. Forgiveness covers the years in which he held them wrongly, and the reward then applies to what remains.

**A Mighty Reward, Not a Proportionate One**

The reward is described as *great* — ʿaẓīm — and the adjective is doing work against any attempt to calculate it. Patience is not compensated in proportion to its cost; if it were, the person who suffered most would receive most, and suffering would become a currency. The Qurʾān elsewhere removes the arithmetic altogether: *"Only those who endure patiently will be given their reward without limit"* (39:10). The phrase *without limit* — bi-ghayri ḥisāb — describes a payment that is not counted, and it is applied to patience alone among the virtues. That is a striking allocation. Prayer, charity and pilgrimage all have specified rewards; patience is the one whose reward the Book refuses to quantify. Read against this verse, the *mighty reward* is not a large number but the same uncounted thing described in a shorter phrase. It follows that the exception here is not a trade. Nobody endures hardship in order to purchase an outcome; the reward is attached to a disposition, and the disposition is what is being formed by the enduring.

**Why the Exception Arrives at This Point**

The placement of the verse is part of its argument. It follows two verses describing a person who is destroyed by deprivation and destroyed again by relief, and it interrupts that portrait rather than completing it. Without the exception, the two verses would read as a general statement about humanity — everyone is ungrateful when poor and arrogant when rich. The exception denies that reading. There is a group whose inner state is not set by their circumstances, and the sūrah marks them out at the exact moment the generalisation would otherwise close. This is a recurring method in the Qurʾān: a sweeping description of human failure, then a narrow clause that opens a way out of it. The narrowness is the point. The exception is not a majority and is not easy, and the verse does not soften either fact. It gives two requirements and two promises, and leaves the reader to work out which side of the line he has been standing on.

The exception also sets up the sūrah's long middle section. Everything that follows this verse is narrative: seven messengers, town after town, and in each account a small group that held while the majority collapsed. Nūḥ's handful of believers, Hūd's few, Ṣāliḥ's company, Lūṭ's household, Shuʿayb's followers — every one of those groups is an instance of the exception named here, and every one of them endured a specific hardship without abandoning a specific good. The sūrah therefore states the rule and then demonstrates it eighty times over. That is why the verse can afford to be short. It is not offering a definition of the virtuous in the abstract; it is issuing a description that the following narratives will fill with names, dates and outcomes. A reader who wants to know what patience and good action look like under pressure does not have to imagine them. The sūrah is about to show them.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:12

> Perhaps you ˹O Prophet˺ may wish to give up some of what is revealed to you and may be distressed by it because they say, “If only a treasure had been sent down to him, or an angel had come with him!” You are only a warner, and Allah is the Trustee of All Affairs

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse Addressed to the Messenger's Chest**

Most of the sūrah's early verses speak to the disbelievers or about them. This one turns inward and addresses the Prophet's own interior: a wish he might entertain, and a tightness in his chest. That is rare in the Qurʾān and it changes the register of the passage. We are not being shown an argument here but a strain. The strain has a concrete cause, and the sūrah reports it in the disbelievers' own words. They had asked him to bring a different recitation or to alter the one he brought, as recorded in the preceding sūrah: *"Bring us a different Quran or make some changes in it"* (10:15). They had also demanded that a mountain be turned to gold as proof of his mission, or that angels accompany him to testify. Unable to produce either, he was called a liar and mocked, and the mockery hurt. The verse names the hurt without minimising it, and it names the temptation that the hurt produced — the wish to omit part of the message in order to make the rest tolerable to deliver.

**"Perhaps" That Does Not Mean Perhaps**

The particle *laʿalla* ordinarily carries expectation or possibility, and its appearance here puzzled the commentators, because omitting part of a revelation is not possible for a prophet. Conveying the message in full is one of the mandatory conditions of prophethood, so the possibility cannot be real. Their resolution is that *laʿalla* functions here as a rhetorical intensifier rather than an expression of doubt. The verse is not saying the Prophet might actually do this; it is saying that the pressure toward it was strong enough to be named. The distinction protects two things at once. It protects the Prophet from being described as capable of suppressing revelation, and it protects the reader from treating the verse as a formality. A warning that addressed a non-existent temptation would be empty. Read as intensification, the verse becomes a measure of how heavy the demand on the messenger had become: the pressure to soften the message is named at the exact level it actually reached.

**Two Demands, One Underneath the Other**

The disbelievers' request contains two items, and they are not equivalent. A treasure sent down would be a public proof addressed to the senses — wealth appearing where there was none, which would establish the messenger's standing without requiring anyone to change his beliefs. An angel coming with him would be a witness, an independent authority confirming the claim. The first demand is about status, the second about verification, and together they cover the two ways a claim can be resisted: by doubting it, and by refusing to honour the one who makes it. Notice what both demands share. They ask for evidence that can be consumed without being obeyed. A man who sees a treasure may still decline to worship one God; a man who sees an angel may still decline to abandon an idol. The demands are therefore not really about proof. They are a way of setting the price of listening high enough that it will not be paid, which is why the answer does not engage the price.

**Conveying the Whole Is Not a Matter of Preference**

The temptation named at the start — omitting *some* of what has been revealed — identifies the precise shape of the compromise. Nobody proposes abandoning the message. The proposal is to remove the part that provokes, to keep the moral appeal and drop the condemnation of the gods, so that the recitation becomes bearable to its audience and the messenger's life becomes bearable to him. The Qurʾān refuses this at its root. The messenger has no role in the composition of what he delivers: *"It is not for me to change it on my own. I only follow what is revealed to me"* (10:15). The logic is not that the offending portion is more important than the rest; it is that the message is not his to edit. A courier who removes the sentence his recipients dislike has ceased to be a courier. That is the standard this verse enforces, and it explains why the sūrah's stories of earlier messengers follow shortly afterwards: every one of them faced the same request, and every one of them delivered the whole.

**"You Are Only a Warner" Read as a Relief**

Four words close the first half of the verse, and they are usually heard as a rebuke. They are also, and more immediately, a release. *You are only a warner* — the office has a boundary, and the boundary is not failure. The same sentence appears as a consolation elsewhere: *"You ˹O Prophet˺ are only a warner. And every people had a guide"* (13:7). A person who understands his role as producing belief in others will measure himself by their response, and every refusal will register as his own deficiency. A person who understands his role as delivering the warning measures himself by the delivery. The Prophet's distress in this verse came partly from the first understanding; the sentence corrects it. This is the same clarification he states about himself: *"I am only a man like you, ˹but˺ it has been revealed to me that your God is only One God"* (18:110). The office is a channel with a defined content, and its success is not identical with its effect.

**The Trustee of All Affairs**

The verse ends where the pressure actually sits. *Allah is the Trustee of All Affairs* — *wakīl*, the one entrusted with the management of a matter, the guarantor who holds it on behalf of another. The commentators draw the implication directly: guidance and misguidance are not in the messenger's hands but in God's alone. That single clause dissolves the entire problem the verse began with. The Prophet's distress arose from carrying a responsibility that was never assigned to him — the responsibility for whether his townsmen believed. Once the affair is in the hands of a Trustee, the messenger's duty reduces to accuracy and endurance, both of which are within his control. The Qurʾān makes the same assignment explicit: *"But if they turn away, We have not sent you ˹O Prophet˺ as a keeper over them. Your duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺"* (42:48). The verse therefore does not merely rebuke a wish. It removes the ground the wish was standing on.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:13

> Or do they say, “He has fabricated this ˹Quran˺!”? Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Produce ten fabricated sûrahs like it and seek help from whoever you can—other than Allah—if what you say is true!”
