The verse completes the sentence begun before it, and the completion is remarkably narrow. A messenger addressing a town for the first time might be expected to say many things — about God's nature, about the afterlife, about the moral reform required. Nūḥ says one: worship none but Allah. Everything else is implied by it or follows from it. The commentators read the clause as a call to cast aside the idols, to stop worshipping them, and to obey God alone. That is not a reduction of the message but a compression of it. If the town accepts the sentence, every other command follows as a consequence; if it refuses, no other command can be established. This is the same four-word demand that opened this sūrah, and the sūrah is showing that the demand is not new. It has been the opening statement of every messenger from the first one onward, which is why the Mekkan objection that this teaching is an innovation will not survive the narratives.

**Every Messenger Opens With the Same Sentence**

The formula recurs with near-identical wording across the Qurʾān, and the recurrence is itself an argument. *"Indeed, We sent Noah to his people. He said, 'O my people! Worship Allah—you have no other god except Him'"* (7:59). *"O my people! Worship Allah ˹alone˺. You have no god other than Him"* (23:23). The messengers sent from every direction to the people of al-Ḥijr said the same thing: *"Worship none but Allah"* (41:14). Hūd, addressing the people of ʿĀd, uses the identical clause followed by an almost identical fear: *"Worship none but Allah. I truly fear for you the torment of a tremendous day"* (46:21). The consistency is what the sūrah needs. A single messenger with a novel teaching can be dismissed as an eccentric; a line of messengers all opening with the same sentence, in different towns and different centuries, establishes a tradition rather than an opinion. The sūrah will present that line in the verses that follow, and this verse is its first link.

**A Fear Held by the Messenger, Not a Threat Issued by Him**

The second clause is in the first person and it expresses dread: *I truly fear for you.* The preposition is important. He fears *for* them, not from them, and what he fears is something that will happen to them rather than something he will do to them. This is the same register the sūrah's opening used when the Prophet was told to say that he feared a formidable Day for those who turned away. The formulation does more than describe an emotion. It identifies the messenger's position in the exchange. He is not a prosecutor with a sentence to impose; he is a man who has seen what is coming and is telling the people he lives among. The Qurʾān attributes the same posture to other messengers and treats it as a mark of the office rather than a personal temperament. A messenger indifferent to his audience would have no reason to keep warning them after the warnings stopped working, and Nūḥ's mission will run for centuries.

**Pain Attributed to a Day**

The object of the fear is described as *the torment of a painful Day*, and the adjective is attached to the time rather than to the punishment. The construction is a figure — days do not feel pain — and its effect is to make the day itself a bearer of suffering. Everything in it hurts; there is no part of the day that offers relief. The Qurʾān uses the same construction elsewhere, and in one parallel the adjective is even stronger: *a tremendous day* (46:21). The commentators take the phrase to cover both the punishment that would fall on Nūḥ's people in this world and their punishment after it, and the double reference suits the grammar. A day can be the day of the flood and the day of the reckoning, and the warning is not weakened by the ambiguity. What matters for the hearer is that a specific day is coming, that it has been identified, and that its character is pain.

**The Warning Comes Before the Punishment, and That Is the Mercy**

The sūrah named after Nūḥ states the order explicitly: *"Warn your people before a painful punishment comes to them"* (71:1). The warning precedes the punishment, and the precedence is not incidental. A punishment delivered without warning would be an act of force; a punishment delivered after a warning that was refused is a consequence. The Qurʾān treats the interval between the two as the space in which a person's freedom operates, and the sūrah's opening verses have already described that interval as an appointed term. Nūḥ's mission is the longest such interval in the Book, and the sūrah will report how it was used. The verse therefore ends where every prophetic warning ends: with the danger fully named, the exit fully stated, and the decision left in the hands of the people being warned. The response comes immediately, from the notables of the town, and it does not address the demand.

**Worship Stated Before Law**

The order of Nūḥ's sentence is worth isolating, because the sūrah's whole argument depends on it. He does not begin with prohibitions, with a moral code, or with a critique of his town's behaviour. He begins with worship. Everything else is downstream. A town that worships one God will accept the rest; a town that does not will reject whatever else is proposed, however reasonable it looks. This is why the notables' reply in the next verse is so revealing: they do not engage the demand at all, because the demand is about allegiance and they have already given theirs elsewhere. The sūrah's own opening followed the same order — worship none but Allah, then seek forgiveness, then turn — and the pattern holds across every narrative that follows. Each messenger opens with worship and only then reaches the specific wrong of his town: Hūd to pride, Ṣāliḥ to corruption, Shuʿayb to short measure. The particular reform always rests on the general allegiance, and no reform survives its collapse.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:27

> The disbelieving chiefs of his people said, “We see you only as a human being like ourselves, and we see that no one follows you except the lowliest among us, who do so ˹hastily˺ without thinking. We do not see anything that makes ˹all of˺ you any better than us. In fact, we think you are liars.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Notables Answer, Not the People**

The verse identifies who replies, and the identification is the first thing to notice. The disbelieving chiefs — the *malaʾ*, the council, the people whose opinion sets the terms of public speech — answer before anyone else does. The commentators identify them as the leaders of Nūḥ's society, and the same group answers in the parallel accounts: *"But the chiefs of his people said, 'We surely see that you are clearly misguided'"* (7:60). Their priority is not accidental. A message that reaches a town is first assessed by those with the most to lose from it, and their assessment becomes the town's assessment. The verse thus records the mechanism by which a warning is neutralised before most people have heard it. Nūḥ is not being answered by a considered public debate. He is being answered by the people who control what the public will be permitted to consider. The same group appears in the sūrah's own present: the notables of Mekka, who made the same objections to the same demand.

**"A Human Being Like Ourselves"**

The first objection is that the messenger is a man. *We see you only as a human being like ourselves.* The word *only* does the work — not that he is human, which is uncontested, but that he is nothing more. The objection assumes that a message from God should arrive through a being of a different order, and it appears in identical form across the Qurʾān: *"You are no more than humans like us!"* (14:10), and *"This is only a human like you, who wants to be superior to you. Had Allah willed, He could have easily sent down angels ˹instead˺"* (23:24). The assumption underneath is that proximity disqualifies. A man who grew up in the town, whose family is known and whose trade is ordinary, cannot be the bearer of a divine communication, because the people around him have seen him eat and walk in the markets. The objection is not really about ontology. It is about the inconvenience of being addressed by a peer.

**The Followers' Rank Offered as Evidence Against the Message**

The second objection concerns the followers. *No one follows you except the lowliest among us.* The commentators explain that these were poor people employed in trades considered of low standing, and that their social position was the reason they were despised. The parallel account puts the objection as a question: *"How can we believe in you, when you are followed ˹only˺ by the lowest of the low?"* (26:111). The argument is an appeal to the quality of the audience as proof about the message, and it contains a hidden premise: truth is adopted by the eminent. That premise is worth isolating, because it is doing all the work and it is never defended. If the eminent of a town are the ones most invested in the town's existing arrangements, their adoption of a new teaching would be the surprising event, not their refusal. The verse records the objection without answering it, and the sūrah's later verses will show Nūḥ refusing to dismiss these very people at the notables' request.

**"Hastily, Without Thinking"**

The phrase translated as *who do so hastily without thinking* renders an idiom whose sense is that they followed on the surface, without depth of consideration — the outward appearance of a decision rather than a considered one. The charge is that the followers are impressionable people who attached themselves to a movement for reasons that would not survive examination. It is a common accusation and it is unfalsifiable as deployed here, because any evidence of the followers' sincerity would be read as further evidence of their gullibility. The Qurʾān records Nūḥ's answer to it in the parallel account, and the answer is a refusal to audit other people's interiors: *"And what knowledge do I have of what they do?"* (26:112). Their account is with their Lord. The notables' demand, in substance, is that the messenger certify the quality of his own supporters before the notables will consider the message — a condition that hands them control of the discussion.

**"No Merit Over Us" — An Objection to Equality**

The third clause is directed at both the messenger and his followers, and the commentators note the double address. *We do not see anything that makes all of you any better than us.* No distinction in dress, wealth or standing sets them apart. The objection is about rank, and it reveals what the notables take religion to be: a distinction conferred on distinguished people. On that view a message from God would arrive as an honour awarded to the town's best families, and its arrival among the poor would be evidence of a mistake. The verse is showing the reader the exact shape of the pride the sūrah's opening diagnosed. These men are not disputing evidence. They are disputing the distribution of honour, and they are using the messenger's followers as the ground of the dispute. The sūrah has already stated the opposite principle in verse 24, where the difference between the two parties was framed in terms of sight and hearing — faculties that have nothing to do with rank.

**"We Think You Are Liars" — A Plural Accusation**

The verse closes with a charge in the plural: *we think you are liars*. Nūḥ is one man, and the plural is usually explained as including him and his followers, or as the plural of respect, or as a generalising plural of the kind Arabic uses for a single addressee. Whatever the grammar, the effect is that the accusation covers the whole movement rather than the man. That is consistent with the structure of the verse, which has attacked the messenger's humanity, his followers' rank and their sincerity, and only then their honesty. The charge of lying also has a specific edge in this sūrah, because the same charge was being made in Mekka about the Qurʾān: that it was fabricated. The verse therefore links the ancient town to the present one. The accusation against Nūḥ and the accusation against this Prophet are the same accusation, and the sūrah will answer both with the same record.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:28

> He said, “O my people! Consider if I stand on a clear proof from my Lord and He has blessed me with a mercy from Himself, which you fail to see. Should we ˹then˺ force it on you against your will

**Expanded Commentary**

**He Does Not Answer the Social Argument at All**

The notables raised three objections: the messenger's humanity, his followers' low standing, and their hastiness. Nūḥ's reply addresses none of them directly. He does not defend his followers' intelligence, dispute the description of their rank, or argue that he is more than a man. Instead he restates his own position and then asks a question. The omission is a method. To argue about the social standing of the followers would be to accept the notables' premise that standing is relevant, and the whole dispute turns on whether it is. Nūḥ refuses the frame rather than contesting it. The Qurʾān elsewhere instructs the same procedure: when a people argue about rank, respond with the evidence, not with a counter-ranking. The verse therefore enacts the principle it states. A person who holds a clear proof does not need to win an argument about his followers' occupations.

**A Clear Proof and a Mercy From Himself**

Two things are claimed, and the commentators distinguish them. The clear proof is the clarity and truth of the message itself — the *bayyina* the sūrah described earlier as something a person stands on. The mercy is what God conferred on Nūḥ: prophethood on one reading, guidance and knowledge on another. Both readings converge on the same point, which is that neither the proof nor the mercy is his achievement. The proof comes *from my Lord* and the mercy comes *from Himself*, and the doubling of the source is emphatic. This is the same structure the sūrah established in its second verse, where the messenger described himself as a warner and a bearer of good news *from Him*. The messenger's standing is derivative in every respect, and that is precisely why the objection to his humanity fails. A man can carry a proof he did not produce.

**"Which You Fail to See"**

The clause *and you are blind to it* is short and it carries the whole exchange. Nūḥ does not say the mercy is hidden, mysterious or accessible only to initiates. He says they are blind to it. The verb locates the failure in their perception rather than in the object, and it echoes the sūrah's own parable of the blind and the deaf drawn only four verses earlier. The commentators connect it to the wider Qurʾānic habit of describing those insensible to the message as blind. What makes the clause devastating is its politeness. Nūḥ is not accusing them of lying about the proof; he is telling them they cannot see it, which is a statement about their condition rather than their character. The same formulation is given to this Prophet in identical words: *"Indeed, I stand on a clear proof from my Lord—yet you have denied it"* (6:57). The parallel shows that the sentence is a standard reply, and that the blindness is the standard problem.

**"Should We Force It On You?"**

The rhetorical question that closes the verse is the sūrah's clearest statement of a principle stated elsewhere as a rule: *"Let there be no compulsion in religion, for the truth stands out clearly from falsehood"* (2:256). Nūḥ's question puts the same principle in practical terms. Here is a proof; you cannot see it; do you suppose we could make you see it by force? The implied answer is no, and the reasoning is worth spelling out. Belief coerced is not belief. A person who says the words under pressure has not perceived anything; he has avoided a penalty. Since the entire message concerns what a person actually sees and hears, coercion would destroy the thing it aimed to produce. This is why the messenger's role is limited to delivery, as the sūrah's twelfth verse stated. The office has no enforcement power, and the verse shows a messenger declining the power even in imagination.

**One Formula Used by Three Messengers in This Sūrah**

The phrase *if I stand on a clear proof from my Lord* is not unique to Nūḥ. Ṣāliḥ uses it to his people later in this same sūrah, and Shuʿayb uses it to Midian, and the Qurʾān instructs this Prophet to use it to the Quraysh. Four messengers, four different towns, four different centuries, one sentence. The repetition has an argumentative function the sūrah depends on. It shows that the messenger's claim has a fixed form, and a fixed form can be examined. A person who invents a case as he goes cannot be checked; a person who states the same claim in the same words in every town can be. The notables' objections varied with local circumstance — in one town the complaint was about rank, in another about prayer, in another about commerce — and the reply did not vary at all. That constancy is the sūrah's answer to the charge of fabrication.

**Why the Proof Is Described as Something to Stand On**

The preposition in *I stand on a clear proof* is literal, and it does more work than a metaphor of possession would. A person who *has* a proof keeps it in his pocket and produces it when useful. A person who *stands on* one is supported by it continuously, in every position, including the ones where it is inconvenient. The image also implies visibility. Something you stand on is under you and around you; it is not a private possession. Nūḥ's proof is not an interior conviction he asks his town to trust. It is a ground on which he is standing in public, and the town is invited to inspect it. This is the same claim the sūrah's seventeenth verse made about the person who stands on a clear proof from his Lord, backed by a witness and preceded by the Book of Moses. The two verses use one formula, and the formula defines the messenger's epistemic position: not inspired, not privileged, not mystically informed — simply standing somewhere that can be examined. That is why the notables' answer, which never examines it, is the real content of the exchange.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:29

> O my people! I do not ask you for a payment for this ˹message˺. My reward is only from Allah. And I will never dismiss the believers, for they will surely meet their Lord. But I can see that you are a people acting ignorantly

**Expanded Commentary**

**No Payment Asked**

The verse opens by refusing a payment, and the refusal answers an objection that has not been stated in this sūrah. The commentators supply it: the notables had told Nūḥ that if he wanted them to believe, he should send away the people currently following him, because those people were not fit to stand on the same ground as the town's leading families. The refusal of payment comes first because it establishes the messenger's independence. A man who takes money from his audience speaks for his audience. A man who takes nothing can say what he has been given to say. The Qurʾān makes the same statement through Hūd in almost identical words later in this sūrah: *"O my people! I do not ask you for any reward for this ˹message˺. My reward is only from the One Who created me"* (11:51). The formula is a standard part of the prophetic address, and its function is to remove the suspicion that the messenger has an interest in the outcome beyond the message.

**Why the Source of the Reward Matters**

*My reward is only from Allah.* The word *only* is the operative one. A reward taken from people makes the messenger their employee; a reward taken from God makes him God's messenger, and the two positions produce different speech. The verse is not describing an ascetic preference. It is describing a structural necessity. A person whose livelihood depends on an audience will soften what that audience dislikes, and the sūrah has already named that temptation in its own present, when the Prophet was described as possibly wishing to omit part of what had been revealed to him because of what his hearers said. Nūḥ's statement forecloses the same temptation nine centuries earlier. The reward's source also determines the reward's reliability. An audience can withdraw its payment; the arrangement described here cannot be cancelled by the people being addressed.

**A Demand Nobody Wanted to Say Aloud**

The second half of the verse responds to a request the sūrah does not quote directly, and the commentators preserve it: dismiss the believers. The reason given was social rather than theological. The notables did not object to the followers' arguments; they objected to their presence. The Qurʾān records the same demand being made to this Prophet in explicit terms, with a warning attached: *"Do not dismiss those ˹poor believers˺ who invoke their Lord morning and evening, seeking His pleasure. You are not accountable for them whatsoever, nor are they accountable for you. So do not dismiss them, or you will be one of the wrongdoers"* (6:52). The parallel is exact, and it shows that the demand is a recurring feature of every mission rather than a local grievance. In both cases the request is presented as a practical concession that would make the message more acceptable to the people with the most influence.

**"I Will Never Dismiss the Believers"**

The refusal is absolute and it comes with a particle of emphasis: I am not one to drive away the believers. The verb is strong — to repel, to push away, to expel. Nūḥ is not being asked to demote his followers or to hold a separate gathering for the notables. He is being asked to remove them, and he refuses in terms that do not leave room for negotiation. The refusal is the sūrah's practical answer to the objection recorded in the previous verse. The notables argued that the followers' low standing was evidence against the message; Nūḥ's reply is that he will not trade them for the notables' approval, and that the trade was never his to make. The verse thereby separates two things the notables had fused: the standing of a message and the standing of its earliest adherents. A messenger's truth is not a function of his followers' rank, and his loyalty to them is not a bargaining position.

**"They Will Meet Their Lord"**

The reason Nūḥ gives is not sentimental. He does not say they are good people, or that they have been loyal, or that dismissing them would be unkind. He says they will meet their Lord. The clause relocates the whole question. The believers' standing is not decided by the town's assessment or by the messenger's treatment of them; it is decided at a meeting that will take place regardless. That meeting is also the place where the dismissal would be examined. The commentators draw the implication explicitly: on the Day, these people will make their case against the one who drove them away. The verse therefore answers the notables' demand by pointing past them. Their opinion of the followers is not the relevant opinion, and their request is not addressed to anyone with authority to grant it.

**"A People Acting Ignorantly" — a Verdict With Restraint**

The verse ends with a judgement, and the judgement is measured. *I can see that you are a people acting ignorantly.* The word *jāhilūn* describes people acting from ignorance rather than from settled malice, and it is the same phrase Hūd uses to his people in an identical construction: *"But I can see that you are a people acting ignorantly"* (46:23). The restraint is worth noting after the notables called him a liar. They accused him of falsehood; he accuses them of not understanding. The asymmetry is characteristic of the prophetic register throughout this sūrah. The messenger does not return the charge in kind, and he does not concede it either. He names the condition that produced the demand — a people who could not see why the followers' rank was irrelevant — and leaves the naming to do its work. The next verse then puts a question to them that makes the condition concrete.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:30

> O my people! Who would protect me from Allah if I were to dismiss them? Will you not then be mindful

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question About Protection, Not About Feelings**

The verse is one line long and it converts the dispute into a question of practical exposure. Nūḥ does not ask whether dismissing the believers would be kind, or whether they would be hurt, or whether the notables would keep their word. He asks who would protect him from God if he did it. The question has a single expected answer: nobody. The notables have no standing with God, no influence over the decree, and no capacity to shield a messenger from the consequences of disobeying his sender. By framing the matter this way, Nūḥ moves the discussion from the social ground the notables chose to the ground where the decision actually sits. The Qurʾān states the same fact as a general principle: *"You can never escape ˹Him˺ on earth, nor do you have any protector or helper besides Allah"* (42:31). The verse is that principle applied to a specific proposal.

**The Case They Would Bring Against Him**

The commentators give the question a fuller form: who would prevent God's punishment against me if I drove away people who will, on the Day of Judgment, make a case against me before God? The addition matters because it identifies the second party. A dismissal is not a private administrative act. It has witnesses, and the witnesses are the ones dismissed. On the Day they will speak, and what they will say is that they were removed for the convenience of men who had no authority over them. The verse therefore answers the notables' demand by showing them its full cost. The concession they asked for looks small — send these people away — but it creates a claim that will be presented in a forum where the notables have no voice at all. Nūḥ is not being sentimental about his followers. He is being precise about his own exposure.

**The Messenger's Fear Points Upward**

Two verses earlier Nūḥ said he feared for his people. Here he fears from God. The two fears are not in tension; they are the correct order. A messenger's fear for his audience concerns their fate, and his fear of God concerns his own conduct. The verse shows the second fear operating in real time, on a decision that would have been easy to justify. Dismissing a few poor followers to gain the town's leaders could have been presented as strategy — a temporary measure, a concession for the sake of a greater good, a way to reach more people. The verse refuses the framing entirely. It asks one question: who stands between me and God if I do this? When the answer is nobody, the strategy collapses. This is the sūrah's answer to the temptation described in its twelfth verse, where the Prophet was described as possibly wishing to omit part of the revelation to ease the pressure. The mechanism is the same, and so is the answer.

**"Will You Not Then Be Mindful?"**

The verse closes with the appeal to memory that the sūrah has used before. *Will you not then be mindful?* The verb concerns taking heed and recollecting, and the particle *then* marks the sequence: given what I have just asked you, will you not think? The question is addressed to the notables, and it treats them as capable of answering. Nūḥ is not dismissing them as hopeless; he is putting a question to them that they could work out for themselves. The same formula appears throughout the Qurʾān at points where a demonstration has been given and nothing further needs to be added. Its function is to hand the conclusion to the hearer. A person told what to think can resist; a person asked to think has already begun. The verse's brevity is part of this method — after four verses of argument, the messenger stops arguing and asks a question.

**What the Verse Establishes About Leadership**

Read as a statement about authority, the verse is unusually clear. A leader's obligation runs to the people he leads and upward to the one who appointed him, and the upward obligation governs the downward one. Nūḥ's followers cannot protect him. The notables cannot protect him. Only the one who sent him can, and that protection is forfeited by the very act the notables requested. The verse therefore states a rule that applies well beyond this story: no constituency is worth losing one's standing with God for, and the calculation is not close. The sūrah will illustrate the same rule in every narrative that follows. Each messenger is pressed by the eminent of his town to compromise in a specific way, and each refusal is grounded in the same exposure. What makes Nūḥ's case the model for the rest is that his pressure came from within his own community and lasted for centuries.

**The Answer to Every Proposal of Compromise**

The verse is short enough to serve as a rule, and it answers proposals that will be made in every age. The compromise is never presented as a betrayal. It is presented as a temporary measure for a greater good: send these people away and the leaders will listen; soften this passage and the town will come; accept this condition and the movement will grow. The verse's reply to all of them is a single question. Who protects me from God if I do it? When the answer is nobody, the proposal has no weight left, however attractive its projected results. The sūrah has already stated the same principle in its Mekkan section, where the messenger's distress at the demands of his hearers was answered by four words: *you are only a warner*. The office defines the limits of what may be traded, and nothing the audience offers can extend them. Nūḥ's question is therefore not a moment in an ancient argument. It is the standing reply to every suggestion that a small concession now will produce a larger good later, and it works because it asks about protection rather than about outcomes.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:31

> I do not say to you that I possess Allah’s treasuries or know the unseen, nor do I claim to be an angel, nor do I say that Allah will never grant goodness to those you look down upon. Allah knows best what is ˹hidden˺ within them. ˹If I did,˺ then I would truly be one of the wrongdoers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Four Claims the Messenger Refuses**

The verse is a list of disclaimers, and the list is the argument. Nūḥ does not assert what he is; he denies what he is not. Four denials follow one another: he does not possess God's treasuries, he does not know the unseen, he is not an angel, and he does not say that God will withhold good from the people the notables despise. Each denial answers a specific expectation the notables had brought to the exchange. Together they define the office by subtraction, and the method is characteristic of the Qurʾān's account of prophethood. A messenger is not a superhuman being with special access to wealth, knowledge or nature. He is a man who has been given a message, and every claim beyond that is refused here in advance. The sūrah's twelfth verse made the same point from the other side: *you are only a warner*. This verse lists what the warning does not include.

**The Treasuries: A Refusal of Wealth**

The first denial addresses a demand the sūrah has already recorded in its Mekkan context, where the disbelievers asked why a treasure had not been sent down to the messenger. Nūḥ's people made a related assumption: that a messenger would arrive with visible provision, and that the absence of wealth was evidence against him. The denial is exact. He does not hold the keys to God's stores and cannot produce provision on demand. The Qurʾān states the corresponding fact about God's giving elsewhere: *"How many are the creatures that cannot secure their provisions! ˹It is˺ Allah ˹Who˺ provides for them and you ˹as well˺"* (29:60). Provision is distributed by God through channels of His choosing, and a messenger is not one of the channels. This is also why the sūrah's sixth verse matters: every moving creature's provision is guaranteed by God, which makes a demand that the messenger produce wealth a demand addressed to the wrong person.

**The Unseen: A Refusal of Knowledge**

The second denial is the most consequential. *Nor do I know the unseen.* A messenger who knew the unseen could answer every question put to him, predict every event and settle every dispute, and the absence of that knowledge was one of the charges brought against the Prophet in Mekka. Nūḥ refuses the claim outright, and the refusal is not modesty. It is a statement about the limits of what was given to him. He was given a message, not an information service. The Qurʾān has this Prophet state the same limit in his own words: *"I have no power to benefit or protect myself, except by the Will of Allah. If I had known the unknown, I would have benefited myself enormously"* (7:188). The consequence for the audience is practical. They cannot test the messenger by asking him what will happen tomorrow; they can only test the message he has already delivered, and the sūrah's challenge to produce ten sūrahs was framed on exactly those terms.

**An Angel: A Refusal of a Different Nature**

The third denial answers the objection recorded two verses earlier — that he is only a human being like the rest of them. Nūḥ does not dispute the observation. He confirms it: I do not claim to be an angel. The Qurʾān records the same expectation elsewhere, where peoples ask why an angel was not sent instead: *"Had our Lord willed, He could have easily sent down angels ˹instead˺"* (41:14). The refusal matters because it declines the terms of the debate. The notables wanted a messenger of a different order, on the assumption that a peer could not carry a divine message. Nūḥ accepts the premise about his nature and denies the conclusion drawn from it. A man can carry a proof. That is the whole of the claim, and the sūrah's twenty-eighth verse has already stated it. What the notables are asking for is not more evidence but a different kind of messenger, and no amount of evidence would satisfy that request.

**"Allah Knows Best What Is Hidden in Them"**

The fourth denial concerns the followers, and it returns to the demand that Nūḥ dismiss them. He will not say that God withholds good from people the notables look down on, because he does not know what is inside them. The clause *Allah knows best what is hidden within them* is the same formula the sūrah used in its fifth verse about the contents of the heart, and it does the same work here. The notables' assessment of the followers was based on visible rank. Nūḥ's refusal to endorse that assessment is based on the invisibility of what actually counts. A person's standing with God is not readable from his occupation, and a messenger who ranked people by their social position would be claiming a knowledge he does not have. The verse therefore links two of its four denials: the refusal to know the unseen includes the refusal to know the interiors of the poor.

**"Then I Would Be One of the Wrongdoers"**

The verse closes by turning the disclaimers into a self-accusation. If I said these things — if I claimed the treasuries, or the unseen, or an angelic nature, or the authority to declare that God withholds good from the despised — I would be one of the wrongdoers. The conditional is emphatic and it uses the same vocabulary the sūrah has applied to the destroyed towns. The verse therefore places the messenger inside the same moral universe as everyone else. He is not above the standard he preaches; he is subject to it, and the standard condemns the very claims his opponents wanted from him. That is the sharpest feature of the verse. The notables were asking him to become a wrongdoer in order to satisfy them, and he tells them so. The sūrah's eighteenth verse had already defined the worst wrong as fabricating a lie against God, and every one of the four claims refused here would have been exactly that.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:32

> They protested, “O Noah! You have argued with us far too much, so bring upon us what you threaten us with, if what you say is true.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument Declared Closed**

The verse records the point at which the exchange stops being an argument. The notables do not offer a counter-position, dispute a premise or propose an alternative explanation. They announce that the discussion has gone on long enough. The verb they use carries the sense of prolonging and wearying: you have disputed with us at length. What has wearied them is not the content of Nūḥ's case, which they never address in this verse, but its repetition. He has kept saying the same thing, and they have run out of patience with it. That is a recognisable moment in any dispute. A person who cannot answer an argument can always complain about its length, and the complaint shifts the ground from truth to endurance. The sūrah records it without commentary, and the recording is the commentary: a people's refusal is dated by the moment they stop answering and start objecting to being answered.

**A Complaint About Method, Not Content**

The verse's first clause is worth isolating because of what it omits. Not one of Nūḥ's claims is engaged. He said he stood on a clear proof; they do not examine the proof. He said he asked no payment; they do not dispute it. He said he would not dismiss the believers; they do not argue the point, they simply press the demand again in a different form. Instead the complaint is procedural: too much argument. This is the same pattern the sūrah described in its opening, where the hearers covered their heads with their garments to avoid the recitation. The gesture and the complaint are one behaviour in two registers. A person who will not listen says the speaker is tedious; a person who will not look away says there is nothing to see. The sūrah's parable of the blind and the deaf, given eight verses before this one, describes exactly this state, and the notables have now reached it in their own words.

**"Bring On What You Threaten"**

The second clause is a demand for the punishment to arrive. This is not courage; it is a wager, and the sūrah has already analysed it. Its eighth verse describes the same response in the present: when the punishment is delayed, people say *what is holding it back?* Nūḥ's people are making the same move in an older town. The demand has a public function. Issued in front of the town, it makes the messenger look powerless and the notables look unafraid, and it transfers the burden of proof onto time. If nothing happens, the threat is exposed as empty; if something happens, the notables have at least been seen to invite it. The Qurʾān records the same challenge put to other messengers, and it treats the challenge as a settled pattern rather than a local outburst. The sūrah's answer is given in the verse that follows: only God can bring it, and when He does, there will be no escape.

**"If You Are Telling the Truth"**

The conditional at the end of the verse is the notables' only remaining argument, and it is a weak one. They have not produced evidence that Nūḥ is lying. They have produced a test that they believe he will fail: produce the punishment. The condition is unfalsifiable from his side, because the timing of the punishment was never his to set. The sūrah made this exact point in its Mekkan section, where the messenger was told that he is only a warner and that the affair belongs to God. A demand that the messenger control the schedule is a demand that he be something other than a messenger. The notables are therefore not testing the truth of the message. They are testing whether the messenger has powers he explicitly disclaimed one verse earlier — the treasuries, the unseen, an angelic nature. Having heard those disclaimers, they make a demand that requires all of them.

**What the Demand Reveals**

The verse is the sūrah's clearest illustration of a point it has been making since its opening: ridicule and the demand for haste are the same act. Both are ways of treating a warning as a performance rather than as information. A person who takes a warning seriously asks how to avoid the thing warned of. A person who treats it as a performance asks when the show will start. The notables' question is a question about the show. That is why the sūrah's eighth verse answered the same question with a description of the day on which the punishment cannot be turned away, and why it ended by saying that the mockers would be enveloped by the very thing they mocked. The verse here is the ancient instance of the pattern, and the sūrah places it first in its sequence of narratives so that every subsequent town can be read against it. The next verse answers the demand in a single clause, and the answer refuses the notables' framing entirely.

**Haste as the Last Refuge of an Unanswered Argument**

The demand to hasten the punishment is the final move available to a person who has run out of replies, and the Qurʾān treats it as a diagnostic sign. It appears in other narratives in the same words: *"so bring upon us what you threaten us with, if what you say is true"* is echoed wherever a messenger's people abandon argument. The reason it is a last refuge is that it requires nothing of the person making it. He does not have to explain his position, defend his gods, or account for his conduct. He only has to wait. That is what makes the move so attractive in a public setting and so useless as an argument. The sūrah's eighth verse named the flaw: the delay has an appointed end, and the interval is not evidence. Nūḥ's people are treating a schedule they cannot see as a verdict they can rely on. The verse is also the pivot of the narrative. After it, the exchange stops being verbal. The next stage is the command to build, and the town's mockery of a man constructing a vessel far from water.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:33

> He responded, “It is Allah Who can bring it upon you if He wills, and then you will have no escape

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Answer That Refuses the Framing**

The notables asked Nūḥ to bring the punishment. His reply does not attempt it and does not explain why he cannot; it relocates the matter entirely. *It is Allah Who can bring it upon you.* The emphasis falls on the subject. The messenger is not the agent, has never been the agent, and could not become the agent if he wanted to. One verse earlier he had listed the powers he does not hold — the treasuries, the unseen, an angelic nature — and this verse applies the list to the specific demand in front of him. The reply is therefore not a refusal to cooperate. It is a correction of a factual error. The notables have addressed their challenge to a man who has no part in the thing they are challenging him to produce, and the verse says so in one clause.

**"If He Wills" Ends the Wager**

The condition attached to the statement is the part that dismantles the challenge. *If He wills.* The notables' demand assumed that the punishment was a performance the messenger could give on request, and that its absence would prove the threat empty. The condition removes the assumption by removing the schedule from human hands. The punishment will come if God wills it, when God wills it, and its timing carries no information about its reality. The sūrah has already made this point in its Mekkan section, where the delay of a punishment until an appointed time was described as part of the decree rather than a failure of it. Nūḥ's answer is that same point applied to a specific demand. A wager requires a date. This verse declines to supply one, and in declining it refuses to play.

**No Escape Once It Arrives**

The second half of the verse shifts from possibility to certainty. *And then you will have no escape.* The word is the same one the Qurʾān uses repeatedly to deny flight from the decree: *"They ask you ˹O Prophet˺, 'Is this true?' Say, 'Yes, by my Lord! Most certainly it is true! And you will have no escape'"* (10:53), and *"O my people! Persist in your ways, for I ˹too˺ will persist in mine. You will soon know who will fare best in the end. Indeed, the wrongdoers will never succeed"* (6:135). The clause answers the only rational response the notables have left, which is to prepare. If the punishment cannot be escaped, then demanding it is not bravery but a miscalculation of what is coming. The verse states the fact without elaboration, and the flatness is deliberate: a man who has been told there is no escape and continues to demand the thing has stopped reasoning. The clause also answers the question the sūrah raised earlier about the delay. What holds the punishment back is neither weakness nor indecision; it is a term that has already been set. Knowing that there is no escape when the term ends is the only fact here capable of changing behaviour, and it is given without qualification.

**The Messenger Declines to Be the Instrument**

There is a second reason Nūḥ could not bring the punishment even if he had the power, and the verse implies it. A messenger sent with a warning is not sent as an executioner. His function is to deliver the message and leave the decision with the hearers; if he controlled the punishment, the decision would be coerced, and the sūrah has already ruled coercion out of the matter. *Should we then force it on you against your will?* The reply here is consistent with that question. The messenger's relation to the punishment is entirely one of report. He describes it, warns of it and waits. This is why the sūrah's twelfth verse could tell this Prophet in four words what he is: only a warner. The limitation is not a weakness in the office. It is what makes the warning meaningful, since a warning that can be enforced is not a warning but an order.

**Why the Delay Is Not Explained Here**

The verse does not tell the notables when the punishment will come, and the omission is significant. Nūḥ could have said something about the interval — that it will be long, that it has a limit, that it will end. He says none of that. The reason is that the information would be useless to them and dangerous for the reader. A date would turn the interval into a countdown and let the notables plan around it; a general assurance would be treated as a postponement. The sūrah's eighth verse made the same choice in the Mekkan context, describing the delay as running to an appointed time without naming it. What the hearer is given is not a schedule but a fact: there is no escape when it comes. That is the only piece of information that changes behaviour, and it is the only piece the verse supplies.

**The End of the Argument**

Structurally this verse closes the verbal exchange. After it, Nūḥ stops answering objections. The remaining verses of the narrative describe a revelation, a command to build, a period of mockery and a flood. The transition is worth marking because it shows how the Qurʾān handles a dispute that has exhausted itself. There is no final rebuttal, no summary of the case, no attempt to win the last word. The messenger states the fact about who controls the punishment and the fact about escape, and the narrative moves on. The sūrah treats the notables' refusal as settled at this point, and everything after it is consequence. The reader is left with the exchange as it actually stood: a demand for a sign, an answer that declined the demand, and a town that kept asking.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:34

> My advice will not benefit you—no matter how hard I try—if Allah wills ˹for˺ you to stray. He is your Lord, and to Him you will ˹all˺ be returned.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Advice That Has Run Out of Use**

The verse is the messenger's own assessment of his work, and it is unsentimental. My advice will not benefit you. He is not describing a temporary setback or a hard audience. He is stating that the instrument he has been using — counsel, warning, patient explanation — has reached the end of its effectiveness for these people. The word *nuṣḥ* covers sincere advice, the kind given by someone who wants the outcome for the hearer rather than for himself. Nūḥ has been giving it for centuries. The verse records the point at which sincerity stops being sufficient, and it does so without bitterness. A person who has advised a town for nine hundred and fifty years could reasonably conclude that the town is wicked. He concludes instead that the advice has stopped working, and he gives the reason in the second half of the sentence.

**"No Matter How Hard I Try"**

The clause translated as *no matter how hard I try* renders a form that carries intensity and desire: if I wanted to advise you, my wanting would not help. The emphasis is on the messenger's will, and it is emphatic enough to be worth pausing on. Nūḥ is not tired of the work. He is describing a man who still wants to advise them and cannot make the advice land. That distinction separates this verse from resignation. Resignation would say *there is no point*. This verse says *there is every point, and it will not reach you*. The Qurʾān addresses the same situation to this Prophet in direct terms: *"Perhaps you ˹O Prophet˺ will grieve yourself to death over their disbelief"* (26:3), and *"We certainly know that your heart is truly distressed by what they say"* (15:97). The distress of a messenger whose counsel is not received is treated throughout the Book as a real and understandable human reaction, and it is answered rather than rebuked.

**What "God Wills You to Stray" Means**

The condition is the hardest phrase in the verse. *If Allah wills for you to stray.* The commentators treat it as equivalent to the Qurʾān's more common statement that God leaves some people to stray, and the mechanism it describes is not arbitrary. A person who refuses guidance repeatedly reaches a state in which guidance no longer reaches him; the refusal becomes a capacity, and the capacity becomes a nature. The sūrah has already traced this in its own vocabulary, describing people who could not hear and did not see, and describing the gestures by which they made themselves unable. The verse here names the theological description of the same process. It is not that God decided at the outset to mislead these people. It is that their sustained rejection produced a condition which God then willed to leave in place. The will operates on a history the people themselves wrote.

**The Condition Is Not the Cause**

Read carelessly, the verse could be taken to mean that the messenger's effort was irrelevant because the outcome was fixed. The grammar does not support that reading. The condition is *if Allah wills for you to stray* — a conditional about their state, not a decree about the mission. Nūḥ was commanded to warn, he warned, and the warning was genuine; the verse does not retract any of it. What it states is that the effect of a warning depends on the condition of the hearer, and that a hearer who has closed the channel cannot be reached by further counsel. This preserves both halves of the sūrah's teaching. The messenger's duty is real and unchanging; the hearer's responsibility is real and unchanging. Neither is absorbed into the other. The verse is therefore not a statement about predestination in the abstract. It is a report on a specific town after a specific history of refusal.

**"He Is Your Lord"**

Two short clauses close the verse and they do different work. *He is your Lord* restates ownership at the moment the mission has failed. The people have rejected the messenger; they have not thereby escaped the owner. The sūrah's fourth verse made the same point in its opening address — *to Allah is your return* — and this verse returns to it after four hundred years of argument have changed nothing. The restatement is not a threat. It is the ground on which the messenger's own position rests. He has been told to warn, he has warned, and the fact that the warning was refused does not alter whose people these are or whose business he was conducting. A messenger whose audience rejects him has not failed in his office; he has completed it.

**The Return Named at the End of a Mission**

The verse ends where the sūrah's opening ended: *to Him you will all be returned.* The repetition is the structure of the whole chapter in miniature. The opening address stated the return as a doctrine; the narrative now shows it at the end of the longest mission in the Book. Between the two lie centuries of warning, and at the end of them the same sentence holds. The placement is also a comfort addressed to the messenger rather than to the town. A man who has spent a lifetime advising people who will not listen needs to know that the matter is not closed by their refusal. It is closed by a return, at which the advice given and the advice rejected will both be present. The verse therefore lets Nūḥ put the work down. He has done what he was sent to do, and the rest is not his.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:35

> Or do they say, “He has fabricated this ˹Quran˺!”? Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “If I have done so, then I bear the burden of that sin! But I am free from your sinful accusation.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Narrative Interrupted by the Present**

The sūrah has been inside the story of Nūḥ for ten verses. This verse steps out of it without warning and addresses Mekka directly. The shift is abrupt enough to look like an intrusion, and commentators note that the verse concerns the Makkan idolaters and their accusation that the Prophet had fabricated the Qurʾān. The abruptness is the point. The narrative has reached the point where Nūḥ's people have exhausted their arguments and demanded the punishment, and the sūrah seizes the moment to show that the same conversation is happening now, in the same words. The charge against Nūḥ and the charge against this Prophet are not similar; they are identical. By placing the Mekkan verse here, the sūrah makes the reader hear the ancient story as a present event and the present event as an old pattern.

**Why the Interruption Comes at This Exact Point**

The placement is not random. Verse 32 recorded the notables demanding that Nūḥ bring the punishment; verse 33 gave the answer; verse 34 closed the mission's verbal stage. At that point the ancient exchange has nothing left to say, and the sūrah turns to the exchange that is still open. There is also a thematic reason. The accusation of fabrication was raised in verse 13 and answered with the challenge to produce ten sūrahs. Now, in the middle of the narratives, it is raised again and answered differently — not with a challenge but with a declaration of responsibility. The two answers together cover the ground completely. The challenge addresses the public claim that the text is human. The declaration addresses the private question of what the messenger is risking by saying what he says.

**"My Guilt Upon Me"**

The Prophet is told to accept the conditional in its strongest form. *If I have done so, then I bear the burden of that sin.* The commentators gloss the phrase as equivalent to saying *then I shall be punished for my sin*, and the gloss brings out what is being offered. This is not a rhetorical deflection. It is a man staking his own punishment on the truth of his claim, in a Book that repeatedly describes punishment as certain. If the Qurʾān were his invention, he would be committing the worst wrong the sūrah has named — fabricating a lie against God — and he would be doing it while telling people that fabricating a lie against God is the worst wrong there is. The offer only makes sense if he knows the claim is true. The verse therefore functions as a test of the messenger's own confidence, and it is a test the audience can weigh without having to judge the language.

**"I Am Free of What You Do"**

The second clause separates the two cases. *I am free from your sinful accusation.* The word rendered as sinful accusation is *ijrām*, from the same root as the crime he has just accepted responsibility for, and the construction is deliberate. He takes the guilt of fabrication onto himself conditionally, and he disclaims any share in their crime absolutely. The two crimes are not the same and the verse keeps them apart. Fabricating a message from God is one offence; accusing an innocent man of it is another, and the accusers carry the second whether or not the first occurred. The Qurʾān uses the same formula elsewhere to close an argument: *"O my people! Persist in your ways, for I ˹too˺ will persist in mine. You will soon know who will fare best in the end. Indeed, the wrongdoers will never succeed"* (6:135). The separation of accounts is the sūrah's answer to the way public accusations work, in which the accused inherits the guilt of the accusation simply by being accused.

**Two Charges, Two Standards of Proof**

The verse quietly distinguishes between the burden the messenger carries and the burden the accusers carry, and the distinction is evidential. A man who claims to carry a revelation stakes his own punishment on it, and the stake is verifiable: if he is lying, the sūrah's own teaching condemns him first. A man who accuses him of lying stakes nothing. He offers no counter-text, no alternative account of the book's origin, no evidence about the messenger's character beyond assertion. The sūrah has already exposed this asymmetry in its challenge to produce ten sūrahs. This verse exposes it from the other side. The person making the positive claim has accepted a penalty; the person making the negative claim has accepted none. That is not proof of the claim's truth, but it is a reliable indicator of which side has thought about consequences.

**A Legal Formula in the Middle of a Story**

The verse reads like a deposition, and its formality is part of its function. Two statements, a conditional admission and an absolute denial, with no elaboration between them. There is no appeal to the audience's sympathy, no description of the messenger's sincerity, no rhetorical question. The sūrah places this legal register in the middle of a narrative about a flood, and the contrast sharpens both. The story shows what happens to a people who refuse a warning; the verse shows the messenger's standing before the charge. Together they answer the two questions a reader might have — what happens to those who reject, and what is the one who warns risking. The narrative resumes immediately afterwards with a revelation to Nūḥ, and the sūrah does not explain the interruption. It does not need to.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:36

> And it was revealed to Noah, “None of your people will believe except those who already have. So do not be distressed by what they have been doing

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Revelation That Closes a Mission**

The verse records a communication to Nūḥ, and its content is a statement of fact rather than a command. No more of his people will believe. The mission is not being extended, modified or given new instructions; it is being closed. The form of the revelation matters. It is not a prediction offered for comfort or a warning designed to motivate. It is information delivered at the point where information is the only thing left to give. A messenger who has warned for centuries needs to know when warning has stopped being useful, and the verse supplies that knowledge. The sūrah places it immediately after the exchange has exhausted itself, which is where a reader would expect it. The argument is over; the fact is stated; what follows is construction.

**Nine Hundred and Fifty Years**

The length of the mission is given elsewhere in the Qurʾān, and the number is what makes this verse extraordinary. *"Indeed, We sent Noah to his people, and he remained among them for a thousand years, less fifty. Then the Flood overtook them, while they persisted in wrongdoing"* (29:14). Nine hundred and fifty years of warning, and the verse here reports the final count of converts as a fixed group that will not grow. The number does two things. It removes the objection that Nūḥ's people were not given enough time, since no people in the Book were given more. And it removes the objection that the messenger gave up too soon, since the closure comes at the end of the longest mission recorded rather than at its beginning. The sūrah needs both facts, because the narratives that follow are meant to console a messenger in Mekka who was in the early years of his own mission and had no way of knowing how long it would run.

**"Except Those Who Already Have"**

The exception is precise and its precision is painful. Not *except a few who will believe*, but *except those who already have*. The group is closed and its membership is complete. The commentators take the phrase to mean those who had believed up to that point, and the reading gives the sentence its full weight. After centuries of preaching, the number of believers is the number already standing there, and no further addition will be made. The verse does not soften this. It does not say that a few more might come, or that the last generation will respond, or that the young will differ from their fathers. It states a fixed total. The sūrah's account of the flood will confirm it — *and none believed with him except for a few* — and the smallness of the group is not presented as a failure of the message. It is presented as the outcome of a town's sustained refusal.

**"Do Not Be Distressed" Addresses the Messenger's Heart**

The second half of the verse turns from information to consolation. *So do not be distressed by what they have been doing.* The commentators gloss the word as sadness approaching despair, and the address is to a man's feelings rather than to his conduct. The Qurʾān repeatedly speaks to the prophets' human reactions to their trials, and this is one of the clearest instances. Nūḥ is not being rebuked for grief; he is being relieved of a burden he has been carrying. The same address is given to this Prophet: *"Perhaps you ˹O Prophet˺ will grieve yourself to death over their disbelief"* (26:3). The pattern shows that a messenger's distress over his people is expected and normal, and that the cure offered is not a command to stop feeling but a statement of fact that makes the feeling unnecessary. Nobody will believe. There is nothing further to be achieved by grieving.

**Effort and Result Are Not the Same Measure**

The verse is the sūrah's clearest statement on a question that troubles anyone who works at something without visible results. Nūḥ's mission produced almost no converts, and it is presented in the Qurʾān as a success. The measure of a messenger's work is the delivery, not the reception, and the sūrah has established that principle in its Mekkan section: *you are only a warner*. A man who warned for nine hundred and fifty years and converted a handful has completed his office perfectly. A man who converted thousands by softening the message would have failed. The verse therefore answers the calculation the notables had implicitly been making throughout the narrative, in which the size of the following was treated as evidence about the message. The sūrah reverses the calculation entirely and then closes the account.

**Why the Verse Comes Before the Command to Build**

The order of the next two verses is deliberate. First the mission is declared finished; then the command to build the Ark arrives. The sequence tells the reader what the Ark is. It is not an evangelistic project or a second chance for the town. It is provision for the people who already believed, made after the door has closed. The prohibition on pleading for the wrongdoers, given in the following verse, follows from the same fact. There is nothing to plead for, because the decision has been reached and the information has been given. The verse's placement therefore does structural work: it ends one phase of the narrative and opens the next, and it does so by stating a fact rather than by announcing a change. A reader who understands why the Ark is being built understands everything that follows in the story.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:37

> And build the Ark under Our ˹watchful˺ Eyes and directions, and do not plead with Me for those who have done wrong, for they will surely be drowned.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Command to Build, Not to Flee**

The instruction is practical and it is addressed to a man who has just been told his mission is over. Nūḥ is not told to leave the town, abandon the people, or wait for rescue. He is told to build. The verb is ordinary — construct, make — and the object is a vessel. The command places the messenger inside the coming event rather than outside it, and it gives him work to do during the interval between the closure of the mission and the arrival of the flood. That interval will be long enough to be publicly ridiculed, as the next verse records. The command therefore shapes the whole narrative. A man building a ship is visible, questionable and mockable, and the sūrah uses the visibility deliberately. The construction becomes the final public statement of the mission, made in wood rather than in words.

**"Before Our Eyes" — Supervision as Protection**

The phrase translated as *under Our watchful Eyes* is figurative, and the commentators gloss it as God watching over and protecting the building of the Ark. The image does not describe a physical organ; it describes continuous attention. Its function here is twofold. It assures the builder that the work is observed, which matters to a man about to spend years on a project his entire town considers insane. And it marks the work as protected, which matters because the notables had already shown themselves willing to pressure the messenger into dismissing his followers. A construction undertaken under divine supervision cannot be interfered with by the people mocking it. The same idiom appears elsewhere in the Qurʾān to describe a person raised under divine care, and in each case the emphasis is on protection during a vulnerable period rather than on surveillance.

**"By Our Revelation" — the Design Was Given**

The second phrase states that the Ark was built by God's revelation, and the commentators take this to mean that God instructed Nūḥ in how to build it. The detail is worth isolating because it changes the character of the vessel. This is not a boat designed by an experienced shipwright using local techniques. Its specifications came down. The point has an argumentative edge for the sūrah's original audience. A messenger who receives instructions about carpentry receives them in the same way he receives instructions about worship — as revelation, from the same source, with the same authority. The verse therefore makes a quiet claim about the range of what revelation covers. It is not confined to doctrine. It reaches into the practical arrangements by which a small group of believers is preserved, and the sūrah's account of the flood depends on the vessel having been built correctly.

**The Prohibition on Pleading**

The second half of the verse forbids something Nūḥ had been doing for centuries. *Do not plead with Me for those who have done wrong.* The commentators read this as a prohibition on interceding for them and on praying that the punishment be withheld. The instruction is a change in the messenger's standing. Up to this point he had been warning them, and warning includes the hope that they will respond. Now the door is closed, and with it the legitimacy of the plea. The verse does not criticise the earlier pleading; it marks the moment after which it becomes inappropriate. That distinction is important, because it defines the limit of a messenger's advocacy. He is authorised to warn and to hope; he is not authorised to negotiate with the decree once the decree has been issued. The sūrah will show Nūḥ testing this limit later in the narrative, over a member of his own family, and the test will not go well.

**The Method Named in Advance**

The verse ends by specifying how the wrongdoers will die: they will be drowned. The specificity is unusual and it does argumentative work. A punishment announced in advance, in a particular form, at a time when no water is present, is a claim that can be checked. The town will have years to consider the prediction, and the sūrah's next verse records them laughing at the man building a ship. The prediction also shapes the narrative that follows. The reader knows what is coming and in what form, which turns the account of the flood from a story into the fulfilment of a stated term. This is the same structure the sūrah established in its eighth verse, where a punishment delayed until an appointed time was said to arrive in a way that cannot be turned away. Here the appointed time has a named mechanism, and the mechanism is the one thing the town considers impossible.

**Why the Command Comes With a Prohibition**

The verse joins two instructions that belong to different domains: build the vessel, and stop praying for the doomed. Putting them together defines the messenger's position at this stage of the story. His practical task is now entirely directed toward the believers — the vessel is for them — while his relation to the rest of the town has ended. There is nothing further to say to them and nothing further to ask for them. The combination also answers a question the sūrah's Mekkan audience would have had. What does a messenger do after his people have refused him completely? The answer is not that he leaves, and not that he keeps arguing. He builds what the believers need, and he stops pleading for those who will not be saved. The verse is a description of the transition from mission to preparation, and it is delivered in a single sentence.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:38

> So he began to build the Ark, and whenever some of the chiefs of his people passed by, they mocked him. He said, “If you laugh at us, we will ˹soon˺ laugh at you similarly

**Expanded Commentary**

**Obedience Before Understanding**

The verse opens with an act of obedience that cannot have made sense to the man performing it. Nūḥ begins to build a vessel. The sūrah does not tell us where he built it, and the commentators differ about the location, but every reading agrees on the essential oddity: the Ark was constructed away from water, in a place where a ship had no function. A man following an instruction he cannot justify to his neighbours is in a specific moral position, and the verse is interested in that position rather than in the carpentry. Nūḥ builds because he was told to, and the telling came with the design. The sūrah's whole account of prophethood rests on this kind of obedience. A messenger does not execute the parts of a revelation he finds reasonable and skip the rest; the sūrah's twelfth verse had already ruled out selective delivery. The building is therefore the practical form of the same principle.

**The Visibility of the Work**

A construction project is public. It occupies ground, requires material, takes time and can be watched. The verse notes that the chiefs *passed by* him, which means the site was on a route people used. Nūḥ did not build in secret, and the command did not ask him to. The visibility matters for the argument the sūrah is running. A man who quietly holds an unpopular belief can be ignored; a man who spends years constructing an absurd object in public forces a question on everyone who walks past. The town had to form a view of him daily. Their response was laughter, and the verse records it as a repeated event rather than a single incident — *whenever* they passed by, they mocked. The mockery was not a spontaneous reaction but a settled habit, which is what makes it diagnostic of the state the sūrah described earlier as the inability to hear or see.

**The Chiefs, Again**

The verse identifies the mockers as chiefs, the same group that opened the exchange eleven verses earlier. Their priority in the narrative is consistent. The people with the most invested in the town's existing order are the first to respond to anything that threatens it, and their response sets the tone for everyone else. The mockery of a man building a ship is a public performance with a social function. It signals to the town that the messenger is not to be taken seriously, and it does so without requiring anyone to answer his argument. The sūrah has already shown this mechanism in its Mekkan section, where the disbelievers' ridicule was described as a way of treating a warning as a performance. The chiefs here are not engaging with Nūḥ's claims. They are managing his reputation, and laughter is an efficient instrument for that purpose.

**Laughter as the Weapon of the Comfortable**

Mockery is available to people who feel safe. It requires a position from which the thing mocked looks implausible, and it works only if the audience shares that position. The chiefs laughing at a man building a ship are demonstrating their own security: they have homes, standing, water where water belongs and no expectation of catastrophe. The verse therefore records not just a reaction but an assumption. The town's confidence rests on the stability of its world, and Nūḥ's construction is an offence against that stability rather than against their theology. This is why the mockery is so much more intense than the theological objection was. A man can be argued with; a man whose actions imply that the world is about to end has insulted everyone's sense of normalcy. The sūrah's eighth verse described the same posture from the other side, where people ask what is holding the punishment back.

**"We Will Laugh at You Similarly"**

Nūḥ's reply is unexpected. He does not defend the Ark, explain the flood, or rebuke them for their insolence. He announces a reversal: if you laugh at us, we will laugh at you in the same way. The symmetry is exact — the same act, returned. The commentators read this as referring to the final state of affairs, when the believers will laugh at the disbelievers as the disbelievers laughed at them, and the Qurʾān describes that scene directly: *"Indeed, the wicked used to laugh at the believers"* (83:29), and *"But on that Day the believers will be laughing at the disbelievers, as they sit on ˹canopied˺ couches, looking on"* (83:34–35). The reply is not gloating. It is a statement about the durability of positions. Laughter is a claim about who is right, and the verse says the claim will be tested.

**The Reversal Belongs to a Wider Law**

The exchange in this verse is an instance of a principle the Qurʾān states repeatedly: what people do comes back to them. The sūrah has already given the principle in its Mekkan section, where those who mocked the messengers were overtaken by the very thing they had mocked. Nūḥ's answer applies the same law to a specific act. It also identifies the timing, which the sūrah handles carefully. The reversal is not immediate. The chiefs will laugh for years, and Nūḥ will build for years, and nothing will happen to contradict them. The reversal arrives all at once, and it arrives in the form of the thing they thought absurd. That is what makes the verse more than a piece of repartee. It is a statement about how a settled mockery ends, and the sūrah is about to narrate the ending.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:39

> You will soon come to know who will be visited by a humiliating torment ˹in this life˺ and overwhelmed by an everlasting punishment ˹in the next˺.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Punishments Stated in One Sentence**

The verse names two punishments and keeps them distinct. One is humiliating and belongs to this life; the other is lasting and belongs to what comes after. The commentators identify the first as the flood, which would disgrace Nūḥ's people in front of the world they had been so confident in, and the second as their state of punishment in the Hereafter. The distinction is not decorative. A single punishment would leave the reader unable to tell whether the story ends with the water. Two punishments establish that the flood is not the conclusion but the first instalment, and that the mockery recorded one verse earlier has consequences that outlive the bodies it drowned. The sūrah uses the same vocabulary of disgrace elsewhere, and it uses the vocabulary of permanence elsewhere, but here it joins them in a single announcement made before the event.

**Humiliation as the Specific Form of the First Punishment**

The adjective chosen for the worldly punishment is *mukhzī* — one that disgraces, exposes, strips a person of standing. That is a precise choice for these people. The chiefs had spent years mocking a man building a ship, and their mockery rested entirely on their own security. They had homes, position, and a world that behaved as expected. The punishment announced against them is not merely destructive but specifically deflating: the thing they laughed at becomes the instrument, and the position from which they laughed disappears. The Qurʾān describes the same reversal elsewhere in a scene where the wicked who used to laugh at the believers are laughed at in turn (83:29–34). The verse therefore answers the previous verse's exchange directly. Nūḥ said the laughter would be returned; this verse specifies that the return begins in this life and takes the form of disgrace.

**An Everlasting Punishment After the Water**

The second punishment is described as *dāʾim* — lasting, continuous, without interruption. The commentators take it as the punishment of the Hereafter, and the Qurʾān uses the same word elsewhere for the Fire. Its placement after the flood is significant for the sūrah's argument. The towns that will be narrated in the coming chapters are all destroyed inside history, and a reader could reasonably conclude that destruction is the whole of the consequence. This verse denies that. The flood kills the body; the lasting punishment concerns what survives the body. The sūrah makes the same point structurally by returning to the Day of Judgment at intervals throughout its narratives, so that no destroyed town is ever presented as a closed case. Every one of them has a second hearing. The pairing also protects the reader from a common misreading of the destroyed towns, in which their fate is taken as the total of what they owed. The flood, the blast, the stones and the cry are what these peoples met in history; the verse adds what they met after it, and the addition is what makes the sūrah's warnings about the Hereafter consistent with its narratives about the past.

**"You Will Soon Know"**

The announcement is framed as future knowledge: you will come to know. The verb carries certainty and nearness at once, and the formula is one the Qurʾān uses when a fact is about to be demonstrated rather than argued. The notables have been told repeatedly; they have not been persuaded. The verse therefore shifts the ground from testimony to experience. There will come a point at which the question of who was right is no longer a matter of argument but of observation, and the observers will be the people who disputed it. The same formula appears elsewhere in the sūrah, in Shuʿayb's exchange with Midian, and in both cases it closes an argument that has run out of replies. Its function is not to threaten but to date the end of the dispute.

**The Verse Identifies the Real Question**

Read carefully, the verse does not say *you will be punished*. It says *you will know who* will be punished. The object of the coming knowledge is the identity of the punished party, which is precisely what the exchange has been about. The chiefs asserted that Nūḥ was a liar and his followers were nobodies; Nūḥ asserted that he stood on a clear proof.
