The vocabulary of this verse is worth holding onto, because it is the vocabulary of refusal in every age. Nothing here sounds hostile. The words are procedural: no clear proof, not upon your word, not believers in you. A reader scanning the verse for insults will find none, and that is what makes it dangerous. The sūrah's other narratives contain open hostility — the demand to bring the punishment, the accusation of being bewitched — but this verse is the polite form, and the polite form is harder to recognise in oneself. Every clause is defensible on its own. Taken together they amount to a decision that was made before the discussion started, dressed in the language of a discussion still in progress. The sūrah records it because the reader needs to be able to hear the difference between an objection and a position, and because most people cannot.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:54

> All we can say is that some of our gods have possessed you with evil.” He said, “I call Allah to witness, and you too bear witness, that I ˹totally˺ reject whatever you associate

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Last Stage of Refusal**

The verse records the point at which ʿĀd stop arguing. Their words are hedged — *all we can say is* — and the hedge is not modesty. It marks the end of what they are willing to offer: not a counter-claim, not a reason, but a diagnosis. Some of our gods have struck you with evil. The commentators explain that this reflects their belief that Hūd was a madman put under a spell by one of the idols, because he had cursed it and called them to stop worshipping it. The move is worth naming precisely, because it is the most efficient way to end a discussion. A person accused of being bewitched cannot defend himself, since any defence he offers can be read as further evidence of the spell. The sūrah has seen the same move in Mekka, where the Prophet was called a magician and, elsewhere, insane (23:70; 81:22).

**The Gods Made to Act**

There is an unintended claim inside the accusation, and the claim deserves attention. ʿĀd assert that one of their gods has done something — struck Hūd with evil. Moments earlier they had declined to abandon these gods on Hūd's word, and had denied that he brought any proof of his own authority. Now they attribute an action to the idols. The attribution is the only act of divine power the idols perform in the entire narrative, and it is performed on the man who says they are nothing. The Qurʾān presses this point elsewhere, asking what these beings actually create or provide, and the sūrah will press it again in its own voice near the end of the chapter, where the gods invoked beside Allah are said to have helped not at all when the command came. The accusation therefore concedes a function to the idols that the rest of the Book denies them.

**Hūd Does Not Answer the Diagnosis**

What follows is striking for what it omits. Hūd does not deny being bewitched, does not argue about his mental state, and does not ask what evidence they have. He moves directly to an oath. The omission is method. A charge of insanity cannot be rebutted by the person charged, and any attempt to rebut it stays inside the frame the accusers chose. Hūd refuses the frame in the only way available: he makes a statement about himself and calls witnesses to it. The sūrah has shown the same procedure before, when Nūḥ declined to argue about his followers' social rank and restated his own position instead. A messenger who answers every accusation on its own terms ends up defending himself indefinitely. A messenger who states his position once, under oath, closes the matter.

**Two Sets of Witnesses Called**

The oath has an unusual structure. *I call Allah to witness, and you too bear witness.* God is invoked as witness, and the accusers are invited to serve in the same capacity. The double invocation does two things. Calling God makes the statement a matter of record before the one who knows what is true. Calling the people makes it a public act that they cannot later claim not to have heard. The sūrah has been concerned throughout with what will be testified on the Day, where witnesses will identify those who lied against their Lord. Hūd is creating that record now, in advance, and he is enrolling his opponents in it. The verse thereby converts their accusation into an occasion for a declaration, which is the only useful thing a messenger can do with an unanswerable charge.

**"I Am Quit of What You Associate"**

The content of the oath is a disavowal, and the word is strong: I am free of it, innocent of it, clear of it. The commentators note that prophets make the same disavowal elsewhere of the beliefs of the peoples they are sent to (6:19; 6:78). The disavowal is not a statement of disagreement. Disagreement would leave open the possibility that the idols exist and are merely not to be worshipped. This is a declaration of separation from the entire arrangement — the beings, the worship, the practice and the belief. The verse's wording is deliberately incomplete, breaking off before its object is finished, and the next verse completes it. The effect is a sentence that continues past the verse boundary, which mirrors the substance: the disavowal is not confined to one statement but runs through everything that follows.

**Why the Verse Ends Mid-Sentence**

The structural feature of this verse is its incompleteness. Hūd's speech begins here and finishes in the next verse, and the reader is carried across the boundary in the middle of a clause. The sūrah has used this device before, in the opening address that ran from verse 2 to verse 4, and there it served to bind a command, a promise and a threat into a single unbreakable utterance. Here the effect is similar but sharper. The disavowal cannot be separated from the challenge that follows it, because grammatically it has not ended. A person who accepts the first half must take the second, and a person who rejects the challenge cannot keep the disavowal. The verse is therefore both a declaration and a trap for selective hearing, and it is delivered at the moment when Hūd's people have stopped listening to anything he says.

****Why the Oath Is the Only Available Reply****

Hūd's choice of an oath over an argument is not a rhetorical preference. It follows from the shape of the accusation. A charge of madness cannot be met with reasoning, because the reasoning is itself evidence for the charge in the accuser's eyes. A charge of lying can be met with a track record, but ʿĀd had already declared that they would not believe him. What remains available to a man in that position is a public act of commitment that does not depend on his audience accepting anything about his character. An oath before God and before witnesses is exactly that. It places the matter on a record that both parties will have to face, and it does so without requiring the audience to concede a single point. The sūrah will return to this record on the Day, when witnesses speak before the accused.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:55

> with Him ˹in worship˺. So let all of you plot against me without delay

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence Completed**

The verse opens by finishing a disavowal begun in the previous one, and the completion is short: whatever you associate with Him. The object of Hūd's rejection is now fully named, and it is everything. Not some of the gods, not the excessive forms of worship, not the innovations of a particular generation — the whole set of what ʿĀd associate with God. The brevity of the completion is worth noticing after the length of the speech that led to it. The substance of the disavowal fits in six words. Everything that came before was the framework: the oath, the witnesses, the public record. The framework exists because the substance is absolute, and an absolute disavowal made to a hostile town needs witnesses attached to it.

**A Challenge Addressed to Everyone at Once**

The second half of the verse is a challenge, and its scope is total. *So let all of you plot against me.* The commentators note that the challenge is addressed both to the people and to their gods, and that reading is supported by the context: Hūd has just disavowed the gods, and the accusation against him was that one of them had struck him. He is therefore inviting both parties to act. The word for plotting is the one used for concerted scheming, and the imperative form makes the invitation explicit rather than hypothetical. Do it. The challenge has a specific audience problem it is solving. A man who says the gods are nothing, in a town that has just claimed one of them harmed him, has made a claim that can be tested on the spot. The verse invites the test.

**"Without Delay"**

The final phrase removes the one excuse that could preserve the idols' reputation. *Do not give me respite.* Hūd is not asking for a fair fight on favourable terms; he is refusing the terms that would let the outcome be explained away. If a plot against him failed after a delay, ʿĀd could say the gods were being patient, or that the timing was not right, or that the matter had been deferred. By demanding immediacy, Hūd forecloses every interpretation of failure except one: nothing happened because there is nothing there. The sūrah uses the same logic in its challenge to produce ten sūrahs, where the demand was public and the terms were specified in advance. In both cases the point is not the difficulty of the task but the impossibility of reinterpreting the result.

**What the Challenge Presupposes**

A challenge of this kind only makes sense on one assumption: that the challenger cannot be harmed by the thing he is challenging. Hūd is not brave in the ordinary sense here, since ordinary bravery involves acting despite real danger. He is stating a conclusion he has already reached. The gods his people worship have no capacity to affect him, so a plot conducted through them will fail, and a plot conducted by the people alone is a matter for God rather than for the gods. The next verse supplies the premise explicitly, grounding the challenge in trust and in the fact that every creature is held by its forelock. The verse here is the practical application of that premise, and it is addressed to a town that has no way to refute it except by acting.

**Why No One Accepted**

The narrative does not record any attempt on Hūd's life, and the silence is the verse's argument. The challenge was issued publicly, to a town that had already accused him of being bewitched and had refused to abandon its gods on his word. The motive to eliminate him was total: he had insulted the gods, undermined the ancestral religion, and just told the entire population to attack him. Nothing is recorded. The commentators take the challenge as meaning that neither ʿĀd nor their gods would be able to do anything, and the narrative confirms it by omission. This is the same evidential structure the sūrah used in its Mekkan section, where the challenge to produce ten sūrahs was never answered. An open offer that remains open is a statement about the party that failed to take it up.

**The Verse as the Turning Point of the Narrative**

Structurally this verse closes the argumentative phase of the Hūd narrative. After it, the messenger stops engaging with objections and states his position: trust in God, the universality of divine control, the justice of God's way, and the announcement that the message has been delivered. The dialogue with ʿĀd is over, and the sūrah does not record another word from them. The transition mirrors the Nūḥ narrative exactly, where the demand to bring on the punishment was followed by a statement about who controls the punishment and then by the command to build. In both stories the pattern is the same: the people exhaust their objections, the messenger states the facts, and the narrative moves toward the event. The challenge in this verse is the last thing said to ʿĀd before the facts are set out.

****The Verse and the Sūrah's Mekkan Challenge****

The challenge in this verse belongs to a family of challenges the Qurʾān issues, and comparing them shows a consistent design. The challenge to produce ten sūrahs specified the object, the number and the help available. The challenge to bring on the punishment, made by Nūḥ's people and refused, specified the timing. This challenge specifies the participants and the absence of delay. In each case the terms are set so that failure cannot be reinterpreted, and in each case the terms were never met. The pattern is the sūrah's evidential method: not assertion, but an open offer with defined conditions. A reader who doubts the claim is invited to test it, and the test is one he can conduct. The refusal of every such test, by every people in the chapter, is what the sūrah presents as the argument.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:56

> I have put my trust in Allah—my Lord and your Lord. There is no living creature that is not completely under His control. Surely my Lord’s Way is perfect justice

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ground of the Challenge Stated**

The verse gives the reason for the challenge issued in the previous one, and the commentators identify it as Hūd's firm reliance on God. The word is *tawakkul* — trust that has been placed, settled, committed. It is not a feeling of confidence but a transaction: a person has handed an affair to someone competent to handle it. The verse therefore explains the previous one without softening it. Hūd invited the whole town and its gods to plot against him because he had already transferred the matter out of his own hands. The sūrah's closing verse will make the same pairing the summary of the whole chapter: worship Him and put your trust in Him. Here the pairing appears inside a narrative, in the mouth of a man standing alone in front of a hostile town.

**"My Lord and Your Lord"**

The phrase joins two relations in three words, and the join is the verse's quiet argument. Hūd does not say *my Lord* and leave it there, which would make his trust a private arrangement. He says *my Lord and your Lord*, which places his opponents inside the same jurisdiction they are trying to escape. The construction answers the accusation of the previous verse directly. They claimed one of their gods had struck him; he replies that the being who holds him also holds them. The formula is the standard prophetic statement of shared creaturehood, and it appears in similar form elsewhere in the Qurʾān. Its function here is to remove the possibility of two separate realms — one governed by Allah, another by the idols. There is one governance, and both parties are inside it.

**Every Creature Held by Its Forelock**

The central image is the most vivid in the narrative. There is no creature that crawls, says Hūd, except that God holds it by its forelock. The commentators read the forelock as a metaphor for control and authority over all creatures, and the image is exact. The forelock is the hair at the front of an animal's head; to hold it is to direct the animal completely, since it cannot move in any direction its holder does not permit. The verse extends the image to every *dābba* — every moving creature — which is the same word the sūrah used in its sixth verse, where every moving creature's provision was said to lie with God. The two verses belong together. The one who provides for a creature is the one holding it, and neither the provision nor the holding can be delegated to a carved object.

**Why the Image Answers the Accusation**

The connection between the image and ʿĀd's claim is worth spelling out. They asserted that one of their gods had struck Hūd with evil. The verse replies that no creature — including the one making the claim and the one being claimed about — moves except under a held forelock. The idols are therefore not merely false; they are inside the category of things that are held. An object under control cannot exercise control. The Qurʾān makes the same point in a different register elsewhere, asking what these beings create and observing that they are themselves created. The sūrah's version is more concrete because it uses an image from herding, which ʿĀd would have understood immediately. A tribesman knows what it means to hold an animal by the forelock, and he knows that the animal's intentions are irrelevant to where it goes.

**"My Lord's Way Is Perfect Justice"**

The verse closes with a statement about God's path, and the commentators gloss it in two directions that reinforce each other. God is upon the path of truth; He is just; He does not let wrongdoers escape. And He does not abandon one who relies on Him. The first gloss answers ʿĀd: their plotting will not succeed because injustice does not succeed in the long run. The second answers Hūd's own situation: his trust is not misplaced, because the one he has trusted does not drop what He has taken up. The phrase *straight path* is the same one the Qurʾān's opening sūrah asks to be guided along (1:6), and its use here makes a claim about God rather than a request of Him. The sūrah's earlier verses described the wrongdoers as those who strive to make the path appear crooked. This verse states the actual condition of the path.

**The Verse as a Statement of Where Safety Lies**

Read together, the three clauses describe a complete position. Trust has been placed; everything is held; the holder's way is just. Nothing in that position depends on the town's behaviour, on the idols' power, or on the outcome of the plot. The sūrah has been building toward this kind of statement since its opening, where provision was guaranteed, the record was complete, and the return was certain. Hūd's verse is the practical form of the same doctrine: a man who believes those things can stand in front of an entire town and invite it to try. The narrative now moves to the announcement that the message has been delivered, and the announcement carries the same calm, because the ground it stands on has just been stated.

****Trust Stated in Public, Not Held in Private****

One feature of this verse distinguishes it from most statements of reliance in the Qurʾān. Hūd says it out loud, to the people who have just accused him of being bewitched. Trust is ordinarily an interior condition, and the Book usually describes it as such. Here it is announced as part of an argument, and the announcement does work that a private conviction could not. It tells ʿĀd why the challenge in the previous verse was not bravado. It tells the believers standing behind him where their safety lies. And it puts the statement on the same public record as the oath. The sūrah's closing verse will make trust one of its two final commands, alongside worship, which suggests that the pairing is not incidental. A person who worships and does not trust has accepted the demand without accepting the arrangement that makes it bearable.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:57

> But if you turn away, I have already delivered to you what I have been sent with. My Lord will replace you with others. You are not harming Him in the least. Indeed, my Lord is a ˹vigilant˺ Keeper over all things.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Condition Stated Without Pressure**

The verse opens with a conditional that carries no threat in its first clause. *But if you turn away.* Hūd does not say what will happen; he says what he will have done. The conditional is therefore not a lever but a closing statement. He is describing a completed transaction: whatever ʿĀd decide, the delivery has already occurred. The sūrah established the principle in its Mekkan section, where the messenger's duty was limited to conveying the message and guidance was placed elsewhere. This verse is the principle spoken by a messenger at the end of his mission, and its calm is the mark of a man who has understood the limits of his own office. Nothing further can be added by him, and nothing further is needed.

**"I Have Already Delivered"**

The tense of the verb matters. Hūd does not say *I am delivering* or *I will deliver*. He says the delivery has happened. The mission is complete at the moment of speaking, regardless of what the audience does next. That is the sūrah's measure of success, and it is the same measure applied to Nūḥ nine hundred and fifty years earlier: a mission that produced a handful of believers was a completed mission. The verse thereby separates two things that the notables of every town have fused — the messenger's performance and the audience's response. Hūd is accountable for the first and has discharged it. The second belongs to ʿĀd and to God. A reader who evaluates a messenger by his converts has applied the wrong standard, and the sūrah's whole narrative section exists to correct that error.

**"My Lord Will Replace You With Others"**

The second clause is the verse's only forward-looking statement, and the commentators gloss it plainly: God will destroy ʿĀd and bring another people who worship Him alone. The Qurʾān makes the same statement elsewhere as a general principle: *"And if you turn away, He will replace you with another people; and they will not be like you"* is the sense carried in several parallel verses (9:39; 47:38). The claim answers an assumption ʿĀd had not stated but clearly held — that they were indispensable. A tribe in possession of land, strength and an ancestral religion naturally supposes that its continuation is necessary to the order of things. The verse denies it. The world will not lack worshippers because this town is removed, and the removal will not leave a gap that cannot be filled.

**"You Are Not Harming Him in the Least"**

The third clause is the theological core of the verse. ʿĀd's rejection injures God in no way. The commentators give two readings, and both are worth keeping. The first: they do not harm God by turning away, nor does any harm come to Him if He destroys them, since He did not create them out of need for them. The second: they will have no ability to harm Him if He replaces them. The first reading is the deeper one, because it removes the premise on which every negotiation with God is conducted. A person who believes his obedience benefits God has something to bargain with; a person who understands that God created without need has nothing. The Qurʾān states the same fact repeatedly — that whoever is guided is guided for himself, and that God is free of need of all creation. The verse applies it to a town that had just told its messenger he was bewitched.

**"A Vigilant Keeper Over All Things"**

The verse ends with a divine name, and the name is chosen for the situation. *Ḥafīẓ* — one who preserves, guards, keeps watch, holds a record. The commentators connect it to God's surveillance of all things, and the word carries both senses at once: protection for what is entrusted to Him, and observation of everything else. For Hūd the name is a guarantee; he has placed his trust and the trust is held. For ʿĀd it is a warning; nothing they have said or plotted has gone unrecorded. The sūrah has used this pairing before, in its fifth verse about the knowledge of what hearts conceal and its sixth about the perfect Record. The verse therefore closes Hūd's speech on the same ground the sūrah opened on, which is what makes the speech feel finished rather than merely stopped.

**What the Verse Establishes About the End of a Mission**

Read as the conclusion of an address, the verse is a model of what a messenger says when he is done. He states that the delivery is complete. He states that the audience is replaceable. He states that their refusal harms no one but themselves. He names the one who is watching. There is no appeal, no last attempt at persuasion, no emotional register of any kind. The sūrah places this speech immediately before the arrival of the punishment, and the placement gives it its weight. A man who knows his mission is finished can watch the end of a town without needing it to have gone otherwise. The next verse reports that end in one sentence, and the sentence distinguishes sharply between the rescued and the rest.

****A Messenger Who No Longer Needs an Outcome****

The emotional register of this verse is worth identifying, because it is rare. There is no anger, no disappointment, no appeal. Hūd states four facts and stops. The absence of feeling is not coldness; it is the condition of a man whose work is defined by delivery rather than by result. The sūrah showed the opposite register in Nūḥ, who grieved over his son and asked God about a promise, and in this Prophet, whose chest was described as constricted by what his hearers said. Both registers are legitimate, and the sūrah preserves both. What distinguishes this verse is its position: it comes at the end of a mission that was refused immediately, so there is no long history of hope to grieve over. The messenger has done his work, the work is finished, and the verse records the sound a finished mission makes.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:58

> When Our command came, We rescued Hûd and those who believed with him by a mercy from Us, saving them from a harsh torment

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command, and What It Was**

The verse begins with the arrival of the command, and the sūrah leaves the word unexplained here because the parallel accounts supply it. The punishment that came on ʿĀd was a wind, and the Qurʾān describes it in detail elsewhere: a wind that tore people out as though they were uprooted palm trunks (54:20). The commentators connect the wind to the drought described earlier in this narrative, and the connection is what makes the account devastating. ʿĀd had been in drought, Hūd had offered rain as the reward of repentance, and when a cloud finally appeared they said: *"This is a cloud bringing us rain"* (46:24). The next verse answers them: it was the very thing they had asked to be hastened, a wind carrying a painful punishment, destroying everything by its Lord's command until nothing was visible except their dwellings (46:25). A people who mocked a warning were destroyed by the thing they most wanted.

**A Rescue Described Twice**

The verse says two things that look like one. God rescued Hūd and the believers by a mercy from Him, and He saved them from a harsh punishment. The commentators distinguish the two: the mercy refers to their being guided to faith and to righteous action, while the saving refers to their deliverance from the punishment. The distinction is the verse's central claim. What rescued these people was not their location, their intelligence or their timing. It was faith, which the verse calls a mercy — something given rather than achieved. The physical rescue follows from the prior one. The sūrah has been making this argument since its fortieth verse, where the few who believed were distinguished from the family by nothing except belief, and its forty-sixth, where a prophet's son was excluded by his conduct.

**"By a Mercy From Us"**

The phrase is worth pausing on because it removes any reading in which the believers earned their survival. The mercy is from God, and it precedes the rescue. The commentators gloss it as guidance to faith and to righteous deeds, which means the mercy is not only the escape but the belief that made the escape meaningful. A person rescued without faith has been preserved for nothing; a person rescued with faith has been preserved for a purpose. The sūrah's next narrative will develop this point, where the rescued group is described as saved by a mercy and spared the disgrace of that Day. The doubling is deliberate. Rescue and dignity are different gifts, and the verse attributes both to the same source.

**The Size of the Saved Group**

Some commentators record that those who accepted Hūd's message numbered forty thousand, and the figure, if sound, is striking in comparison with Nūḥ's handful. The sūrah does not give a number, and its silence is consistent with its method: it counts the saved only when the smallness of the number is part of the point, as it was in the flood narrative. Here the emphasis falls on the mercy rather than the tally. What the reader is given is the fact of a rescue and its ground. The Qurʾān's parallel account closes the episode in similar terms: *"So We saved him and those with him by Our mercy and uprooted those who denied Our signs. They were not believers"* (7:72). The two halves of that sentence are the whole of this verse expanded.

**Why the Punishment Is Called Harsh**

The verse describes what the believers were saved from as a *harsh torment* — ʿadhāb ghalīẓ, a punishment that is heavy, dense, severe. The adjective is not the one used for the worldly destruction alone, and the commentators connect the rescue to both punishments: the wind and what follows it. The sūrah has made the same distinction in the Nūḥ narrative, where a humiliating torment in this life was paired with an everlasting one in the next. The verse therefore does more than report a survival. It states that the believers were removed from the whole sequence, not merely from its first stage. That is what makes the mercy complete, and it is why the verse repeats the idea of saving in two different formulations rather than stating it once.

**The Narrative Closed in One Sentence**

The Hūd narrative occupies nine verses, against twenty-four for Nūḥ, and its ending occupies one. The compression is not carelessness; it is proportion. ʿĀd's case was simpler. They were told to worship one God, they said they would not abandon their gods on a man's word, they diagnosed him as bewitched, and they were destroyed. There was no son, no promise to interrogate, no centuries of negotiation. The sūrah gives the case the space it needs and no more. What it lingers on, in every narrative, is the dialogue — and in this one the dialogue was short because the refusal was immediate. The verse's brevity is the sūrah's judgement on ʿĀd: a people who did not need much narration, because they did not offer much resistance to examine.

****What the Rescue Does Not Say****

The verse is silent on several things a reader would expect. It does not describe how the believers were saved — whether by being elsewhere when the wind came, by shelter, or by any other means. It does not record their number, their names, or what they did afterwards. The silence is consistent with the sūrah's method throughout its narratives, where the mechanism of rescue is never the subject. What matters is that the rescue was grounded in mercy and that the mercy preceded it. A reader looking for practical information about survival will not find it here, and the omission is a judgement about what the story is for. The sūrah is not a manual for escaping punishment. It is an account of what distinguishes those who are saved from those who are not, and the answer it gives is a single word: belief.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:59

> That was ’Âd. They denied the signs of their Lord, disobeyed His messengers, and followed the command of every stubborn tyrant

**Expanded Commentary**

**"That Was ʿĀd" — a Case Closed**

The verse opens with three words that function as a verdict. *That was ʿĀd.* Not *these were the people of ʿĀd*, not *such was their story*, but a flat identification that reads like a file being closed. The sūrah uses the same formula at the end of its later narratives, and its function is always the same: the account is finished, and what follows is the summary of the charge. The abruptness is deliberate. After a narrative containing dialogue, a challenge and a rescue, the reader is given a label. The label reduces a civilisation to a name, and the reduction is the point. A town that occupied nine verses of narrative is disposed of in three words, which is the sūrah's way of stating how much weight it assigns to a people who refused a messenger.

**Three Charges in Sequence**

The verse then lists three things, and the sequence traces a decline. First, they denied the signs of their Lord. Second, they disobeyed His messengers. Third, they followed the command of every stubborn tyrant. The order moves from rejection to disobedience to allegiance, and the third charge is the completion of the first two. A people who deny signs and disobey messengers have created a vacancy in their allegiance, and the verse says what filled it. The commentators note the parallel statement elsewhere that every stubborn tyrant fails (14:15), and they read this verse as meaning that those who follow such people will ultimately be unsuccessful, since God aids His messengers against them. The verse is therefore not describing three separate failures but one failure in three stages.

**Denying the Signs**

The first charge concerns the evidence. ʿĀd had been given signs, and the verse says they denied them. The word for denial here is stronger than mere refusal; it carries the sense of rejecting something known. This connects directly to their complaint two verses earlier, that Hūd had brought no clear proof. The verse contradicts the complaint as a matter of fact. Signs existed, and they were denied. The commentators note that ʿĀd were unable to see Hūd's proofs because of their obstinacy, which means the denial was not a failure of perception but an act of will. The sūrah has been tracing this mechanism since its fifth verse, where people folded their chests to hide what was inside them. By the time a people reach the stage of denying signs they have seen, the condition is settled.

**Disobeying the Messengers — a Plural for a Single Man**

The second charge uses a plural: they disobeyed *His messengers*. Hūd was one man. The plural is usually explained in one of two ways, both of which the verse supports. The first is that disobeying one messenger is disobeying all of them, since they carry the same message; the sūrah has insisted on that identity throughout, showing four messengers using the same formula about standing on a clear proof. The second is that the plural is one of generalisation, common in Arabic for a single addressee. Either reading makes the same point: ʿĀd's refusal was not a local disagreement with a local man. It was a rejection of the office, and therefore of everyone who had held it and everyone who would. The sūrah's whole argument with Mekka depends on this identification.

**Following the Command of Every Stubborn Tyrant**

The third charge names the alternative allegiance, and the word for tyrant is *jabbār ʿanīd* — one who is both overpowering and stubbornly opposed. The two qualities together describe a leader who cannot be reasoned with and cannot be resisted. The verse says ʿĀd followed *every* such figure, which is not a statistical claim but a description of a pattern: whenever a hard, unyielding man told them what to do, they did it. The charge is the sūrah's diagnosis of how a town loses its capacity to hear. It is not that the people were too stupid to resist. It is that they had formed a habit of obedience to force, and a messenger who came with argument rather than force had no standing with them. The verse thereby completes the account of the parable given thirty-five verses earlier: the blind and the deaf are people who have trained themselves not to perceive.

**What the Verse Establishes About Leadership**

Read as a statement about political allegiance, the verse is unusually direct. A people's fate follows from whose command they accept. ʿĀd were not destroyed primarily for their theology; they were destroyed for a chain of obedience that made their theology unchangeable. The sūrah makes the same point later about Pharaoh, whose command was not rightly guided and whose people followed it into the Fire. The two cases are the sūrah's paired illustration of one principle: a community is judged on the direction it takes from its leaders, and the leaders are judged on the direction they give. The verse's placement immediately after the rescue of the believers sharpens the contrast. The believers followed a messenger; the rest followed tyrants. Both groups lived in the same town and heard the same words.

****The Verse as the Sūrah's Method of Summary****

This verse establishes a pattern the sūrah will use twice more. After the narrative proper ends, a short verdict follows: the name of the people, the charges against them, and the outcome. The pattern gives the narrative section its shape. Each story is not left to speak for itself; it is closed with a statement of what it means. The method has an argumentative purpose. A reader left to draw his own conclusion from a story may draw several, and the sūrah is not interested in several. It wants one conclusion, repeated across seven cases, and the summaries are how the repetition is enforced. The summaries also make the section portable. A person who remembers nothing else from the narrative can remember three charges and a dismissal, and that is enough to recognise the pattern when it appears again.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:60

> They were followed by a curse in this world, as they will be on the Day of Judgment. Surely ’Âd denied their Lord. So away with ’Âd, the people of Hûd

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Curse That Runs Across Two Worlds**

The verse describes a curse with an unusually long span. ʿĀd were pursued by it in this life, and they will be pursued by it on the Day of Judgment. The commentators gloss the curse as distance from God's mercy, and the gloss explains why it does not end with death. A punishment inflicted on a body ends when the body ends. A removal from mercy is a condition, and conditions persist. The sūrah has already defined the term in its doctrinal section, where God's condemnation was said to be upon the wrongdoers and glossed as exile from mercy. This verse applies the same definition to a named people and states its duration. The double span also answers an assumption the sūrah has been countering throughout: that a destruction inside history closes the case. It does not. The wind was the first instalment, and the curse is the continuing state.

**Why the Curse Is Named Twice**

The verse mentions the curse in two realms, and the doubling is not repetition. The curse in this world was visible: a town erased, its dwellings left standing empty, its people torn out like uprooted palm trunks. The curse on the Day will be declared. The sūrah has described that declaration earlier, in the scene where witnesses identify those who lied against their Lord. What ʿĀd experienced in history was the effect; what they will experience at the accounting is the naming. The verse therefore covers both the private fact and the public announcement of it. This is consistent with the sūrah's treatment of every destroyed people: the historical event and the final judgement are never collapsed into one another, and neither is treated as sufficient.

**"Surely ʿĀd Denied Their Lord"**

The third clause restates the first charge of the previous verse, and the restatement has a function. After a list of three failures — denying signs, disobeying messengers, following tyrants — the verse reduces them to one: they denied their Lord. The reduction identifies the root. The signs were denied because the one who sent them was denied; the messengers were disobeyed for the same reason; the tyrants were followed because no higher allegiance was recognised. The sūrah's method throughout is to trace behaviour back to a single disposition, and this verse performs the tracing explicitly. It also uses the title *their Lord* — *rabbihim* — which carries the sense of the one who nurtures and maintains. ʿĀd were denying the being who had sustained them, which is what makes the denial more than an intellectual error.

**"Away With ʿĀd" — the Refrain Begins**

The verse closes with an exclamation that will recur. *Away with ʿĀd, the people of Hūd.* The commentators note that this formula functions as a refrain throughout the sūrah, used here for ʿĀd, later for Thamūd, and finally for the Midianites. A refrain is a structural device, and its repetition across three narratives does specific work. It marks each story as complete, it gives the section a rhythm the reader can hear, and it establishes that the verdict is identical in every case. The words are not an outburst. They are a formula of dismissal, spoken over a people who have been removed from the account. The addition of *the people of Hūd* ties the verdict to the messenger rather than to the place, which is consistent with the sūrah's insistence that a community is defined by its response to the one sent to it.

**What the Refrain Does to the Reader**

The repetition of the formula across three narratives produces an effect the sūrah depends on. By the time the reader reaches the third instance, the exclamation is expected, and expectation is what makes it land. Each destroyed town is not an individual tragedy but a case in a series with a fixed outcome. The sūrah's closing verses will make the point explicitly, describing these accounts as stories of the destroyed cities, some still standing and others mown down. The refrain is that point delivered as sound rather than as argument. It also prepares the reader for the sūrah's own present. Mekka had heard the formula's content — worship one God or face the consequence — and the refrain tells them that the sequence has a fixed ending for towns that refuse.

**The Verse as the End of the Second Narrative**

Structurally this verse closes the second of seven narratives, and comparing it with the end of the first shows the sūrah's variation. The Nūḥ narrative ended with an instruction to disembark, a blessing on descendants, and a command to patience. The Hūd narrative ends with a curse and a dismissal. The difference follows the material. Nūḥ's story contained a survivor's question about a promise, and the sūrah had to answer it. Hūd's story contained no such question; his people refused immediately and were destroyed quickly. The sūrah gives each narrative the ending its material requires and moves on. The next narrative begins in the same formula that began this one, and the reader is now trained to hear what the formula means.

****Why the Refrain Is Not Directed at the Reader****

The exclamation that closes this verse is easy to misread as hostility, and it is not. *Away with ʿĀd* is a verdict on a people who are gone, not a curse on anyone still deciding. The sūrah's own present contains people doing exactly what ʿĀd did, and the refrain is addressed to them as a warning rather than as a sentence. The distinction matters because the sūrah's whole structure depends on the reader still having time. Every narrative in the chapter ends with a refrain or a summary that sounds final, and every one of them was final for the people in it and remains open for the person reading it. That is why the sūrah ends not with a refrain but with a command to worship and to trust. The dismissals belong to the past; the last word of the chapter belongs to the reader.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:61

> And to the people of Thamûd We sent their brother Ṣâliḥ. He said, “O my people! Worship Allah. You have no god other than Him. He ˹is the One Who˺ produced you from the earth and settled you on it. So seek His forgiveness and turn to Him in repentance. Surely my Lord is Ever Near, All-Responsive ˹to prayers˺.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Narrative, the Same Opening**

The verse that begins the story of Ṣāliḥ uses the formula the sūrah has now used twice: *We sent to X their brother Y.* By the third repetition the reader recognises the shape before the content arrives, and that recognition is the point. The sūrah is not assembling independent stories; it is running a series in which the constants are visible and the variables are what matter. The constants here are the sending, the kinship, the demand for exclusive worship. The variables are the argument Ṣāliḥ adds and the sign he is given. A reader who has followed Nūḥ and Hūd knows what is coming and can attend to what is new, which is exactly how the sūrah wants the material received.

**"Produced You From the Earth and Settled You On It"**

The new element is an argument from origin. God brought you forth from the earth and made you live in it. The two verbs are precise: *anshaʾakum*, He originated you, caused you to arise; *istaʿmarakum*, He settled you, gave you the land to inhabit and develop. The second word is from the same root as settlement and cultivation, and it carries the sense of being placed somewhere with a purpose. The argument is aimed at a people whose identity was tied to their land. Thamūd inhabited a rocky plain in western Arabia between the Ḥijāz and Syria, carving dwellings into stone, and their standing rested on that achievement. Ṣāliḥ tells them the land is not theirs by right of construction. They were placed in it.

**Why This Argument Appears Here and Not Before**

Nūḥ's people were not given an argument from origin; Hūd's were not either. The commentators note that Ṣāliḥ's call to worship is word for word what Hūd said, and then he adds this. The addition follows the audience. Thamūd were builders, and a builder's natural assumption is that what he has made belongs to him. The argument from origin meets that assumption directly: the material, the site and the capacity to work it all came from elsewhere. The sūrah's method throughout is that each messenger begins from what his people already accept and moves from there. Nūḥ began from the flood; Hūd from the drought; Ṣāliḥ from the ground under their houses. The messenger's argument is chosen for the town, not taken from a stock.

**Seek Forgiveness, Then Turn — the Third Time**

The call to repentance appears in this sūrah at its opening, in Hūd's speech, and now in Ṣāliḥ's. The formula is identical in structure: seek forgiveness, then turn. Its repetition across three missions separated by centuries is part of the sūrah's evidence. The remedy for idolatry does not vary with the culture, the period or the particular idols involved. What varies is the attached promise — provision for a term in the opening, rain and strength for ʿĀd, and here the nearness of God. The variation is responsive rather than arbitrary, and the constancy of the command alongside the variation of the promise is precisely what one would expect from a single source addressing different audiences.

**"My Lord Is Ever Near"**

The verse closes on two divine names, and the first is the sūrah's most intimate. *Qarīb* — near. The commentators gloss it as nearness to those who are sincere in worship and wish to repent, while noting the theological point that God is close to all people, including those who feel distant from Him or deny Him; sincerity is not a condition of His closeness but of awareness of it. The Qurʾān states the same nearness in direct terms: *"When My servants ask you ˹O Prophet˺ about Me: I am truly near. I respond to one's prayer when they call upon Me"* (2:186). Placing nearness at the end of a call to repentance is deliberate. A person asked to abandon an inherited religion needs to know that the alternative is accessible, and the verse tells him it is not far away.

**"All-Responsive" — the Answer Is Already Arranged**

The second name is *mujīb* — one who answers, who responds to the one who calls. The pairing with nearness is exact: a God who is near but does not answer would be present without being available; one who answers but is distant would be reachable only with difficulty. The two together describe the condition repentance requires. The sūrah has described the opposite condition twice — people who cover their heads to avoid hearing, and people who ask what is holding the punishment back. Both are failures of relation. This verse offers the relation from the other side: call, and there is an answer. The name is also a quiet rebuke to the idols, which are addressed constantly by Thamūd and answer never.

**What Changes in the Third Case**

Comparing the three openings shows the sūrah's variation at work. Nūḥ described his office and his fear. Hūd named his people's crime and refused payment. Ṣāliḥ offers an argument from their own land and closes on the nearness of God. The escalation is toward intimacy rather than toward confrontation, which makes what follows sharper. This is the narrative in which the people's objection is the most personal — *we had high hopes in you* — and in which the sign given is the most concrete and the violation of it the most direct. The sūrah has spent two narratives establishing the pattern; the third will break it in a specific way, and the breaking is what the reader is being prepared for.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:62

> They argued, “O Ṣâliḥ! We truly had high hopes in you before this. How dare you forbid us to worship what our forefathers had worshipped? We are certainly in alarming doubt about what you are inviting us to.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Most Personal Objection in the Sūrah**

Every previous reply in this chapter has attacked the messenger's credentials: he is only a man, his followers are nobodies, he has brought no proof, he is bewitched. This reply attacks nothing of the kind. It expresses disappointment. The Thamūd are not disputing Ṣāliḥ's honesty or his sanity. They are telling him he has let them down. The shift in register is the verse's most significant feature, and it makes the objection harder to answer than any of the others. A man accused of lying can produce evidence. A man told that his people had expected better of him has nothing to produce, because the accusation is not about facts. The sūrah places this objection in the third narrative for a reason: it is the objection most likely to move a messenger.

**"We Had High Hopes in You"**

The commentators explain the phrase concretely: before Ṣāliḥ made his call, his people had expected him to become a leader among them, since he was already one of the most noble and respected figures in their society. The verse therefore records a specific social fact. Ṣāliḥ was not an outsider or a marginal figure. He was being groomed for prominence, and his message cost him the position he was expected to occupy. That is what gives the objection its force. It is not an argument; it is a reminder of what he has thrown away. The sūrah has described the same pressure in its Mekkan section, where the messenger was tempted to omit part of the revelation in order to ease the hostility of his hearers. Here the pressure is dressed as affection.

**A Complaint About a Career, Not a Doctrine**

Read carefully, the verse never engages Ṣāliḥ's argument. He had said that God produced them from the earth and settled them on it; the reply does not address it. He had called them to seek forgiveness and turn; the reply does not address that either. What it addresses is the social consequence of his message — that he is now forbidding them to do what they have always done. The objection is about the disruption, not the content. The sūrah has been tracing this pattern since its opening, where the hearers covered their heads rather than listen. This is the same avoidance in a more sophisticated form: a response that appears to engage while declining to touch anything the messenger actually said.

**"How Dare You Forbid Us"**

The middle clause is a question of authority, and its form is worth noting. They do not ask whether the prohibition is true. They ask how he dares issue it. The question assumes that forbidding is a prerogative, and that Ṣāliḥ has exceeded his. The assumption underneath is that religious practice in their town is not subject to individual judgement — certainly not to the judgement of one man, however respected. The verse is therefore a defence of the town's autonomy as much as of its theology. The sūrah identifies the same claim in its Mekkan section, where the demand was that the recitation not condemn the town's gods. In both cases the objection is to the messenger's right to interfere, rather than to the accuracy of what he says.

**The Forefathers Again**

The clause about what the forefathers worshipped is the standard objection of every destroyed people in this sūrah, and the Qurʾān records it repeatedly: *"We found our forefathers worshipping them"* (21:53), *"What we found our forefathers practicing is good enough for us"* (5:104). The Book answers it directly elsewhere: *"˹Would they still do so,˺ even if their forefathers had ˹absolutely˺ no understanding or guidance?"* (2:170). The answer identifies the flaw in the appeal: inheritance is not evidence. The Thamūd's formulation is slightly different from the others, because it is embedded in a complaint about Ṣāliḥ rather than stated as a principle. That embedding is what makes it effective. A principle can be examined; a grievance has to be soothed.

**"Alarming Doubt"**

The final clause names the state the message has produced in them: *we are certainly in alarming doubt about what you are inviting us to.* The word for alarming is *murība*, from a root meaning doubt that disturbs and unsettles. The choice is precise and it is not a confession of open-mindedness. These people are not weighing evidence. They are reporting that their comfort has been disturbed. The sūrah has used the same word elsewhere for a doubt that agitates, and the distinction matters: a person in settled disbelief has closed the question; a person in alarming doubt has had it reopened and resents the reopening. The verse therefore records resentment rather than enquiry, and it is the most honest statement the Thamūd make in the narrative.

**What the Objection Reveals About Their Position**

Taken together, the three clauses describe a people whose objection is entirely social. They are disappointed in a man, affronted by a prohibition, and unsettled by a doubt. Nothing in the verse concerns the truth of the claim. The sūrah records it at this length because it is the objection most readers will recognise in themselves. Nobody says *I have examined the argument and found it false*. People say *I expected better of you*, *who are you to tell us*, and *this is making me uncomfortable*. The verse is therefore the sūrah's most useful diagnostic, and it explains why the reply that follows does not argue either. Ṣāliḥ will restate his position and ask a question about his own exposure, because a social objection cannot be met with evidence.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:63

> He responded, “O my people! Consider if I stand on a clear proof from my Lord and He has blessed me with a mercy from Him. Who could help me against Allah if I were to disobey Him? You would only contribute to my doom

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Formula a Third Time**

Ṣāliḥ's reply opens with the sentence the sūrah has now put in three messengers' mouths: consider if I stand on a clear proof from my Lord, and He has given me a mercy from Himself. Nūḥ said it, and this Prophet was instructed to say it to the Quraysh. The repetition is the sūrah's evidence for the fixed character of the prophetic claim. A man inventing a case adapts it; a man transmitting a message repeats it, and the repetition makes the claim examinable. Ṣāliḥ does not answer the disappointment expressed in the previous verse, does not defend his decision to give up a leadership position, and does not argue about the forefathers. He restates where he stands, which is the only reply available to a messenger facing a social objection.

**A Clear Proof and a Mercy From Himself**

The two elements are the same pair the earlier verses distinguished. The clear proof is the manifest evidence of the message; the mercy, the commentators say, is the conferral of prophethood on Ṣāliḥ. Both come from God, and neither is his achievement. The distinction matters here more than it did in the earlier cases, because Ṣāliḥ's people had a specific social reason to think well of him. They had expected him to lead them. The verse redirects that estimation: whatever standing he has is not the standing they were counting on, and it does not come from the sources they recognise. A man whose authority rests on divine conferral cannot be evaluated by the town's hierarchy, and the town's disappointment is therefore based on a category error.

**"Who Could Help Me Against Allah?"**

The rhetorical question is the same one Nūḥ asked thirty-three verses earlier, and the commentators note the parallel. The form is practical rather than theological: if I disobey Him, who will shield me? The expected answer is nobody. The question converts the town's demand into a question about Ṣāliḥ's own exposure. They are asking him to accommodate their practice; he is telling them that accommodation would leave him undefended. The verse therefore answers the objection without addressing it. A man being asked to compromise is not answering with a principle when he says *who protects me if I do?* He is answering with a fact about consequences, and the fact is not negotiable.

**"You Would Only Add to My Loss"**

The clause that follows is unusually blunt. The commentators gloss the loss as misguidance, or as Ṣāliḥ's actions coming to nothing. Either way the statement is direct: your help would harm me. The people who had hoped he would lead them are being told that their support is a liability. The verse is making a claim about the direction of assistance. Help that requires a messenger to soften his message is not help to the messenger; it is help to the audience, purchased at his expense. The sūrah described the same mechanism in its Mekkan section, where the temptation was to omit part of the revelation in order to make the rest bearable. Ṣāliḥ names that mechanism from the other side and refuses it.

**The Question Is Not Rhetorical**

It would be easy to treat Ṣāliḥ's question as a figure of speech, and the sūrah resists that reading. The question has a definite answer that can be checked: no created being has standing with God on the Day. The sūrah has already established this in its doctrinal section, where the wrongdoers were said to have no protector besides Allah and no escape on earth. Ṣāliḥ is applying a settled fact to a present proposal. The verse thereby converts an emotional appeal — *we had hopes in you* — into a practical calculation. The town's affection cannot protect him. Their support cannot shield him. What they are offering him, in exchange for a compromise, is worth nothing on the day that matters, and the verse says so without softening it.

**Why This Reply Answers an Objection It Does Not Mention**

The structure of the exchange is worth identifying, because it is the sūrah's standard method. The people raise a social objection; the messenger answers with his own position and his own exposure. He never meets the objection on its own terms, and the refusal is the answer. To argue about whether he should have accepted a leadership role would be to concede that the role was his to accept or refuse on those grounds. To argue about the forefathers would be to accept that ancestry is a relevant category. Ṣāliḥ declines both frames and states the only thing he can state: where his authority comes from and what it would cost him to betray it. The verse is the practical form of the principle the sūrah announced at its beginning — the message is not the messenger's to edit.

****What the Verse Leaves for the Reader to Supply****

The reply is short, and its shortness is a feature. Ṣāliḥ does not explain why he gave up a leadership position, does not describe the cost to himself, and does not appeal to his people's affection. He states his ground and his exposure, and stops. What he leaves unsaid is supplied by the reader, who has watched a respected man lose his standing for a message he will not soften. The sūrah depends on that supply. A messenger who narrates his own sacrifice invites sympathy, and sympathy is not what the message needs. A messenger who states his position and lets the facts speak produces something harder to dismiss: the visible fact of a man who has paid and is still standing where he was. The verse is therefore an argument made by omission, and the omission is the most persuasive part of it.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:64

> And O my people! This she-camel of Allah is a sign for you. So leave her to graze ˹freely˺ on Allah’s earth and do her no harm, or a swift punishment will overtake you!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sign Given in Answer to a Demand**

The she-camel is not introduced as an unsolicited miracle. The parallel accounts show that Thamūd had asked for a sign: *"You are only a human being like us, so bring forth a sign if what you say is true"* (26:154). The commentators record that Ṣāliḥ brought the pregnant camel forth from a large rock in response to that request. The sequence matters for evaluating what follows. This is not a case of a people being given an arbitrary test. They set the terms, the terms were met, and the sign was produced in the form and place they specified. The sūrah's method throughout is to place responsibility with the party that had the choice, and here the choice was explicit. A people who ask for proof and receive it have exhausted their objection.

**"This She-Camel of Allah"**

The possessive construction is the verse's theological centre. The camel is not Ṣāliḥ's, not the town's, and not a wild animal. She belongs to God, and the attribution is repeated in the parallel account: *"this is Allah's she-camel as a sign to you"* (7:73). Naming her as God's property does two things. It removes her from the town's jurisdiction, which is why the command that follows concerns leaving her alone. And it makes any harm done to her an act against the owner rather than against an animal. The verse is therefore not a rule about animal welfare in the abstract. It is a statement about ownership, and the ownership is what makes the coming violation a direct offence rather than a local dispute.

**"Leave Her to Graze on Allah's Earth"**

The instruction is a permission granted to the camel and a restriction placed on the people. She is to graze freely, and the earth she grazes on is described as God's. The phrase is significant in a narrative about a people whose identity rested on their land. The ground is not theirs; it is His, and He has allocated a share of it to her. The verse reverses the assumption Ṣāliḥ had challenged in his opening speech, where he reminded them that God had produced them from the earth and settled them on it. The settlement was a grant, and grants can include other beneficiaries. The town's objection to the camel is, at bottom, an objection to sharing what they had come to regard as exclusively theirs.

**The Drinking Arrangement**

The parallel accounts supply the other half of the command, which this sūrah leaves implicit. The water was to be divided: *"She will have her turn to drink as you have yours, each on an appointed day"* (26:155), and *"the ˹drinking˺ water must be divided between them ˹and her˺, each taking a turn to drink"* (54:28). The arrangement is precise and it is the practical form of the test. A shared resource with an alternating schedule is the clearest possible measure of whether a people will honour an allocation they did not choose. The camel takes a full day's water, which in a dry region is a substantial cost, and the cost is what the town resents. The verse is not testing their theology in the abstract. It is testing whether they will keep a schedule.

**"Do Her No Harm"**

The prohibition is stated absolutely, and the consequence is attached: a swift punishment. The word for swift is *qarīb*, near, which is the same root as the divine name given at the end of Ṣāliḥ's opening speech. The nearness offered there was nearness to a penitent; the nearness here is the nearness of the punishment. The pairing is deliberate and it gives the verse its balance. God is near to those who turn and near with consequence for those who harm what is His. The sūrah has made the same pairing throughout, most clearly in the person who despairs when mercy is withdrawn and boasts when it returns. The verse offers both possibilities in one sentence, and the town will choose between them in the next verse.

**A Test, Not a Gift**

The Qurʾān describes the camel's function explicitly elsewhere: *"We are sending the she-camel as a test for them. So watch them ˹closely˺, and have patience"* (54:27). The verse is addressed to Ṣāliḥ, and it tells him what the camel is for. She is not a benefit conferred on Thamūd, not a source of milk or labour, and not a reward for anything. She is an instrument of examination. The description explains why the arrangement is costly rather than convenient. A test that costs nothing measures nothing. The town's resentment of the camel is therefore not a misunderstanding of her purpose; it is the correct perception of a burden, and their response to the burden is what the narrative is recording.

**Why the Sign Is an Animal**

The choice of sign is unusual and deserves a moment. Other messengers were given staffs, hands, winds, floods. Ṣāliḥ was given a camel — an ordinary animal of the region, made extraordinary by her origin and her allocation. The ordinariness is the point. The sign does not overwhelm; it does not compel belief by its spectacle. It sits in the town's watering place every other day and requires a decision. A miracle that compels would destroy the freedom on which the whole prophetic enterprise depends, as the sūrah's twenty-eighth verse established. A camel requires the town to keep behaving normally while accepting a restriction they did not choose. That is a test of character rather than of perception, and it is harder to pass.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:65

> But they killed her, so he warned ˹them˺, “You have ˹only˺ three ˹more˺ days to enjoy life in your homes—this is an unfailing promise!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Whole Violation in Two Words**

The verse reports the central act of the narrative in a single verb: they hamstrung her. No account of the decision, no description of the person who did it, no record of a debate. The sūrah has spent two verses establishing the camel's status, the arrangement for her water, and the warning attached to harming her, and then it disposes of the violation in a word. The economy is a judgement. What Thamūd did was not complicated and does not require explanation. They were given a specific instruction about a specific animal, and they killed the animal. Everything else in the narrative — the disappointment, the appeal to the forefathers, the doubt — was preamble to this.

**Why Hamstringing Specifically**

The verb is precise: *ʿaqarūhā*, they cut her leg sinews. Hamstringing is not the quickest way to kill an animal; it is the way to disable one so that it can be finished at leisure. The choice of method is recorded because it removes any reading in which the killing was an accident, a panic or a mercy. It was a deliberate, staged act performed on an animal the town knew belonged to God. The parallel account states the disposition behind it: *"Then they killed the she-camel—defying their Lord's command"* (7:77). The defiance is explicit, and the sūrah of al-Aʿrāf adds that they then challenged Ṣāliḥ to bring on the punishment he had threatened. The sequence — disable, kill, taunt — is the same sequence the sūrah recorded in Nūḥ's narrative and in its Mekkan section.

**"Three Days in Your Homes"**

Ṣāliḥ's response is a sentence with an unusual shape. He does not announce the punishment; he announces a period of enjoyment. Three days, in your homes. The specification of place is significant. The sūrah will report that morning found them lifeless in their homes, and the two phrases answer each other. The homes they are told to enjoy are the homes in which they will die. The verse is not being ironic for effect. It is stating the terms of the interval exactly: you have three days, and you will spend them where you have always spent them, and nothing about your surroundings will warn you.

**Why Three Days**

The three-day term is distinctive in the sūrah's narratives. Other peoples were destroyed without notice of the interval. Thamūd are given a count, and the count does specific work. It removes the possibility of attributing the punishment to a natural event that happened to arrive. A people told on a specific day that they have three days cannot later say the disaster was unrelated. The sūrah's eighth verse made the same point about the general structure of delay: the interval has an appointed end, and the end is known to the one who set it. Here the end is known to the people as well, which is rare, and the knowledge is what makes the coming event unmistakable.

**"A Promise That Will Not Be Belied"**

The final clause is a guarantee, and its wording is worth holding. The promise will not be falsified, not belied, not shown to be false. The Qurʾān uses the same root elsewhere to describe a promise that cannot be broken. The clause answers the specific form of the town's unbelief. They had been in *alarming doubt*, as the previous verse recorded, and doubt of that kind expresses itself in the expectation that nothing will happen. Ṣāliḥ is telling them that the expectation will be disappointed on a date. The verse is therefore the sharpest form the sūrah's argument takes: not a general warning about a distant Day, but a dated announcement made to people who will live through the interval and be able to check it.

**What the Three Days Were For**

The commentators note that the Thamūd used the interval to challenge Ṣāliḥ further, demanding that he bring on the punishment if he was truly a messenger. On that reading the three days were not a reprieve but a final test, and the town failed it in the same way it had failed every previous one. A reader might reasonably ask what a person in that position could have done. The verse does not say, and the sūrah does not elaborate, but the answer is available in the opening speech: seek forgiveness and turn. The door Ṣāliḥ opened in his first words was not formally closed by his last. What closed it was three days of continued defiance, and the sūrah's account of the interval is the record of that closing.

**The Verse as the Pivot of the Narrative**

Structurally this verse ends the Thamūd story's argumentative phase and opens its conclusion. After it there is no further speech from either side. The narrative moves to the arrival of the command, the rescue of the believers, the blast and the summary verdict. The pivot is marked by the shift in what is being said: the messenger is no longer persuading but dating. The sūrah uses the same structure in every narrative — dialogue, a final statement, then the event — and the consistency is part of its method. A reader who has followed three cases can now predict the shape of the remaining four, and the predictability is what makes the section an argument rather than a collection. What varies is the material; what does not vary is the sequence.

****The Shortest Interval in the Sūrah****

Compared with the other intervals in this chapter, three days is nothing. Nūḥ's people had centuries between the warning and the water. ʿĀd had a drought, a promise of rain, and then a cloud. Thamūd are given a count they can hold in their heads. The brevity changes the character of the test. A long interval allows a person to forget, to rationalise, to convince himself that the warning was rhetorical. A three-day interval permits none of that. Every hour of it is a decision, and the town made the same decision repeatedly until the count ran out. The sūrah is showing that the length of a reprieve does not determine how it is used. Some people are given centuries and do not turn; these were given seventy-two hours and did not turn either.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:66

> When Our command came, We saved Ṣâliḥ and those who believed with him by a mercy from Us and spared them the disgrace of that Day. Surely your Lord ˹alone˺ is the All-Powerful, Almighty

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command Arrives on Schedule**

The verse opens with the arrival of the command, and the phrase is the same one used in the flood narrative and in the destruction of ʿĀd. Its repetition across three cases establishes it as a technical term in this sūrah: the moment at which a stated decree is executed. What makes this instance distinctive is that the timing had been announced. The Thamūd had been given three days, and the command came at the end of them. The sūrah has been arguing since its eighth verse that delay is not evidence against a promise; here it demonstrates the point in a case where the interval was specified in advance. A reader who doubts the sūrah's claim about appointed times has been given a dated example to check.

**Saved by a Mercy — the Same Phrase as ʿĀd**

The rescue is described in words identical to those used eight verses earlier for Hūd's believers: saved by a mercy from Us. The repetition is not carelessness. The sūrah is insisting that the ground of rescue is the same in every case, and that the ground is mercy rather than merit, location or cleverness. The commentators make the same observation for both narratives. What distinguishes this verse is the addition that follows. The ʿĀd believers were saved from a harsh torment; Ṣāliḥ's believers were saved from the torment *and* from something else. The addition is what makes this rescue description the fullest in the sūrah so far.

**"And From the Disgrace of That Day"**

The extra clause is the verse's distinctive contribution. The believers were spared not only the punishment but the disgrace of the day. The word *khizy* denotes humiliation, exposure, the loss of standing — the same word used in verse 39 for the humiliating torment promised to Nūḥ's mockers. The sūrah has treated disgrace as a distinct category throughout, and its treatment is consistent: destruction is one thing, and being disgraced in the destruction is another. A person can die without being humiliated, and the verse says that this mercy was given to the believers. The addition tells the reader that the rescue was complete in a way that mere survival is not.

**Why Disgrace Is Named Separately**

The separation of the two gifts answers a question the Thamūd narrative raises. These people had spent the narrative mocking their messenger — expressing disappointment in him, challenging him to produce a sign, hamstringing the sign he produced, and then daring him to bring the punishment. Their destruction would have been a judgement; their disgrace would have been a spectacle. The verse says the believers were spared the spectacle. That is a meaningful distinction, and it explains why the sūrah keeps the two concepts apart. The punishment is what a person owes. The disgrace is what a person becomes in the eyes of others while paying it. Being removed from both is the fuller mercy, and the verse names both.

**"Your Lord Is the Strong, the Mighty"**

The verse closes on two divine names, and the pairing is characteristic of this sūrah's endings. *Qawī* — strong, possessing power; *ʿazīz* — mighty, unconquerable, whose will cannot be thwarted. The two names answer two different doubts. Strength answers the question of capacity: could God have carried out the punishment? Might answers the question of opposition: could anyone have prevented it? The sūrah has addressed both doubts in its doctrinal section, where the wrongdoers were told they could not frustrate God on earth and had no protector besides Him. The verse restates the point at the moment of execution, which is where it has its full force. The punishment did not arrive because the town was weak. It arrived because the one who decreed it is strong, and nobody could intervene.

**What the Two Names Do for the Reader**

The placement of the names at the end of a rescue narrative is worth noticing. The verse is not primarily about the punished; it is about the saved, and it ends by describing the one who saved them. The effect is to move the reader's attention from the event to its source. A person who reads only the story learns that some people survived and others did not. A person who reads the closing clause learns who decided, and that the decision rested on power that could not be resisted and might that could not be opposed. The sūrah will use the same structure at the end of the Midian narrative, and the consistency teaches the reader how to receive every account in the section. The stories are not about the towns. They are about the one who dealt with the towns.

****The Rescue as the Verse's Real Subject****

It is easy to read this verse as a transition between the punishment and its description, and the sūrah resists that reading. The verse spends more words on the saved than on the destroyed. Ṣāliḥ and the believers are named, their rescue is attributed to mercy, and a second gift is added to the first. The punishment occupies no words at all in this verse; it arrives in the next one. The proportion tells the reader what the sūrah considers the important part of the account. Destruction is the consequence and requires no elaboration. Rescue is the exception, and the exception is what a reader needs to understand, because the exception is the part that can still be chosen.

****Why the Names Close a Rescue Rather Than a Punishment****

The two divine names at the end of the verse could have been placed after the description of the blast, where they would have read as a justification of the punishment. The sūrah places them after the rescue instead, and the placement changes their function. Strength and might, stated over a saved group, describe the one who was able to remove them from harm. The same names stated over a destroyed group would describe the one who was able to harm. Both statements are true, and the sūrah chooses to make the first one last. The choice is consistent with its whole method: the narratives are addressed to people who still have a choice, and a verse ending on capacity to save leaves that choice open in a way that a verse ending on capacity to destroy would not.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:67

> And the ˹mighty˺ blast overtook the wrongdoers, so they fell lifeless in their homes

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Punishment Named as a Sound**

The verse identifies what destroyed Thamūd, and the identification is unusual. It was not water, not wind, not fire. It was a cry — *al-ṣayḥa*, a shout, a blast, an overwhelming sound. The commentators connect it to the earthquake recorded in the parallel account: *"Then an ˹overwhelming˺ earthquake struck them, and they fell lifeless in their homes"* (7:78), and they take the two descriptions as referring to one event, an earthquake accompanied by a terrible sound. The naming of the punishment as a sound is significant. It is the least material of all the punishments in the sūrah. A flood can be escaped by height, a wind by shelter, stones by cover. A sound passes through walls, and the verse says it did.

**The Cry as an Agent**

The grammar of the verse gives the sound an active role. *The cry seized them* — the verb is the one used for grasping or taking hold of a person. The sound is not described as occurring; it is described as doing something to them. The sūrah uses the same verb elsewhere for divine seizure, and its use here makes the punishment an act rather than an event. The Qurʾān describes the same agency in its account of how God deals with societies entrenched in wrongdoing, speaking of a grip that is painful and severe. The verse is therefore not reporting a natural disaster that coincided with a prophecy. It is reporting an execution, and the instrument happens to be acoustic.

**"Those Who Did Wrong"**
