The verse identifies its victims by a moral description rather than by name. Not *Thamūd*, but *those who did wrong*. The choice is deliberate and it does two things. It connects the destruction to the charge rather than to the ethnicity, which means the reader cannot treat the event as something that happened to a particular ancient tribe. And it makes the description transferable. Any group that does what Thamūd did falls under the same label, and the sūrah will apply the same vocabulary to Midian later in this chapter. The verse is therefore a rule stated in the form of a report, which is the sūrah's preferred method throughout its narrative section.

**"Morning Found Them Lifeless"**

The second clause fixes a time, and the time is ordinary. Morning. The verse does not say the cry came at night, though the implication is clear, and the choice to mark the morning rather than the night is significant. Morning is the moment of discovery. The sūrah is describing what the survivors — meaning Ṣāliḥ and the believers — found when the day broke. The people who had been given three days to enjoy their homes were in them, dead, at the start of the fourth. The specification removes any ambiguity about the timing and confirms that the promise had been kept to the day. The sūrah has been arguing throughout that appointed terms are real; this verse is the confirmation in a case where the term had been announced.

**Why the Homes Matter**

The verse is insistent on location. They fell lifeless *in their homes*. The phrase is repeated in the next verse and again in the Midian narrative, and the repetition is doing work. Thamūd were builders; their standing rested on structures carved into rock. The sūrah's opening speech from Ṣāliḥ had reminded them that God produced them from the earth and settled them on it. The verse now shows them dead inside the settlement, in the houses that were the evidence of their achievement. The location is the completion of the argument. What they built did not protect them, and the place where they felt most secure is where they fell. The verse is not making a point about architecture. It is making a point about what a person relies on.

**The Verse as a Report Rather Than a Description**

The economy of this verse is worth identifying, because it is characteristic of the sūrah's endings. There is no description of the sound, no account of what it felt like, no detail about the destruction of the buildings. Two clauses: the cry took them, morning found them dead. The sūrah withholds the spectacle deliberately. A narrative that lingered on the horror would become a story about suffering; a narrative that reports the facts in a flat register keeps the reader's attention on the sequence of choices that produced them. The Thamūd asked for a sign, received it, disabled it, killed it, taunted the messenger about it, were given three days, and then were dead in their houses. The verse states the last item in that list and nothing more.

****Why No Detail Is Given****

The verse withholds everything a narrative would ordinarily supply. There is no description of the sound, no account of the shaking, no report of what happened to the rock-cut houses. The commentators must go to the parallel account in al-Aʿrāf to learn that an earthquake accompanied the cry. The withholding is a method rather than a limitation. A detailed description would make the event a spectacle, and a spectacle invites the reader to observe rather than to apply. The flat report keeps the reader's attention on the sequence: a sign demanded, a sign given, a sign killed, a term announced, a term completed. Every step in that sequence is a decision, and the decisions are what the verse is about. The sound is only the last item in a list the town compiled itself.

****The Verse and the Sūrah's Mekkan Audience****

The original listeners had a specific reason to attend to this account. Thamūd's territory lay in western Arabia on the route between the Ḥijāz and Syria, a route the Makkans travelled for trade. The destroyed towns were not distant legends to them; they were places they passed. The sūrah makes the point explicitly near its end, noting that some of the destroyed cities are still standing and others have been mown down. The verse therefore reports an event whose remains the audience could verify, and the verification is part of the argument. A punishment that left visible traces in a landscape a merchant caravan crossed twice a year is not the same kind of claim as a story about a people nobody had heard of.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:68

> as if they had never lived there. Surely Thamûd denied their Lord, so away with Thamûd

**Expanded Commentary**

**"As Though They Had Never Thrived There"**

The verse opens by completing the previous one, and the phrase it uses is precise. The Arabic verb behind *lived* carries the sense of thriving, prospering, dwelling in comfort — not merely existing. The Thamūd did not simply occupy their homes; they flourished in them, and the verse says the flourishing left no trace. The commentators note that the identical wording is used for Midian later in this sūrah, which marks it as a formula. The formula's function is to describe a specific kind of erasure. A town that is destroyed but remembered has left something behind. A town described as though it had never prospered has left nothing, and the nothing is what the verse is reporting.

**The Erasure of a Civilisation's Evidence**

Thamūd were the sūrah's builders. Their dwellings were cut into rock, which is the most permanent form of construction available to an ancient people, and the Qurʾān elsewhere describes them as carving houses from mountains with skill. The verse says the result was as though none of it had happened. The claim is not that the structures vanished; the sūrah notes elsewhere that some destroyed cities are still standing and others have been mown down. The claim is that the prosperity is gone and cannot be recovered. A reader standing among the ruins sees stone, not a civilisation. The verse therefore separates two things that are usually fused: the survival of the remains and the survival of the life. The first may continue; the second does not.

**"Surely Thamūd Denied Their Lord"**

The middle clause repeats the charge the sūrah made against ʿĀd eight verses earlier, in the same words with a different name. The repetition is the sūrah's method of summary: after the narrative, the root failure is named. Denial of the Lord is the origin of everything else — the rejection of the messenger, the appeal to the forefathers, the hamstringing of the camel, the taunt about bringing on the punishment. The verse reduces eight verses of narrative to that single diagnosis, and the reduction is what makes the section portable. A reader who remembers nothing else can remember the charge, and the charge is the same in every case. The title used is *their Lord*, the one who nurtures and maintains, which is what makes the denial more than an intellectual position.

**"Away With Thamūd" — the Refrain a Second Time**

The exclamation that closed the ʿĀd narrative closes this one too, and the commentators note that it will come a third time for Midian. The repetition is a structural device with a specific effect. By the second instance the reader knows what is coming, and the expectation is what gives the formula its weight. It is not an outburst; it is a verdict spoken in a fixed form over a people who have been removed from the account. The verse adds nothing to it. There is no enumeration of crimes, no description of suffering, no reflection on what might have been. A civilisation that occupied eight verses of narrative is closed in three words, and the brevity is the sūrah's assessment of what a refusal amounts to.

**What the Refrain's Repetition Teaches**

The formula's recurrence across three narratives produces a cumulative effect that no single instance could. Each destroyed town is not an individual tragedy but one case in a series with a fixed outcome, and the series is what the reader is meant to carry away. The sūrah will make the point explicitly near its end, describing these accounts as stories of the destroyed cities, some still standing and others mown down. The refrain is that point delivered as sound rather than as argument, and sound is what a recited text has available to it. A person hearing this sūrah read aloud hears the same dismissal three times, and by the third time it is no longer information but a rhythm.

**The Verse and the Reader's Own Town**

The refrain is addressed to a people who are gone, and it is spoken to an audience who are not. The sūrah's original listeners in Mekka were doing what Thamūd did: appealing to the forefathers, doubting the messenger, asking for signs, and expressing disappointment in a man they had respected. The verse is therefore not a closed statement about an ancient tribe. It is a formula with an open application, and the application is decided by the hearer's conduct rather than by his ancestry. That is why the sūrah does not end its narrative section with a refrain. It ends with a command to be steadfast, to pray, and to trust. The dismissals belong to the peoples who were dismissed; the last word of the chapter is addressed to whoever is still listening.

****The Refrain as a Unit of Measure****

By the time the refrain appears for the second time, it has become a measure. Each destroyed people is weighed and given the same verdict in the same words, and the uniformity is the sūrah's claim: the cases are equivalent. ʿĀd had a drought and were offered rain. Thamūd had a sign and killed it. The circumstances differed; the failure did not. The refrain records the equivalence without arguing for it, which is why the sūrah can use so few words. A verdict that has to be explained at length is a verdict in dispute. A verdict stated in a fixed formula three times over is a verdict the sūrah treats as settled, and it invites the reader to treat the fourth case the same way.

****What Remains, and What Does Not****

The verse's claim that it was as though they had never thrived there is compatible with the survival of physical remains, and the sūrah says so elsewhere: some destroyed cities are still standing and others have been mown down. The two statements together define what is and is not permanent. Stone survives. Prosperity does not. A traveller passing the rock-cut dwellings of Thamūd sees the work but not the life, and the gap between the two is the verse's subject. The claim is therefore not about archaeology but about what a civilisation actually consists of. It consists of people in a relation to the one who settled them, and when that relation is severed the stones become evidence of nothing except that someone once carved them.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:69

> And surely Our messenger-angels came to Abraham with good news ˹of a son˺. They greeted ˹him with˺, “Peace!” And he replied, “Peace ˹be upon you˺!” Then it was not long before he brought ˹them˺ a ˹fat,˺ roasted calf

**Expanded Commentary**

**A New Kind of Narrative Begins**

The fourth narrative differs from the three before it in structure. There is no opening formula — no *We sent to X their brother Y* — and no demand for exclusive worship addressed to a town. The verse opens with visitors arriving at a house. The sūrah has spent forty-four verses on the pattern of a messenger sent to a people; here it turns to a domestic scene, and the turn is the point. Ibrāhīm is not being shown preaching. He is being shown hosting, and the narrative that follows will concern his family and his intercession rather than his public mission. The reader who expects the familiar shape will not find it, and the change of shape signals a change of subject.

**"Our Messengers" — Plural and Unnamed**

The verse calls the visitors *our messengers*, and the commentators identify them as a group of angels, with the reports varying on their number. The Qurʾān describes them elsewhere as Ibrāhīm's honoured guests (51:24) and simply as his guests (15:51). Neither their names nor their number is given here, and the sūrah's silence is consistent with its treatment of the unseen throughout: it reports what is needed and omits what is not. What matters for the narrative is that they came from God, that they came with news, and that Ibrāhīm did not initially know what they were. The verse sets up a scene in which a prophet is uncertain about his own guests, and the uncertainty drives everything that follows.

**The Greeting Exchanged**

The visitors say *peace*, and Ibrāhīm answers *peace*. The exchange is short and its form is worth noting: the reply repeats the greeting rather than extending it, and the parallel account records that the reply was given with a reservation in his mind — *"˹These are˺ an unfamiliar people"* (51:25). The two facts together describe a host who is courteous and alert at the same time. He returns the greeting properly and he notices that these are strangers. The sūrah records both because both matter: the courtesy explains what he does next, and the alertness explains what he feels in the following verse. A reader who takes the greeting as mere formality misses the tension the verse is building.

**"He Hastened" — the Speed of Hospitality**

The verb describing Ibrāhīm's movement carries haste. He did not send for food; he went quickly and brought it himself. The parallel account is more explicit: *"Then he slipped off to his family and brought a fat ˹roasted˺ calf"* (51:26). The detail is not incidental. A man of Ibrāhīm's standing had household members who could have served, and the verse shows him doing it personally and quickly. The sūrah is recording a character through an action rather than describing it through adjectives. The haste is what the following verse measures the guests against: a host who moves this fast expects his guests to eat, and their failure to do so will mean something.

**A Fattened, Roasted Calf**

The food is described with two adjectives: fat and roasted. The commentators note that the parallel account calls it a fattened calf (51:26). The specificity serves the narrative rather than the appetite. A fattened animal is one kept for an occasion and slaughtered for it; roasting takes time. Ibrāhīm did not offer whatever was at hand. He gave the best available thing, prepared properly, to strangers who had not asked for anything. The sūrah is establishing the standard of the host before it introduces the anomaly of the guests, and the establishment requires detail. A reader needs to know how much was offered in order to understand what the refusal cost.

**Why the Sūrah Records the Meal**

The meal has no theological content. It is not a sign, not a miracle, not a command. It is a man feeding strangers. The sūrah records it at length because it is the ground on which the rest of the narrative stands. The fear in the next verse arises from the meal; the reassurance follows the fear; the good news follows the reassurance; the intercession follows the good news. Every step in the sequence depends on a host having been generous quickly. The verse is therefore not a digression into domestic detail. It is the first link in a chain, and the sūrah's method throughout — that behaviour reveals character and character determines outcome — is being applied here to a prophet rather than to a destroyed town.

**What the Verse Sets Up**

Read as an opening, the verse creates a specific expectation and prepares to break it. A guest who is greeted with peace and fed the best available food is a guest who will eat. The reader is being led toward the ordinary conclusion of a hospitality scene, and the next verse will refuse it. The sūrah uses this method throughout its narratives: it establishes what a people or a person expects, and then delivers what actually happens. Here the expectation is social rather than theological, which makes the reversal more intimate. Ibrāhīm is not about to be told that his town will be destroyed. He is about to discover that his guests are not what they appear, and the discovery will bring both news and dread in the same breath.

****The Verse's Place in the Sūrah's Sequence****

Coming after three narratives of destruction, this verse changes the emotional temperature of the chapter. The reader has just heard a blast take a town at morning and a refrain dismiss it. Now there is a greeting, a greeting returned, and a man hurrying to fetch food. The contrast is not accidental relief. The sūrah is showing that the same God who destroys towns receives guests, and that a prophet's life contains hospitality as well as warning. The placement also prepares the intercession that follows. A reader who has only seen judgement will read Ibrāhīm's plea as an anomaly. A reader who has just watched him serve a fattened calf understands where the plea comes from: a man who feeds strangers quickly will not watch a town be destroyed without saying something.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:70

> And when he saw that their hands did not reach for the food, he became suspicious and fearful of them. They reassured ˹him˺, “Do not be afraid! We are ˹angels˺ sent ˹only˺ against the people of Lot.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Signal That Something Was Wrong**

The verse turns on a small physical fact: their hands did not reach for the food. Nothing is said, nothing is refused in words, no explanation is offered. The guests simply do not eat. The sūrah records the observation rather than the conclusion, which is a mark of its narrative method — it shows the reader the evidence and lets the reaction follow. The parallel account gives the same sequence: he placed the food before them and asked *"Will you not eat?"*, and when they did not, he grew fearful (51:27–28). The fear is not presented as a failure of perception. It is the correct reading of a signal that, in the culture of the time, meant something specific.

**Hospitality as a Test of Intent**

The commentators explain the cultural logic directly: refusing to eat a host's food was a sign of hostility. A guest who accepts food has entered into a relation with his host and has, in effect, given a guarantee. A guest who declines has not. Ibrāhīm's fear is therefore not paranoia. He has performed the full rites of hospitality at speed and with the best he had, and the guests have declined the one act that would have established their intentions. The verse is recording a genuine assessment made by a man who understood the codes of his own society. The sūrah elsewhere shows the same sensitivity to social signals, and it treats them as evidence rather than as sentiment.

**Fear in the House of a Prophet**

The verse states plainly that Ibrāhīm was afraid, and the statement is worth holding. A prophet is not depicted here as serene, untroubled or supernaturally informed. He is a host who has noticed something wrong and is frightened in his own house. The Qurʾān's account of the same scene in al-Dhāriyāt records that he had already thought them an unfamiliar people (51:25), so the fear had a foundation before the food was refused. The sūrah's willingness to record a prophet's fear is consistent with its treatment of the messengers throughout: Nūḥ distressed, this Prophet's chest constricted, Ṣāliḥ told to watch and be patient. The verse is not diminishing Ibrāhīm. It is describing a man who has been given incomplete information and is responding to what he can see.

**"Do Not Be Afraid"**

The reassurance is immediate and it is the guests' first direct statement about themselves. The commentators note that the reply was prompted by a question Ibrāhīm asked, recorded in the parallel accounts: *"What is your mission, O messengers?"* (51:31; 15:57). The sequence is therefore: fear, question, reassurance, disclosure. The sūrah compresses the question and reports the answer, which keeps the movement fast. The reassurance is not a general comfort. It is a specific correction of a specific misapprehension, and it is followed at once by the information that makes the correction meaningful. The verse does not leave the fear unresolved for long, and the speed of the resolution is part of the narrative's economy.

**"Sent Against the People of Lūṭ"**

The disclosure is blunt. The commentators gloss it plainly: they have come to destroy them. The verse does not soften the phrase, does not explain the reason, and does not describe what will happen. It names the target and stops. The naming is what produces the rest of the narrative, because Lūṭ's town is close enough to Ibrāhīm's concern that he will plead for it. The sūrah's parallel accounts give the same disclosure in almost identical words: *"We have actually been sent to a wicked people"* (51:32; 15:58). The consistency across three sūrahs marks it as a fixed element of the story, and its fixedness suggests that the disclosure itself — not the destruction — is what the narrative is about.

**Why the Destination Is Stated at Once**

The guests could have identified themselves without naming their target. They did not, and the immediate naming does narrative work. It converts Ibrāhīm from a frightened host into an intercessor in a single sentence, because he now knows both that his guests are harmless to him and that they are carrying destruction to a town he cares about. The verse is structured to produce that conversion. The fear is removed and the concern is created in the same breath, and the following verses will show the concern operating. The sūrah's method here is the same one it used with Nūḥ, where information was given to a prophet specifically so that he would act on it. Information in this sūrah is never merely informational.

**The Verse as the Turn of the Narrative**

Structurally this is the hinge of the Ibrāhīm section. Before it, the scene is domestic and its tension is social. After it, the scene is judicial and its tension is between a decree and a plea. The turn happens in one sentence, and the sūrah gives it no elaboration. The effect is to make the reader experience what Ibrāhīm experienced: a rapid movement from personal alarm to public concern, with no time to settle in either state. The verse therefore does double duty. It resolves the hospitality scene and opens the intercession, and it does both without pausing between them. What follows is the good news, which arrives inside the same encounter.

****What the Verse Does Not Explain****

The verse never tells the reader why the guests did not eat. The commentators supply the reason — they were angels, and angels do not consume physical nourishment — but the sūrah itself leaves it unstated, and the omission is deliberate. Ibrāhīm does not learn the explanation from a narrator; he learns it from what they say next. The verse therefore keeps the reader inside the scene rather than above it. We discover what the host discovers, when he discovers it, and in the same order. This is the sūrah's narrative method at its most controlled: information is withheld until the moment it functions inside the story, and the reader's uncertainty is the same uncertainty the prophet is feeling. The effect is that the disclosure lands with the weight it had for him.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:71

> And his wife was standing by, so she laughed, then We gave her good news of ˹the birth of˺ Isaac, and, after him, Jacob

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Wife Standing, Not Sitting**

The verse notes a posture, and the note is deliberate. Ibrāhīm's wife was standing. The detail places her inside the scene rather than at its edge — present, upright, attending to what is happening rather than withdrawn from it. The parallel account in al-Dhāriyāt describes her differently, coming forward with a cry and clasping her forehead (51:29), which suggests movement across the two tellings. This sūrah's version has her stationary and watching, and the stillness makes the laughter more striking. A person who is standing in a room where strangers have refused to eat, and who then laughs, has drawn a conclusion. The verse does not state the conclusion; it records the sound that came from it.

**The Laughter, and What It Meant**

The commentators offer two readings of the laughter, and both are worth keeping. The first is that she laughed from relief — the guests were not a threat to her husband, and the tension the previous verse described dissolved. The second is that she laughed at the news of the coming destruction of Lūṭ's people. The first reading fits the sequence better, since the good news of a son is given after the laughter in this verse rather than before it. The second has support in the parallel accounts, where the astonishment follows the announcement. What both readings share is that the laughter is a release. Something she had been holding — fear, or expectation, or both — let go, and the verse marks the moment with a single verb.

**Two Names Given in One Verse**

The good news is specific and it names two people: Isḥāq, and after him Yaʿqūb. The naming distinguishes this account from the parallel ones, where the son is described as knowledgeable (51:28; 15:53) without being named in the same breath. The sūrah's choice to give both names does specific work. It is not announcing a birth; it is announcing a line. A son is one generation; a son and a grandson are a beginning. The verse therefore transforms a domestic announcement into a statement about history, and it does so with two proper nouns. The reader is told, in the middle of a scene about frightened hospitality, that two of the prophets in this chapter's lineage are about to exist.

**"And After Him, Yaʿqūb"**

The addition of the grandson is the most remarkable feature of the verse. A couple being told they will have a child in old age is already an extraordinary announcement. A couple being told they will live to see that child's child is a different order of promise, because it guarantees not only the birth but the survival of the parents, the son's maturity, and his own offspring. The verse is therefore not merely reversing barrenness. It is projecting a family forward across three generations in a single sentence. The sūrah's interest in lineage is consistent with its larger purpose: it is narrating a chain of messengers, and this verse is the point at which one link guarantees the next.

**The Structure of the Promise**

The verse's grammar is worth noticing. The laughter comes first, then the good news. In the parallel accounts the order is different, with the announcement preceding the astonishment. This sūrah's arrangement produces a specific effect: the woman's reaction is recorded before its cause is stated, so the reader meets the laughter as an unexplained fact and then learns what produced it. The method is characteristic of the sūrah's narrative style, which regularly presents an action and then supplies its ground. The effect on the reader is to make him reconstruct the scene rather than receive it. A person who has to work out why someone laughed attends more closely than one who is told.

**Why This Verse Follows the Fear**

The placement of the good news is the final thing to notice. It comes immediately after the disclosure that these guests carry destruction to Lūṭ's town, and the two announcements share a single encounter. The sūrah is not separating the joyful material from the grim material. It delivers them together, in the order the encounter produced them, and the juxtaposition is what makes the following verses possible. Ibrāhīm receives a promise about his own family and a report about a town's end in the same conversation, and the next verses will show him acting on the second. A reader who separates the two announcements loses the tension the narrative depends on: a man holding new hope for his house while pleading for someone else's.

****Sārah's Position in the Story****

The verse is one of the few places in this sūrah where a woman is present inside the scene rather than mentioned from outside it. She is standing, she laughs, she speaks in the next verse, and she is answered. The sūrah records her reaction in her own words and treats the words as worth preserving. The detail matters for the narrative's larger argument about households. The blessing pronounced two verses later is addressed to *the people of this house*, and this verse shows the house containing more than the prophet. The promise of a son and a grandson is given to a couple, and the wife's astonishment is recorded at the same length as the husband's would have been. The sūrah's account of Ibrāhīm's family is therefore an account of a household, not of a man.

****The Verse and the Sūrah's Concern With Lineage****

The sūrah has been tracking descent since its forty-eighth verse, where the blessing on the Ark's passengers was said to extend to communities arising from them, and where the destroyed peoples to follow were identified as their descendants. This verse is the fulfilment of that promise in its positive form. A house is being established from which prophets will come, and the naming of Isḥāq and Yaʿqūb marks two links in the chain. The reader who has followed the section can now see both branches of the earlier statement: communities blessed and communities punished, all descending from the same survivors. The verse is the point at which the blessed branch becomes visible in the narrative.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:72

> She wondered, “Oh, my! How can I have a child in this old age, and my husband here is an old man? This is truly an astonishing thing!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Exclamation, Not an Argument**

The verse records a woman's first response to an impossible announcement, and the form of the response is informative. It opens with an exclamation — *oh, my* — which is not a word of protest but a sound of overwhelm. The commentators note the traditional identifications: this is Sārah, and the accounts give her age at the time as ninety-nine, with Ibrāhīm at a hundred and twenty. The verse does not state the ages; it states the reaction, and the reaction carries the ages inside it. The sūrah's method here is consistent with its treatment of the whole encounter: it reports what people said and did, and leaves the reader to reconstruct the circumstances. What is recorded is not disbelief but astonishment, and the distinction is what the next verse will confirm.

**Two Ages Named**

The exclamation names two facts, and their order is significant. *How can I have a child in this old age, and my husband here is an old man?* She begins with herself and then adds her husband. The sequence is not self-centred; it is the sequence of the physical facts as they bear on the case. Her own age is the first obstacle, and her husband's is the second, and both are stated without exaggeration or complaint. The verse is recording a woman taking the measure of an announcement against what she knows, and the measure is accurate. Nothing in her statement is false. The sūrah preserves it in full precisely because it is true, and because a true statement of impossibility is what makes the promise worth stating.

**"This Husband of Mine"**

The phrase used for her husband is intimate rather than formal, and the commentators note its tone. She does not say *Ibrāhīm* or *the prophet*. She says *this old man, my husband*, in the register of a person describing someone she has lived with for decades. The detail matters because it locates the announcement in a marriage rather than in a prophecy. The sūrah has been narrating a domestic scene since the guests arrived, and this phrase keeps it there. A reader who treats the account as a theological statement about divine power is missing half of what the verse records, which is a long-married couple being told something that contradicts everything they know about themselves.

**"An Astonishing Thing"**

The final clause names the category the announcement falls into: something astounding, strange, unheard of. The word is not a term of rejection. It is the word a person uses when an event exceeds the range of what he has experienced. The sūrah uses the same vocabulary elsewhere for the response to extraordinary news, and its use here is consistent: the woman is not denying that God could do this. She is saying that it has no precedent in her life. The distinction is what the angels' reply in the next verse addresses, and the reply treats her words as wonder rather than as refusal. A reader who hears defiance in this verse has misread its register.

**Wonder That Is Not Doubt**

The sūrah is careful to distinguish between two responses that sound similar. Doubt questions whether a thing can happen. Wonder acknowledges that it cannot happen by any known route and waits to see. The woman's words belong to the second category, and the evidence is in what follows: she receives the good news, she conceives, and the line continues. The sūrah records the wonder at length because it is the honest response of a person told something beyond his experience, and because suppressing it would make the promise less remarkable. A reader who expects prophets and their families to receive impossible news without reaction has misunderstood what the sūrah is showing. The reaction is part of the evidence that the news was impossible.

**The Verse and Other Such Wonders**

The Qurʾān records the same response in a later prophet's case, and the parallel is instructive. Zakariyyā asks how he can have a son when his wife is barren and he has reached old age, and the answer he receives is direct: *"So will it be! Your Lord says, 'It is easy for Me, just as I created you before, when you were nothing!'"* (19:9). The two cases share a structure: an elderly man, a barren or aged wife, an astonished question, and an answer that locates the possibility in creation rather than in biology. The sūrah of Hūd gives the answer in a different form, and the difference is worth noting in the verse that follows. Here the reply is not an explanation of mechanism but a question about the hearer's premise.

****What the Verse Assumes About Marriage****

The woman's exclamation treats her husband's age as relevant to her own capacity, and the assumption behind it is worth naming. She does not say *I am too old*. She says *I am old and he is old*, as though both facts bear on a single matter. The verse is recording a married couple as a unit, and the unit is what the promise addresses. The sūrah's concern with households runs through the whole Ibrāhīm section: the blessing is on the house, the son and grandson are named together, and the intercession that follows is made by a man whose own family has just been secured. The verse is therefore not only about an impossible birth. It is about a marriage being given a future it had stopped expecting.

****The Honesty of the Record****

The sūrah preserves this exclamation in full, including its bluntness. A less candid account would have shortened it, or attributed the wonder to someone else, or omitted it altogether. The verse keeps every clause: the cry, the two ages, the judgement that this is astounding. The preservation is a mark of the sūrah's method throughout, which is to record what people actually said rather than what would have been more edifying. Nūḥ's question about his son is preserved; the notables' mockery is preserved; the son's refusal is preserved in his own words. The verse belongs to that pattern, and the pattern is what makes the narratives usable. A reader can only recognise himself in an account that does not tidy up its characters.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:73

> They responded, “Are you astonished by Allah’s decree? May Allah’s mercy and blessings be upon you, O people of this house. Indeed, He is Praiseworthy, All-Glorious.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question Answering a Question**

The reply to the woman's wonder is not an explanation. It is a counter-question: are you astonished at the decree of God? The form is significant. An explanation would have described a mechanism — how a barren woman conceives, how an old man fathers. The question instead addresses the premise of the astonishment. The commentators note the parallel with God's answer to Zakariyyā's prayer (19:9), and the two replies differ in method: that one explains by pointing to creation, this one corrects by pointing to the source. The sūrah chooses the second form because the woman's wonder was not about biology. It was about whether such a thing could be decreed at all, and the counter-question answers exactly that.

**"The Decree of Allah"**

The phrase is the verse's theological centre. What has been announced is not a favour arranged informally but a decree — *amr Allāh*, the command or affair of God. The word carries the sense of something settled and issued, not something hoped for. By naming it, the reply relocates the announcement from the category of the improbable to the category of the decided. The sūrah has used the same vocabulary throughout for the decisions that end its narratives: the command that came, the decree carried out, the word that had already gone forth. The verse applies that vocabulary to a birth, which tells the reader that a promise of life and a decree of destruction belong to the same order of thing. Both are settled; both arrive.

**A Blessing Spoken Over a House**

The reply then shifts register entirely and pronounces a blessing: may God's mercy and blessings be upon you. The commentators identify the two terms as covering both spiritual mercy and material blessing, and the combination is characteristic of the Qurʾān's language of divine favour. The shift from a corrective question to a benediction is worth noticing. The woman's wonder is not rebuked and then left hanging; it is answered and then covered with a blessing. The sūrah's treatment of the prophets' families is consistently of this kind — honest about their reactions and generous in what follows. The verse is therefore not a reproof. It is a correction wrapped in a prayer, delivered by guests who have come with both news and destruction in the same errand.

**"O People of This House"**

The address is to a household, not to an individual. The commentators note that the phrase became a designation of special honour, and its use here identifies the recipients as a family unit under a single blessing. The choice matters for the narrative's larger purpose. The sūrah has been concerned throughout with what a family line carries, and this verse names the line explicitly. The promise given in the previous verse was of a son and a grandson; the blessing here is pronounced over the house that will contain them. The verse is therefore stating that the promise is not a private gift to two elderly people. It is the establishment of a household that will produce prophets, and the sūrah's own narrative section depends on that fact.

**Praiseworthy, All-Glorious**

The verse closes on two divine names, and the pairing answers the woman's astonishment in a third way. *Ḥamīd* — praised, worthy of praise, the one whose acts merit gratitude. *Majīd* — glorious, noble, abundant in honour and generosity. The first name addresses the response owed to the decree: it is to be praised rather than marvelled at. The second addresses its character: what God does is generous and abundant, not grudging or minimal. Together the names tell the hearer how to receive an impossible announcement. The sūrah uses the same device at the end of its other narratives, closing an account with the names that summarise its meaning. Here the names are spoken by guests, which makes them part of the dialogue rather than a narrator's comment.

**The Angels' Answer as a Rule**

Read apart from the story, the reply is a general statement about how to receive divine action. Astonishment at what God decrees is treated as a category error, not as a sin. The woman is not condemned for her wonder; she is asked whether the wonder is directed at the right object. The rule has application beyond this scene. A person who receives an answer to prayer, or an unexpected mercy, or a reversal he cannot explain, faces the same question: is the surprise about the event, or about the one who decreed it? The sūrah's own opening made the same point in a different register, describing a person who despairs when mercy is withdrawn and boasts when it returns. Both failures come from treating the event as the primary fact. The verse names the alternative in two words.

****Mercy and Blessings Named Together****

The two terms in the benediction cover different ground, and the pairing is standard in the Qurʾān's language of favour. Mercy concerns a person's condition before God — forgiveness, acceptance, nearness. Blessings concern what is given — increase, provision, continuity. The verse pronounces both, and the combination answers the woman's astonishment at two levels. Her wonder was about the physical impossibility; the blessing addresses it with increase. Her wonder may also have carried a question about standing — whether such a thing could be decreed for them; the mercy addresses that. The sūrah uses the same pairing elsewhere, and its recurrence marks it as the complete form of divine favour. Neither half alone is the full gift.

****The Verse as the Sūrah's Answer to Despair****

Placed against the sūrah's ninth and tenth verses, this reply answers a problem the chapter raised early. Those verses described a person who despairs when mercy is withdrawn and boasts when it returns, and the diagnosis was that his estimate of himself moves with his circumstances. The woman in this verse faces the opposite situation: an unexpected mercy arriving after decades of its absence. Her wonder is not despair, but it is a form of measuring the announcement against what she has experienced. The reply corrects the measurement without rebuking her. The verse therefore supplies the alternative to the failure described at the chapter's opening — a way of receiving an impossible gift that neither despairs nor swells.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:74

> Then after the fear had left Abraham, and the good news had reached him, he began to plead with Us for the people of Lot

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two States Named Before the Plea**

The verse describes Ibrāhīm's condition at the moment he begins to intercede, and it names two things. The fear had gone, and the good news had arrived. Both are recorded, and both are conditions of what follows. A man still afraid cannot plead for anyone; his attention is on his own safety. A man who has not received good news has nothing standing behind his confidence. The verse is therefore not simply narrating a sequence. It is describing the state a person must be in to intercede effectively — settled and assured — and it shows that state being reached before the plea begins. The sūrah's method throughout is to give the ground of an action before the action, and here the ground is emotional as well as factual.

**The Sequence Is the Point**

The order of the three elements is worth holding: reassurance, good news, intercession. The sūrah has arranged the encounter so that Ibrāhīm's own house is secured before he turns his attention to someone else's town. The arrangement is not incidental. A person who pleads for others while his own situation is unresolved is not interceding; he is distracting himself. The verse shows the proper order, and the order teaches something about the relation between a person's own standing and his concern for others. The sūrah has made a related point in its doctrinal section, where the exception was defined by patience and good action rather than by circumstance. Here the same principle appears as narrative sequence: settle your own case, then carry someone else's.

**He Pleaded for a Town That Was Not His**

The object of the plea is significant. Lūṭ's people were not Ibrāhīm's people. They were not his tribe, his town, or his mission; the commentators note that the plea consisted of speaking to the angels and trying to dissuade them from bringing destruction on them. The parallel account records the substance of the objection: *"But Lot is there!"* — to which the answer came that God knows best who is there and will save him and his family (29:32). The verse is therefore recording an intercession on behalf of strangers, made by a man who had just been told that the destruction was already decided. The sūrah presents this as characteristic of Ibrāhīm rather than as an anomaly, and the next verse will explain why.

**What the Plea Consisted Of**

The verse does not quote the plea, and the omission is deliberate. The parallel accounts give its content: Lūṭ is in the town; the destruction will take him with the rest. The sūrah of Hūd compresses the exchange into a single statement of the act — he pleaded with Us — and moves to the answer. The compression keeps the reader's attention on the fact of the intercession rather than on its arguments. This matters because the outcome is not determined by the strength of the argument. The plea is described, the answer refuses it, and the refusal is not presented as a rebuttal of Ibrāhīm's reasoning. The verse is therefore about the limits of intercession rather than about the quality of a plea.

**Why the Verse Places the Plea Here**

The placement is the most instructive feature of the verse. The plea comes after the good news, which means Ibrāhīm makes it as a man who has just received an extraordinary mercy for his own household. The sūrah is showing the effect of mercy on a person's concern for others. A man who has been given a son in old age does not become narrower; he becomes more willing to plead for a town full of strangers. The verse therefore answers a question the sūrah has raised repeatedly: what does receiving blessing do to a person? The answer given in its doctrinal section was that most people become arrogant. The answer given here is different, and the difference is the character described in the next verse.

**A Prophet Arguing With a Decree**

The verse records something unusual in the Qurʾān's narratives: a prophet contesting a divine decision. The sūrah does not present this as presumption, and the next verse explains why it is not. Ibrāhīm's character makes the plea intelligible — a man of forbearance and tenderness cannot hear that a town will be destroyed and say nothing. The verse therefore records the plea as an expression of character rather than as a challenge to authority, and the answer that follows corrects the matter without condemning the man. The sūrah has shown a similar exchange in the Nūḥ narrative, where a prophet questioned a promise about his son and was corrected. Both cases preserve the prophet's integrity while establishing the limit.

****What Intercession Is and Is Not****

The verse describes an act the sūrah treats with care. Ibrāhīm pleads, and the pleading is recorded without criticism; the next verse will explain why the character behind it is praiseworthy. But the plea is also refused, and the refusal is final. The two facts together define intercession in this sūrah: it is legitimate, it is characteristic of the merciful, and it does not override a decree that has been issued. The sūrah has established the same limits twice before — Nūḥ told not to plead for the wrongdoers, Nūḥ corrected for asking about his son. The verse is the third instance, and by the third time the reader has a rule rather than a series of incidents. A prophet may plead; a prophet may not reopen a closed matter.

****The Plea and the Sūrah's Mekkan Audience****

The original listeners had a specific interest in this scene. They were being warned of destruction by a man who grieved over them, and the sūrah has recorded that grief more than once. Here they are shown the same grief in an earlier prophet, directed at a town that had no claim on him at all. The verse is therefore answering an unspoken question: does the messenger actually care what happens to us? The narrative's answer is that he cares enough to plead after the pleading has become useless, and that the care does not change the outcome. Both halves of the answer matter. A messenger who does not care is not worth listening to; a messenger whose care can suspend a decree is not telling the truth about the decree.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:75

> Truly, Abraham was forbearing, tender-hearted, and ever turning ˹to his Lord˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Qualities, One Character**

The verse interrupts the narrative to describe a man. It is one line long and it lists three qualities, and the interruption is the sūrah's way of explaining the plea that came before it. A reader might reasonably ask why a prophet would argue with a decree that had already been issued. The verse answers: because of who he is. The three qualities are not a general eulogy. They are the specific traits that produced the specific act, and each one contributes to it. The sūrah's method throughout is to trace behaviour to disposition, and here it does so explicitly, in a single sentence, at the exact point in the narrative where the explanation is needed.

**Ḥalīm — Forbearance**

The first quality is *ḥalīm*, usually rendered forbearing or clement, and the word denotes a person who does not react quickly to provocation. It is the quality of a man who can absorb an offence without answering it in kind. The sūrah has used the same vocabulary elsewhere, and its theological weight is considerable: the name *al-Ḥalīm* is applied to God Himself. Applied to Ibrāhīm, it explains a specific feature of the plea. A man who is quick to anger would have demanded the town's destruction when its people threatened his nephew. A forbearing man keeps looking for a reason it might be spared. The quality is therefore not a soft virtue in this context. It is what makes intercession possible at all, since intercession requires a person to keep arguing for people who have given him nothing.

**Awwāh — Tender-Hearted**

The second quality is harder to render and the commentators give it a range: one who sighs or groans out of compassion, one who is tender-hearted, one who prays much. The root carries the sense of a sound made under emotional pressure. The word describes a man whose feelings are audible, who does not process suffering silently. The sūrah's narratives have shown this quality operating throughout the chapter: Nūḥ grieving over his son, this Prophet's chest constricted by what his hearers said. The verse identifies it as a prophetic trait rather than a weakness. A messenger who felt nothing for the people he warned would have no reason to keep warning them after the warnings failed, and the sūrah's whole account of mission depends on the feeling being real.

**Munīb — Ever Turning**

The third quality completes the set. *Munīb* is the active participle of returning, turning back, and it describes a habitual movement rather than a single act. The sūrah has used the same root throughout for repentance — seek forgiveness, then turn — and here it names the disposition behind the act. A person who turns once has repented; a person who is *ever turning* has made return the shape of his life. The quality explains why the previous verse's plea does not become presumption. A man who is constantly returning to God does not argue with Him from a position of independence. He argues from inside a relation, and the relation is what keeps the argument within limits. The next verse will show the limit being stated, and Ibrāhīm will accept it.

**Why the Verse Interrupts the Narrative**

The placement is worth isolating. The narrative runs: the guests arrive, the food is refused, the fear is resolved, the good news is given, the woman wonders, the angels answer, Ibrāhīm pleads — and then the story stops for a description of the man. The interruption is the sūrah's answer to a question the plea raises. Without it, Ibrāhīm's intercession looks like a challenge to a settled decree, and the reader is left to judge whether the challenge was appropriate. With it, the plea is explained as the natural output of a specific character, and the answer that follows corrects a mistaken premise rather than a wrong attitude. The verse is therefore doing interpretive work, and the sūrah places it exactly where the interpretation is needed.

**The Same Description Elsewhere**

The Qurʾān describes Ibrāhīm in similar terms in another context, and the comparison sharpens the meaning here. In al-Tawbah his prayer for his father's forgiveness is discussed, and the verse closes: *"Abraham was truly tender-hearted, forbearing"* (9:114). The two descriptions share the same core qualities and attach them to two different acts — pleading for a father in one case, pleading for a town in the other. The consistency establishes that these are not situational reactions but settled traits. The sūrah of Hūd adds the third quality, the ever-turning, which the other verse does not name. The addition is what allows the reader to see how the tenderness stays within bounds: the man who pleads is also the man who keeps returning.

****The Three Qualities in Order****

The sequence of the three descriptions is not arbitrary. Forbearance is the capacity not to react; tenderness is the capacity to feel; turning is the capacity to return. Read in that order, they describe a person who can absorb provocation without hardening, feel suffering without being destroyed by it, and keep his relation with God intact through both. Each quality corrects an excess of the one before it. Forbearance without tenderness becomes indifference. Tenderness without turning becomes overwhelm. The third quality is what holds the other two in place, and it is the one the next verse depends on: a man who is ever turning will accept a correction when it comes, which is exactly what happens.

****Why the Sūrah Needs This Verse****

Without this line, the narrative would present a prophet arguing with angels and being told to stop, and the reader would be left to judge the argument. The verse removes the judgement by supplying the character. It also does something larger: it gives the sūrah a portrait of the disposition it has been recommending since its opening. The chapter's exception was defined by patience and good action; its warning was against despair and boastfulness; its command at the end will be to be steadfast and to trust. The three qualities named here are those requirements in the form of a person. The sūrah has been describing a disposition in the abstract for seventy-four verses, and here it shows one.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:76

> ˹The angels said,˺ “O Abraham! Plead no more! Your Lord’s decree has already come, and they will certainly be afflicted with a punishment that cannot be averted!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Command to Stop**

The verse is an instruction, and its form is direct. The commentators take the words as spoken by the angels, and the same expression appears elsewhere in the Qurʾān in a different context: *"O Joseph! Forget about this"* (12:29). The phrase tells a person to drop a matter, and its use here is unambiguous. Ibrāhīm is not being answered on the merits of his plea. He is being told that the pleading has reached its end. The sūrah records the instruction without softening it, and the bluntness is part of the teaching. There is a point beyond which intercession does not merely fail but becomes inappropriate, and the verse marks that point with a command rather than with an explanation.

**"Turn Away From This"**

The wording of the instruction is worth holding. It does not say *your plea is rejected* or *the town deserves this*. It says *turn away from this* — leave the matter, set it down. The commentators gloss the command as a direction to stop the intercession because the decree has been passed. The phrase identifies what the intercessor is to do, not what God has decided. That distinction matters. Ibrāhīm is not being told that his concern was misplaced or that the people were unworthy of it. He is being told that the matter is no longer open. The verse therefore separates two things that are easily fused: the legitimacy of the feeling and the availability of the outcome. The first is affirmed by the previous verse; the second is closed by this one.

**"Your Lord's Decree Has Come"**

The ground given for the instruction is a statement of fact. The commentators gloss it plainly: God's decree and judgement have been passed concerning Lūṭ's people, and therefore they cannot be stopped by supplication or any other means. The word *amr* is the same one used in the previous verse for the decree of a birth, and the shared vocabulary is significant. What produces a son in old age and what destroys a town belong to the same category of settled divine action. The sūrah has been building this identification throughout its narratives, where every destruction is described as a command that came. The verse applies the same term to a decree that has already arrived, and the arrival is what closes the plea.

**"A Punishment That Cannot Be Averted"**

The final clause states the consequence in absolute terms. The punishment is not delayed, not reduced, not conditionable. The word used for averting is the one the sūrah used in its doctrinal section, where the punishment that overtakes a people was said to be incapable of being turned away. The verse therefore links this narrative back to the general rule the sūrah established at its beginning. The reader is being shown the rule operating in a specific case, with a specific intercessor, at a specific moment. The consistency between the rule and the case is what makes the narrative an argument rather than a story: what the sūrah claimed in the abstract is here demonstrated in an instance where the strongest possible plea was made.

**The Limit on a Prophet's Intercession**

The verse establishes a boundary that the sūrah has approached twice before. Nūḥ was told not to plead for those who had done wrong, and he was corrected when he asked about his son. Ibrāhīm is told to stop pleading for Lūṭ's people. In both cases the messenger's concern is treated as legitimate and the plea is nonetheless refused. The sūrah is teaching something specific about the prophetic office: a messenger is authorised to warn, to advise and to hope, but he is not authorised to negotiate with a decree once the decree has been issued. The verse states the limit without elaboration, and the absence of elaboration is itself instructive. There is no argument to answer, because the matter is not in the realm of argument.

**What the Verse Teaches About Decrees**

Read as a statement about divine action, the verse makes two claims at once. The first is that decrees have a point of finality, and that the finality is real rather than nominal. The second is that the finality is knowable — the angels know it, and they say so. The combination answers a common misreading of intercession, in which prayer is treated as a means of altering settled outcomes. The sūrah does not deny the power of prayer; its opening commands it and its sixth verse makes provision depend on God's giving. What it denies is that prayer operates on decisions already issued. The distinction is precise, and the verse delivers it in the hardest available case: the greatest of the prophets, pleading for a town containing his own nephew, told to stop.

****The Verse and the Sūrah's Other Closed Doors****

This is the third closed door in the sūrah's narratives, and comparing the three shows a consistent design. Nūḥ was told not to plead for the wrongdoers before the flood. He was corrected for asking about his son after it. Ibrāhīm is told to stop pleading for Lūṭ's town. In each case the door closes at a specific point, and the point is always the same: when the decree has been issued. The sūrah is not teaching that prayer is useless. It is teaching that prayer operates before a decision, not after it, and that knowing which side of the line one is on is part of the messenger's knowledge. The three instances together give the reader a rule with worked examples.

****What the Reader Is Left With****

The verse ends the Ibrāhīm section without describing the destruction. The punishment is announced as certain and unavertable, and the narrative moves straight to Lūṭ's house. The omission is characteristic of the sūrah's endings: the event is stated rather than narrated, and the reader's attention is kept on the human exchange that preceded it. What remains from this section is not a picture of falling stones. It is a sequence: guests received, food refused, fear resolved, news given, wonder expressed, a blessing pronounced, a plea made, a plea refused. Every step is a person acting, and the destruction is the last item in a list of decisions made by people who are not described at all. The sūrah's method is complete in that distribution.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:77

> When Our messenger-angels came to Lot, he was distressed and worried by their arrival. He said, “This is a terrible day.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**From One House to Another**

The verse picks up the journey the previous section left unfinished. The guests who had eaten nothing in Ibrāhīm's house now arrive at Lūṭ's, and the commentators note that the two settlements were near each other. The transition is immediate and it carries an unwelcome contrast: the same visitors who brought good news to one prophet bring danger to the other. The sūrah does not pause to explain the connection. It moves straight from the closed door of intercession to a new house, and the reader is left to notice that the guests who could not be diverted from their errand have now reached their destination. The verse is the arrival, and everything that follows happens inside a single evening and night.

**Handsome Youths, and a Problem**

The commentators record that the visitors came in the form of handsome young men, and that Lūṭ did not know they were angels. The detail explains the distress in this verse. The problem is not that strangers have arrived; a prophet would welcome strangers. The problem is that these particular strangers, in this particular town, are in immediate danger. The sūrah has already described the men of Lūṭ's people as aggressive in pursuing their desires, and the parallel account records them coming to the house rejoicing (15:67). The verse is therefore recording a host who recognises the danger before anyone has done anything, and the recognition is what makes the evening terrible rather than merely difficult.

**Distressed on Their Account**

The first of Lūṭ's two states is grief for them, and the preposition matters. He is not distressed in general or for himself; he is distressed *on their account*. The commentators gloss it directly: he feared what his people would attempt. The verse is therefore describing a man whose primary feeling is concern for guests he has only just received. The sūrah has shown the same disposition in Ibrāhīm, who hurried to feed strangers, and it treats the concern as the mark of a prophet rather than as mere politeness. The distress is not anxiety about reputation. It is the specific horror of a host who knows his town and knows what it does to visitors, and who has just taken responsibility for three of them.

**Powerless to Protect Them**

The second state is the recognition of his own inability. The commentators gloss it as the fear that his people had come for his guests and that he would be unable to defend them. The verse places the two states together deliberately: concern and incapacity. A man who cares but cannot act is in a specific kind of suffering, and the sūrah names it in two short phrases. The combination is what produces the exclamation at the end of the verse. The reader is being shown the exact point at which a person's moral clarity meets his physical limitation, and the sūrah does not resolve the tension at this stage. It records the tension and lets the next verse show what Lūṭ does inside it.

**"This Is a Difficult Day"**

The exclamation is short and unguarded. The commentators take the day referred to as the day of the guests' arrival, and the word used carries the sense of something hard to bear. The verse preserves the sentence exactly as it would have been spoken — not a prayer, not a statement of faith, but a man saying aloud that the day is hard. The sūrah's willingness to record this is consistent with its treatment of the prophets throughout: Nūḥ questioning God about his son, Ibrāhīm afraid of his guests, this Prophet's chest constricted. The exclamation is not a failure of trust. It is the sound a person makes when he understands his situation completely and has no way out of it yet. The verse ends there, and the next verse brings the people to the door.

**The Verse and the Burden of Hospitality**

The verse is also about an obligation. Lūṭ has received guests, and the obligation to protect them has arrived with them. The sūrah has shown the same obligation operating in Ibrāhīm's house, where it took the form of speed and generosity. Here it takes the form of dread, because the danger is different. The verse therefore connects the two houses through a shared duty: a host answers for his guests. The sūrah's method is to show a single moral requirement operating in two contrasting circumstances, and the contrast teaches more than either case alone would. The same duty that made one evening an occasion for a fattened calf makes this evening the hardest day of a prophet's life.

****The Verse and the Sūrah's Pattern of Arrival****

Each narrative in this section opens with an arrival, and the arrivals differ in what they bring. The messenger arrives among ʿĀd with a warning; the angels arrive among Thamūd's destroyers unseen; the messengers arrive at Ibrāhīm's house with news. Here they arrive at a house where their presence is itself the danger. The sūrah is using the same structural device to make a point about context. A messenger's arrival means one thing in a town that ignores him and another in a town that acts on what it sees. Lūṭ's distress is not about the guests; it is about the town they have walked into. The verse therefore opens the narrative by locating the problem outside the house, and the whole of the following account takes place at that boundary. The arrival is therefore the beginning of the trial rather than its middle, and the reader is placed at the door with the host, knowing what the town is and not yet knowing what the guests are.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:78

> And ˹the men of˺ his people—who were used to shameful deeds—came to him rushing. He pleaded, “O my people! Here are my daughters ˹for marriage˺—they are pure for you. So fear Allah, and do not humiliate me by disrespecting my guests. Is there not ˹even˺ a single right-minded man among you?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A People Coming in Haste**

The verse describes the arrival of the people with a word that carries speed and compulsion, and the parallel account adds that they came rejoicing (15:67). The sūrah's phrase suggests men driven by something rather than walking toward something, and the commentators connect it to the aggression already noted in the previous verses. The haste is the first evidence in the verse, and it is evidence about them rather than about Lūṭ. A group that arrives at a prophet's house in a rush, at night, having heard that strangers are inside, is not arriving to discuss anything. The verse records the movement before it records the speech, and the movement establishes what the speech will fail to change.

**"They Had Long Been Committing Evil Deeds"**

The clause is placed before the dialogue, and its function is to inform the reader rather than the characters. Lūṭ already knows what his people do; the reader is being told so that what follows can be understood. The word rendered here as evil deeds is the same one used in the previous verse for the wrongdoers generally, and its application here is specific rather than general. The verse is therefore establishing that the demand about to be made is not an aberration or a single drunken impulse. It is the settled practice of the town. The sūrah elsewhere describes the same behaviour explicitly: *"Do you commit a shameful deed that no man has ever done before? You lust after men instead of women!"* (7:80–81). The clause here assumes that background without restating it.

**The Offer, and What It Meant**

Lūṭ's response is the verse's central difficulty, and the commentators divide over it. The plain sense of *my daughters* is his own children, and many read it that way. Others hold that the daughters referred to are the women of his people generally, on the reasoning that a prophet is a father to his community. What both readings share is the purpose: the offer is an attempt to redirect the men from his male guests toward lawful marriage. The verse is not recording a transaction in which women are exchanged. It is recording a desperate host offering the only lawful alternative available to him, in the hope that offering something will displace the demand for something else. The sūrah preserves the offer in his own words, and the words carry the strain of the moment.

**"They Are Purer for You"**

The comparative is the phrase that gives the offer its moral weight. Lūṭ does not say the daughters are permissible or available; he says they are purer. The commentators connect the word to the taunt his people used against him and his followers: *"They are a people who wish to remain chaste!"* (7:82; 27:56). The same vocabulary of purity is therefore in play on both sides — his people use it as an accusation, and he uses it as an argument. The verse shows a man appealing to a standard his hearers have already rejected by name. The appeal is not naive. It is the last available ground, and the sūrah records it because the refusal of that ground is what the following verses describe.

**"Do Not Humiliate Me With My Guests"**

The plea names the specific harm Lūṭ fears, and it names it in terms of honour rather than of law. He does not say *do not commit a sin* at this point, though the command to fear God precedes it. He says *do not disgrace me in respect of my guests*. The verse is therefore recording a host's understanding of what his obligation costs him. A guest harmed in a man's house is a humiliation to the host, and Lūṭ says so plainly. The sūrah has shown the same obligation from the other side in Ibrāhīm's house, where hospitality was speed and generosity. Here it is a man standing between a mob and three strangers, and the verse makes the personal cost of the position explicit rather than leaving it implied.

**"Is There No Right-Minded Man Among You?"**

The final question is the verse's most searching line. Lūṭ is not asking for a favour; he is asking whether a single person of sound judgement remains in the town. The question assumes what the previous clause stated — that the practice is general — and asks whether the generality is total. The sūrah has already answered the question elsewhere: Lūṭ's household contained no believing family except one, and the wife was among those who remained behind (29:33). The verse therefore records a question whose answer the reader already knows, and the effect is to make the refusal that follows more complete. A man asks whether anyone among a whole town can think straight, and the answer given in the next verse is not that one man can. It is that none of them want what he is offering.

****What the Verse Records About a Host's Options****

Read as a catalogue of resources, the verse lists what a man in Lūṭ's position actually had. He had his household, his standing as a host, his appeal to God, and his voice. He did not have force, followers or time. The verse records him using all four available resources in a single speech, and the sequence moves from the practical to the moral to the personal to the general. A reader can watch a man exhausting his options in the order they occur to him. The sūrah preserves the full speech rather than summarising it because the exhaustion is the evidence. What follows is not a refusal of a weak argument; it is a refusal of everything a prophet could offer in the space of a few sentences.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:79

> They argued, “You certainly know that we have no need for your daughters. You already know what we desire!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Answer That Refuses the Offer**

The reply is short, and its brevity is the verse's first feature. Lūṭ has made an argument, offered an alternative, appealed to God, invoked the duty of a host, and asked whether any reasonable man remains. The response addresses none of it. It does not argue that the demand is permissible, does not claim a right, does not offer a reason. It simply states that the offer is not what they want. The sūrah records the refusal in its barest form because the bareness is the point: there is no position being defended here, only a desire being asserted. The verse therefore shows the limit of what argument can achieve. A person who is not making a case cannot be answered with a case.

**"You Know We Have No Claim to Your Daughters"**

The commentators gloss the phrase as meaning they have no need of the daughters. The word used carries the sense of a want or a requirement, and its negation is significant. They are not saying the daughters are forbidden to them or that they have no right to marry them. They are saying they do not want them. The verse is therefore recording a refusal at the level of appetite rather than of law. The distinction matters for what follows, because a refusal of appetite cannot be met with a change of terms. Lūṭ offered the lawful route and it was declined not on grounds but on preference. The sūrah preserves the exact form of the refusal so that the reader can see that nothing further was available to say.

**"You Know What We Desire"**

The second clause is the verse's most striking line, and its force lies in the appeal to knowledge. They do not state the desire; they say that he already knows it. The commentators take this as a reference to the practice the previous verse described, and the parallel accounts make the practice explicit. The rhetorical effect is to place Lūṭ inside a shared understanding he did not choose. He is being told that his own knowledge of the town is what makes his offer futile. The sūrah preserves the phrasing because it reveals the character of the demand: it is not hidden, not ashamed, not presented as anything other than what it is. A people who can say this to their prophet without embarrassment have passed the point at which admonition operates.

**The Verse as the Depth of the Case**

Read in sequence, the verse marks a boundary the sūrah has been approaching. The previous verse contained every available appeal: an alternative, a religious command, a social obligation, and a question about reason. This verse disposes of all four in two clauses. The sūrah's narratives regularly reach this point, where a messenger's arguments are exhausted and what remains is the outcome. The difference here is that the exhaustion happens in a single night rather than over years. Nūḥ argued for nine hundred and fifty; Ṣāliḥ argued until the camel was hamstrung. Lūṭ argues for one evening and is answered with a statement of appetite. The verse is therefore the fastest refusal in the sūrah, and its speed is a measure of how far the town has gone.

**Why the Reply Is So Short**

The sūrah gives the reply two clauses where it gave Lūṭ's plea five. The asymmetry is deliberate. A person making a case needs many words; a person stating a desire needs few. The verse's economy therefore enacts the difference between the two speakers. Lūṭ is reasoning, appealing, offering and questioning. His hearers are reporting what they want. The sūrah does not need to characterise them; the length of their sentence does it. A reader who has followed the exchange can hear the imbalance without being told about it. This is the sūrah's method at its most compressed: the form of the dialogue carries the judgement, and no comment from the narrator is required.

**What the Verse Establishes**

Two things are settled by this exchange. The first is that Lūṭ has done everything a prophet can do in the time available. He identified the danger, offered the lawful alternative, invoked God, invoked the duty of hospitality, and asked whether any sane man remained. Nothing further was possible, and the next verse will record him saying so. The second is that the town's refusal is complete and unargued. The sūrah therefore has what it needs for the outcome: a messenger who exhausted his options and a people who declined without giving a reason. The destruction that follows in four verses is not narrated as a reaction to a single act. It is the consequence of a settled condition, and this verse is where the condition is stated in the people's own words.

****The Verse and the Sūrah's View of Appetite****

The sūrah treats uncontrolled desire as a category of failure distinct from disbelief. The people of Lūṭ are not described as holding a rival theology or defending a competing account of God. They are described as wanting something and refusing an alternative. The verse captures this in two clauses, and the capture is precise: the refusal is stated as knowledge of their own desire rather than as a claim about right. The sūrah elsewhere makes the same distinction, contrasting a person who follows his desire with one who fears standing before his Lord. The verse is therefore describing a condition the sūrah regards as diagnosable and dangerous — a person for whom what he wants has become the measure of what he will accept.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:80

> He responded, “If only I had the strength ˹to resist you˺ or could rely on a strong supporter.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Wish, Not a Prayer**

The verse records an exclamation of a particular kind. It is not addressed to God and it is not a petition. It is a wish about himself — *if only I had*. The sūrah preserves the sentence in that form, and the form tells the reader where Lūṭ's attention is. He is not asking for rescue; he is measuring his own capacity and finding it short. The verse therefore records a moment of complete clarity about limitation. The man has argued, offered and appealed, and having exhausted those routes he turns to the one remaining question: what force do I actually have? The answer is none, and the verse is the sound of a person arriving at that answer in the middle of the night.

**"Strength Against You"**

The commentators gloss the first term as an army — a force of men. The word is plural in form and denotes physical capacity to resist. Lūṭ is not wishing for eloquence or for a better argument. He has already used those. He is wishing for the one thing his situation lacks, which is the ability to stand between a mob and his guests by force. The verse is therefore naming the specific deficit precisely. A host with a household of fighting men would not be in this position. A host alone at night with three strangers is, and the verse records him saying so. The sūrah's honesty about the prophets' physical situations is consistent here: it does not supply a miraculous force at the moment of wish. It records the wish and moves to the answer that comes from elsewhere.

**"A Firm Support"**

The second term is glossed as family, or family and friends together — a person or group to lean on. The two wishes are therefore distinct: a force to fight with, and a person to rely on.
