The first is about capacity, the second about backing. Lūṭ names both because both are missing, and the naming describes the condition of a messenger sent alone into a hostile town. The sūrah has shown the same condition throughout its narratives: Nūḥ with only a few believers, Hūd dismissed as one possessed, Ṣāliḥ told that his people had counted on better from him. The verse gives the condition its clearest expression, because Lūṭ is the only one of these prophets who says aloud what the others endured in silence.

**The Verse and the Prophet's Later Words**

The exclamation is remembered in the tradition for a reason. A messenger who is alone, unsupported and outnumbered says what every messenger in a similar position has felt, and the sūrah preserves it as speech rather than as narration. The preservation matters because it distinguishes between two things easily confused: the correctness of a messenger's mission and the adequacy of his resources. Lūṭ's mission was sound and his resources were nil, and he says so without questioning the mission. The verse is therefore not a complaint about being sent. It is an accurate statement about what was sent with, and the sūrah records it because the distinction between the two is one of the things its narratives are teaching.

**What the Verse Records About Isolation**

The verse is the sūrah's clearest picture of prophetic isolation. Everything Lūṭ names as missing is missing because of who he is. He has no army because he has no followers. He has no support because his household does not share his faith — the accounts state that his family contained no believer except one, and that his wife was among those left behind (29:33). The verse therefore describes a man whose isolation is produced by his own message. The sūrah has been building toward this throughout: a messenger's truth does not bring him allies, and the absence of allies does not make the truth false. The verse states the cost in one sentence, and the following verse supplies what the cost could not.

**The Limit of One Man's Capacity**

Read as a statement about human limits, the verse makes a claim the sūrah supports elsewhere. There is a point at which a person's responsibility ends and his inability begins, and the point is not determined by how much he cares. Lūṭ cared enough to be distressed before anything happened, enough to offer the only alternative he had, and enough to wish for strength he did not possess. The verse records all three and then stops. The sūrah does not criticise the wish or supply the strength. It shows a man at the boundary of what he can do, and it leaves him there for exactly one verse. The following verse is the answer, and the answer does not come from anything Lūṭ has.

****What the Verse Says About Asking****

The verse is not a prayer, and the difference is instructive. Lūṭ does not ask God for strength; he wishes aloud that he had it. The sūrah records the distinction without comment, and the next verse supplies the answer from a direction the wish did not anticipate. A reader is being shown that the help arrived independently of the form of the request. The sūrah's teaching on prayer elsewhere is explicit — call on Me and I will answer — and this verse sits alongside it rather than against it. What it adds is that God's response is not limited to what a person thought to ask for. Lūṭ wished for an army and got three guests already standing in his house, which is a better answer than the one he framed. The verse therefore records a man at the exact boundary of his capacity, and the recording is what makes the answer in the following verse recognisable as an answer.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:81

> The angels said, “O Lot! We are the messengers of your Lord. They will never reach you. So travel with your family in the dark of night, and do not let any of you look back, except your wife. She will certainly suffer the fate of the others. Their appointed time is the morning. Is the morning not near?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Disclosure and Its First Assurance**

The reply opens with an identification and immediately follows it with a guarantee. *We are the messengers of your Lord* answers the wish in the previous verse without mentioning it. Lūṭ asked for strength or a supporter, and the answer is that support has been standing in his house all evening. The commentators gloss the second statement plainly: his people will not be able to harm him in any way. The verse therefore resolves the evening's central problem in its first two sentences, and the resolution comes from the guests themselves rather than from any change in the town. The sūrah's arrangement is exact: the moment Lūṭ states his incapacity completely, the capacity he lacked is disclosed as already present.

**"They Will Not Reach You"**

The phrase is absolute, and its placement before the instruction matters. Lūṭ is told he is safe before he is told what to do, and the order is significant. A man who is still afraid cannot follow a complicated instruction; his attention is on the danger. The verse removes the danger first and then gives the direction. The same structure appeared in the Ibrāhīm narrative, where the reassurance preceded the disclosure. The sūrah's method with frightened people is consistent: settle the fear, then give the command. The verse is therefore not only informing Lūṭ. It is putting him in a state where information can be acted on, and the following clauses assume that state has been achieved.

**The Instruction to Leave at Night**

The command is specific about timing. Travel with your family during a portion of the night. The commentators note that the departure was to be under cover of darkness, and the reason is practical as well as protective: a group leaving a town at night is less likely to be stopped or followed. The verse is giving operational detail rather than general assurance. A reader might expect a miraculous removal; what is given instead is an instruction with a time attached. The sūrah's method here is consistent with its treatment of rescue throughout: the deliverance is God's, and the required action is ordinary and specific. Lūṭ is not carried out of the town. He is told when to walk and who to take.

**"Let None of You Look Back"**

The prohibition is the verse's most unusual element, and the commentators connect it to what is about to happen. A person who looks back at a town being destroyed will see it, and the seeing is what the command prevents. The instruction is addressed to the whole group, which makes it a collective discipline rather than a personal test. The sūrah elsewhere uses similar prohibitions to mark the boundary between the saved and the destroyed: Nūḥ told not to plead for the wrongdoers, Ibrāhīm told to turn away from the matter. The verse adds a physical dimension to that boundary. The saved are not merely removed from the punishment; they are instructed not to witness it, and the instruction separates them from the town in the only way left available.

**The Exception of the Wife**

The verse names one exception and then explains it in the same breath. The wife is excluded from the instruction not to look back because she is excluded from the rescue, and the reason given is that she will certainly suffer the fate of the others. The commentators are consistent on her position: she outwardly followed Lūṭ's religion but was inwardly a disbeliever, and she is counted among those who remained behind (7:83; 15:60; 26:171; 27:57). The sūrah has already established the principle in the Nūḥ narrative, where a prophet's son was refused because he was not of the family in the sense that matters. The verse here applies the same principle to a prophet's wife, and the application is stated in a single clause without elaboration.

**"Is Not the Morning Near?"**

The closing question is the verse's most striking line. It is asked by the angels, and its function is not to seek information. The morning's nearness is a fact they know and Lūṭ does not, and the question makes him feel the time remaining. The commentators note that the appointed time for the destruction was the morning, and the same timing appears in the parallel account: the blast took them at sunrise (15:73). The verse therefore ends the night's events by pointing at the hour, and the pointing does two things at once. It explains the urgency of the instruction and it states the certainty of the outcome. A reader is left with a household walking out of a town in the dark toward a morning he already knows the content of.

****The Verse as the Sūrah's Only Rescue Instruction****

This is the only place in the sūrah's narratives where a prophet is given a detailed plan of escape. Nūḥ was told to build; the escape was the Ark itself. Hūd's believers are said to have been saved by a mercy without any instruction recorded. Ṣāliḥ was told to travel through the land for three days, which was a warning rather than a route. Lūṭ alone receives a time, a group, a prohibition and an exception. The sūrah's choice to give the detail here is connected to the nature of the punishment. A flood takes a whole region; a blast takes a town. An overturning with a rain of stones requires that the household be somewhere specific when the morning comes, and the verse is the arrangement that makes that possible.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:82

> When Our command came, We turned the cities upside down and rained down on them clustered stones of baked clay

**Expanded Commentary**

**"When Our Command Came"**

The verse opens with the sūrah's fixed formula for the moment of destruction, and its use here connects this narrative to every other in the chapter. The command that came to Nūḥ's people through the oven, to Thamūd at morning, and to Midian through the blast comes here in the same words. The commentators link the command in this case to the blast that seized Lūṭ's people at sunrise (15:73), and the identification matters: the sūrah is describing a single event with two aspects rather than two separate punishments. The formula's repetition across the narratives is what makes the section an argument. Each destruction is the same decision arriving in a different form, and the verse's opening words place this one inside the pattern without needing to explain it.

**The Cities Turned Upside Down**

The commentators report that the people of Lūṭ lived in five cities according to some accounts and seven according to others, with Sodom the greatest of them. The verse describes the reversal plainly: the uppermost was made the lowermost. One reading given by the commentators is that the greatest of the cities became the lowliest. The parallel account uses the same language: *"We turned the cities ˹of Sodom and Gomorrah˺ upside down"* (15:74). The sūrah records the inversion rather than the mechanism, and the inversion is the point. A town that considered itself beyond reach is described in the single image that most completely reverses its position. The verse needs no commentary on the pride of the destroyed; the geometry of the punishment states it.

**Stones of Baked Clay**

The second element of the punishment is a rain of stones, and the description of the material is specific. Baked clay is fired earth — a substance made by human hands, used for building, and here used as the instrument of a town's end. The sūrah describes the same rain in three other places: *"We poured upon them a rain ˹of brimstone˺"* (7:84), and the same phrase in 26:173 and 27:58. The recurrence across four sūrahs marks the detail as fixed. The verse's choice of material is worth noting alongside its choice of method: the cities were overturned from below and struck from above, and the stones that struck them were made of the earth the cities were built from. The sūrah does not comment on this; it reports it.

**"In Clusters, One Upon Another"**

The final phrase describes the manner of the falling, and the commentators read it as stones following one another in sequence rather than scattered singly. The word carries the sense of things arranged or marked in rows. The detail transforms the image from a meteor shower into something ordered. A rain that falls in clusters, one stone after another, is a rain with a pattern, and the following verse will make the pattern explicit. The sūrah's method here is characteristic: it gives a physical detail whose meaning emerges in the next verse rather than in this one. A reader who stops at this verse has a description of destruction; a reader who continues has a description of judgement, and the difference is the phrase the verse places at its end.

**The Verse and Its Parallels**

The destruction of Lūṭ's people is narrated in seven places in the Qurʾān, and the accounts differ in emphasis. The version in al-ʿAnkabūt records the dialogue at greater length; the version in al-Qamar is the briefest; the version in al-Ḥijr adds the sunrise timing. This sūrah's account is distinctive in what it omits: it does not describe the people's response to the angels, does not narrate the household's departure, and does not name the cities. The verse is therefore stripped to the punishment itself, and the stripping is deliberate. The sūrah's interest in this narrative has been in the household — the host, the guests, the plea, the exception of the wife. Once those are settled, the destruction needs only a verse and a half.

**Why the Method Differs**

The sūrah has now used four different punishments in five narratives: a flood, a blast, a blast with an earthquake, and here an overturning with a rain of stones. The variation is not arbitrary. Each method corresponds to what the people were proud of. Nūḥ's people were destroyed by the water they mocked him for building against. ʿĀd were destroyed by the wind they mistook for rain. Thamūd were destroyed in the homes they carved. Lūṭ's people were destroyed by the ground beneath their cities and the sky above them. The verse is therefore the fourth instance of a pattern the sūrah has been building, and the pattern is its answer to the question of why punishments differ. The form of the end matches the form of the pride.

****What the Verse Does Not Describe****

The verse omits everything a reader might expect from an account of a town's end. There is no sound, no duration, no description of what the inhabitants did in their last moments, no account of the household's whereabouts during it. The sūrah gives the reversal, the rain, and the arrangement of the stones, and stops. The omission is consistent with its treatment of the other destructions, where the event is stated and the attention is kept on the exchange that preceded it. A reader looking for the spectacle will not find it. What he finds instead is a sequence of verbs with God as the subject, and the sequence is the sūrah's point: this was done, by Someone, deliberately, in a specific way. The verse therefore gives the event in verbs whose subject is God, and the giving is the sūrah's way of refusing to let the destruction read as a natural catastrophe.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:83

> marked by your Lord ˹O Prophet˺. And these stones are not far from the ˹pagan˺ wrongdoers

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Marked By Your Lord"**

The verse's first phrase modifies the stones, and the modification changes what they are. Stones that fall are a natural event. Stones that are marked are instruments, and the marking is attributed to God directly. The commentators explain the attribution: each stone fell according to God's judgement. The phrase therefore converts the previous verse's physical description into a statement about intention. The sūrah has used the same device throughout its narratives, where the punishment is never described as a catastrophe but always as an act. The verse is the clearest instance in the chapter, because the marking is on the individual stones rather than on the event as a whole. What fell was not weather. It was a series of decisions with a destination each.

**Stones With Names**

The commentators report a tradition that each stone had inscribed on it the name of the person for whom it was intended. The same wording appears in al-Dhāriyāt: *"marked by your Lord for the transgressors"* (51:34). The verse does not state the inscription itself, and the tradition supplies what the text implies. Whether or not the inscription was literal, the claim being made is precise: the punishment was distributed by person rather than applied collectively. The sūrah elsewhere distinguishes between a punishment that takes a people as a whole and one that reaches individuals; here it makes the individual claim explicit. A reader is being told that no one in those cities was struck by accident, and that none of them escaped by being overlooked.

**"Not Far From the Wrongdoers"**

The second clause is the verse's turn, and it is the clause the sūrah's original listeners were meant to hear. The commentators give two readings. The first is local and historical: the stones were not distant from Lūṭ's people, meaning none of them escaped. The second is a threat: these stones are not far from the idolaters of Mekka, meaning the same punishment remains available. The verse does not choose between them, and the double reading is characteristic of the sūrah's method. A statement about a past event is simultaneously a statement about the present audience, and the audience is not named. The reader supplies the identification himself, which is more effective than being told.

**The Verse as a Threat to Mekka**

The second reading gives the verse its function in the sūrah's argument. Mekka and the towns of Lūṭ were connected by trade routes, and the sūrah has already made that connection explicit in its forty-ninth verse, where the accounts of the unseen were described as news not previously known. Here it makes a different connection: not that the stories are unknown, but that the means of punishment remain close. The commentators note that the clause functions as a threat precisely because it implies the Makkans would be liable to the same fate if they continued. The verse therefore does what the sūrah's whole narrative section has been doing — converting history into a present possibility — and it does it in six words.

**What the Verse Closes**

The Lūṭ narrative ends without a refrain. The two stories immediately before it closed with the same dismissal — *away with such a people* — and this one does not. The omission is significant. The destroyed towns of ʿĀd, Thamūd and Midian were dismissed with a curse; Lūṭ's people are dismissed with a threat aimed at the listeners. The sūrah's reason is likely structural: the previous refrains marked the end of a people, while this one marks the beginning of an application. A reader is not being told that the story is over. He is being told that the stones are still within range. The change of ending is therefore a change of audience, and it prepares the shift that the following verses will make toward the final narrative.

**The Narrative's Final Note**

Read as the closing of five destructions, the verse establishes a cumulative claim. The sūrah has now shown a flood, two blasts, an overturning with stones, and in each case the same structure: a messenger, a refusal, a decree, an end. The verse's final clause gathers all five into a single present fact. The means are not far. The commentators' reading makes the point explicit for the Makkans, but the point does not depend on them. A reader in any place and time is being told that the distance between a settled pattern of refusal and its consequence is not fixed and not long. The sūrah has spent eighty-three verses establishing the pattern; this verse states its availability.

****The Two Readings and Why Both Matter****

The commentators' two readings of the closing clause are not competing interpretations but two applications of one statement. Read historically, the stones were not far from Lūṭ's people: nobody escaped. Read as a warning, they are not far from anyone doing what Lūṭ's people did. The verse supports both because its wording is deliberately unspecifed about time. The sūrah uses the same technique elsewhere, where a statement about a destroyed nation is phrased so that it also addresses the listeners. The double reading is not a weakness in the text; it is the mechanism by which a historical account becomes a present warning, and the mechanism depends on the verse refusing to fix its own horizon.

****Where the Narrative Leaves the Reader****

Five destructions have now been narrated, and the sūrah has not commented on any of them directly. There is no summary, no moral drawn in the narrator's voice, no list of lessons. What the section provides instead is repetition with variation: the same structure, different peoples, different punishments, the same ending. The reader is expected to draw the pattern himself, and the final clause of this verse is the sūrah's only direct address to him. It tells him where he stands in relation to the pattern — within range of it. The method is characteristic of the whole chapter, which states its doctrines in the first twenty-four verses and then spends sixty demonstrating them without saying so.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:84

> And to the people of Midian We sent their brother Shu’aib. He said, “O my people! Worship Allah. You have no god other than Him. And do not give short measure and weight. I do see you in prosperity now, but I truly fear for you the torment of an overwhelming Day

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sixth and Final Narrative**

This is the last full narrative in the sūrah's cycle, and its position matters. Four destructions have been narrated — Nūḥ's people, ʿĀd, Thamūd and Lūṭ's towns — and the Mūsā account that follows is shorter and differently shaped. The Shuʿayb narrative is therefore the climax of the cycle rather than one more entry in it. The sūrah signals this by giving the narrative the longest dialogue in the section: eleven verses of exchange, against Hūd's eleven and Ṣāliḥ's eight. The reader is being given more speech here than anywhere else in the cycle, and the extra speech is where the sūrah's argument about justice is made. What the previous narratives established as doctrine, this one states as practice.

**A Prophet With a Different Charge**

The charge against Midian is unlike the charges against the previous peoples. ʿĀd were accused of building for display and of violence. Thamūd were accused of enjoying themselves without gratitude. Lūṭ's people were accused of a specific sexual practice. Midian is accused of cheating in trade. The difference is not a softening of the sūrah's standard. It is an extension of it. The verse establishes that commercial honesty belongs to the same category as belief and moral conduct, and that a prophet is sent to address it. The sūrah's decision to place this narrative last in the cycle gives the claim maximum weight: after four accounts of destroyed nations, the final one concerns short measures and false weights.

**"Their Brother Shuʿayb"**

The verse uses the sūrah's standard formula for a prophet's relation to his people, and the commentators add that Shuʿayb is identified by many Muslim authorities with Jethro, the father-in-law of Mūsā, and that he was sent to Midian in northwestern Arabia. The brotherhood is what makes the accusation that follows bearable. A stranger denouncing a town's commerce would be dismissed as an outsider. A brother of the same tribe cannot be dismissed that way, and the people's response in the following verses confirms that they recognise the tie even while rejecting the message. The verse therefore establishes the relation before the charge, and the relation is what gives the charge its force.

**Two Commands Joined in One Sentence**

The opening of the speech is the same in every narrative: worship God, you have no god but Him. The verse then adds a second command in the same breath, and the addition is the distinctive feature of this account. The sūrah does not treat the two commands as separate items on a list. It joins them with *and*, in a single address, as though exclusive worship and honest dealing were one requirement stated twice. The joining is the verse's theological claim. A people who worship God alone and cheat their customers have not fulfilled the first command; a people who trade honestly while worshipping idols have not fulfilled the second. The verse refuses to separate them, and the refusal is what the people's response objects to.

**"I See You in Prosperity Now"**

The clause is an observation, and its placement is deliberate. Shuʿayb acknowledges what his people can see for themselves: they are doing well. The commentators note that the word describes a state of wellbeing or prosperity, and the acknowledgement is not a concession. It is the premise of the warning. A people in poverty might cheat out of need; a people in prosperity cheat out of greed, and the second case is the graver one. The sūrah has made the same point throughout its doctrinal section, where the person who receives mercy becomes insolent. The verse applies the point to a whole town. Midian is not suffering; it is thriving, and the thriving is what makes its practices a moral question rather than a practical one.

**"An Overwhelming Day"**

The warning names the Day with a word that carries envelopment — something that surrounds and covers from every side. The sūrah has used the same description in its third verse, where the punishment to come was said to have no one to turn it away, and the word here matches it. The verse is therefore connecting this narrative to the sūrah's opening claim rather than issuing a new threat. The Day that Shuʿayb fears for his people is the Day the sūrah announced at the beginning, and the connection is made through vocabulary rather than through explicit reference. A reader who has followed the chapter from its first verse hears the echo; a reader who has not still receives the warning, but without the argument behind it.

**Why This Narrative Comes Last**

The ordering of the cycle is instructive. The sūrah moves from a flood to a wind to a blast to an overturning, and the peoples are separated by centuries. The final narrative concerns a sin the Makkans were committing at the time of revelation — the commentators note that short measure was a problem among the Makkans, and Sūrah 83 is devoted to it. The placement is therefore deliberate: after four accounts of distant nations, the last one describes a live local practice. The sūrah is not merely reciting history. It is building to a case its listeners can recognise in their own market, and the recognition is what the following verses will exploit. The verse therefore arrives as the cycle's last word on what a society can be destroyed for, and the answer it gives is smaller and more ordinary than any of the four that came before.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:85

> O my people! Give full measure and weigh with justice. Do not defraud people of their property, nor go about spreading corruption in the land

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Positive Form of the Command**

The previous verse prohibited short measure; this verse commands full measure, and the shift from prohibition to positive instruction is the verse's first feature. A prohibition tells a person what to stop. A command tells him what to do instead. The sūrah's method throughout is to give both, and the pairing is what makes the requirement actionable. A merchant told not to cheat can stop cheating without knowing what honesty requires in practice. A merchant told to give full measure has a standard to measure against. The verse therefore completes the instruction rather than repeating it, and the completion is characteristic of the sūrah's treatment of every command it issues: the prohibition and its positive counterpart appear together.

**"Weigh With Justice"**

The word for justice here is *qisṭ*, and the commentators take the phrase to mean weighing with an even balance — neither short nor, by implication, excessive in the other direction. The requirement is precision rather than generosity. The verse is not asking merchants to be lavish; it is asking them to be accurate. The distinction matters because accuracy is testable. A person can check whether his measure is full and whether his balance is level, and the check can be made by the person on the other side of the transaction. The sūrah's use of a measurable standard is deliberate. A charge that can be verified is harder to dismiss than a charge about intention, and the people's response in the following verses shows them trying to dismiss it anyway.

**"Do Not Defraud People of Their Property"**

The third element names the harm rather than the act. The commentators gloss it as giving people the full measure of what they paid for. The verse's formulation is significant: it describes cheating as taking from a person's property rather than as an irregularity in a transaction. The shift locates the wrong in the loss suffered by the other party. A merchant who short-measures has not merely used a wrong method; he has taken something that belonged to someone else. The sūrah's use of the word *property* connects the offence to the category of theft, and the connection is what elevates a commercial practice into a matter requiring a prophet. The verse is therefore stating that the transaction has a victim.

**"Spreading Corruption in the Land"**

The final phrase widens the scope beyond the marketplace. The commentators link it to the general prohibition against working corruption on the earth, and the same phrase appears in the parallel account in al-Aʿrāf (7:85). The word *fasād* denotes disorder, ruin, the breaking of a thing's proper condition. Applied to a society, it describes a state in which ordinary dealings cannot be relied on. The verse is therefore making a claim about the social effect of the practice: systematic cheating does not merely transfer wealth, it destroys the conditions that make exchange possible. The sūrah elsewhere uses the same vocabulary for the effect of rejecting the messengers, and its application here to commerce places trade inside the same category of moral consequence.

**Why the Charge Is Commercial**

The sūrah's choice to make commerce the central sin of its final narrative deserves attention. The previous peoples were destroyed for idolatry, arrogance, ingratitude and sexual practice. Midian is destroyed for a practice that leaves the temples standing and the public order intact. The verse is therefore making a claim about what qualifies a society for destruction, and the claim is broad. A town can be outwardly orderly, prosperous and religiously conventional, and still be in the condition the sūrah describes as corruption. The verse does not explain the claim; it states the charge. The explanation is left to the following verses, where the people's response reveals what they think their wealth entitles them to do.

**The Verse and Sūrat al-Muṭaffifīn**

The commentators note that short measure was a live problem among the Makkans, and Sūrah 83 is devoted entirely to it: woe to those who give less than they take, who demand full measure when they receive and give less when they give. The connection makes this verse more than a historical charge. The original listeners were hearing a description of their own market practices delivered in the mouth of a prophet whose people were destroyed for them. The sūrah's method throughout has been to make history applicable, and here the application requires no interpretation. The verse describes an act, the act is being committed by the audience, and the outcome of the act has just been narrated four times.

****What the Verse Assumes About a Market****

Behind the four commands lies an assumption the verse does not state but clearly holds: that a market is a moral space. The instructions are not regulations imposed on trade from outside. They are addressed to merchants as merchants, in the vocabulary of their own work, and they assume that the weighing of goods is a place where a person's relation with God is expressed. The sūrah's willingness to make this assumption is what distinguishes its moral teaching from a purely ritual one. A person can be exact in worship and careless in measurement, and the verse treats the carelessness as the graver fault. The assumption is not argued for because it does not need to be: a market in which nobody can trust a measure has already ceased to function, and the verse is describing what keeps it functioning. The verse therefore treats the market as one of the places where a person's faith is actually exercised, and not the least demanding of them.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:86

> What is left ˹as a lawful gain˺ by Allah is far better for you if you are ˹truly˺ believers. And I am not a keeper over you.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**"What Is Left by Allah"**

The phrase translates *baqiyyat Allāh*, and the word means a remainder or that which remains. The commentators give the primary reading directly: wealth obtained through honest practice and given by God is better than gains acquired through fraud, the remainder being what is left after a person gives full measure and abstains from the unlawful. The formulation is economical. It does not prohibit the gain; it compares two kinds of gain and states which is better. The verse is therefore arguing with a merchant on his own terms. The assumption behind the fraud is that more is better. The verse accepts the assumption and disputes the arithmetic: the smaller lawful amount, being what remains after the unlawful has been left, is the greater of the two.

**The Two Readings of the Remnant**

A second reading runs in another direction, and it deserves equal weight. The remnant may mean what God keeps for a person on the far side of this life — His acceptance, His pleasure, a reward that does not run out. On this reading the comparison is not between two sums but between a sum and something with no equivalent in currency. The sūrah states the same preference in other words elsewhere: what is with Allah is far better and more lasting for those who believe and put their trust in Him (42:36). Both readings follow from the word itself, which means simply what stays behind. What stays behind depends on the horizon a person measures against, and the verse leaves the horizon to the hearer rather than fixing it for him.

**"If You Are Believers"**

The condition attached to the claim is the verse's most searching phrase. Shuʿayb does not say the remnant is better, full stop. He says it is better *if you are believers*. The condition identifies what makes the comparison visible. A person who believes that God gives provision can see that a lawful remainder is greater than an unlawful surplus. A person who does not believe this can only compare the two amounts, and by that comparison the surplus wins. The verse is therefore not stating a fact available to anyone who calculates. It is stating a fact available to a person with a specific belief, and it names the belief as the condition. The sūrah's sixth verse made the same point in a different form: God's giving is not distributed according to human deserving.

**"I Am Not a Keeper Over You"**

The closing sentence takes the speaker out of the matter. Shuʿayb has made his case, named the condition under which the case holds, and then stepped aside. He is not a guard posted over his people; he cannot watch them, cannot enforce what he has said, and cannot do the arithmetic for them. The same disclaimer is given to this Prophet again and again across the Qurʾān, and the recurrence marks it as part of the office rather than as one man's modesty. Its position here is what gives it force. A merchant who has just heard that honesty is the better deal may reasonably ask who intends to make him accept it. The verse answers that nobody does, and that the answer is not a weakness in the argument.

**The Verse as an Argument From Economics**

The verse is unusual in the sūrah for arguing from interest rather than from obligation. The previous verse commanded; this one persuades. It tells a merchant that honesty is the better deal, and it makes the claim in the merchant's own vocabulary of gain and remainder. The sūrah's willingness to argue this way is instructive. It does not treat economic self-interest as an unworthy motive; it treats it as a real one and addresses it. The verse therefore meets its hearers where they are rather than where a preacher would prefer them to be. The condition at the end then raises the argument to a level where self-interest alone cannot follow, and the raising is what distinguishes persuasion from preaching.

**Why the Verse Ends Where It Does**

The closing disclaimer changes the verse's register at its last moment. The argument has been close and practical — measure, weight, remainder, gain — and the final sentence withdraws the speaker from the whole matter. The effect is to leave the hearers holding the argument without holding the arguer. The sūrah has used the same device elsewhere, where a messenger states his position and then declines to be responsible for the response. The verse's ending is therefore not a rhetorical flourish. It is the sūrah's consistent teaching about the limit of a messenger's role, stated at the point where a hearer might otherwise assume that the messenger intends to enforce what he has said.

****Why the Argument Is Put in These Terms****

The verse's choice of vocabulary is worth a final note. It speaks of gain, remainder, and what is better — the language of a merchant's own ledger. It does not speak of sin, punishment or obligation, though all three are available and all three appear elsewhere in the speech. The sūrah is showing a messenger selecting his ground. Shuʿayb knows that his hearers weigh things for a living, and he puts the case in weights. The selection is not a compromise with the message; it is the message delivered where it can be heard. A reader is being shown that a prophet's task includes choosing the register in which a truth will land, and that choosing the hearer's own register is not a lowering of the truth but the only way of getting it across to a person who has already decided what he will not listen to.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:87

> They asked ˹sarcastically˺, “O Shu’aib! Does your prayer command you that we should abandon what our forefathers worshipped or give up managing our wealth as we please? Indeed, you are such a tolerant, sensible man!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question Asked in Mockery**

The response is a question, and its form tells the reader how it is meant. The commentators note that the question is a form of mockery, and the sūrah's own wording supports the reading: the people ask whether his prayer *commands* this, as though the idea were absurd. They are not seeking information about the relation between prayer and conduct. They are saying that the relation is laughable. The verse therefore records the first move in a familiar exchange: a messenger makes a demand, and the hearers respond by treating the demand's source as ridiculous. The sūrah has shown the same move three times already, and each time the mockery is preserved in the people's own words rather than reported by the narrator.

**"Does Your Prayer Command You?"**

The phrase identifies prayer as the source of the demand, and the identification is significant. Shuʿayb has not claimed that his message comes from private conviction or social concern. The people understand it as arising from his worship, and their question shows that they understand correctly. What they reject is the implication: that a man's relation with God produces requirements for other people's business. The verse is therefore recording an objection that goes to the heart of the prophetic claim. If prayer only concerns the one who prays, it can be tolerated. If prayer generates obligations for everyone, it cannot. The sūrah's whole narrative section depends on the second position, and this verse states the first position in the words of those who hold it.

**Two Things Named Together**

The people name two requirements in a single breath: abandoning their forefathers' worship, and ceasing to manage their wealth as they please. The joining is their own, not Shuʿayb's, and it reveals how they understand his message. To them the religious demand and the economic demand are one thing, and their objection covers both. The verse therefore confirms what the previous verses asserted — that exclusive worship and honest dealing are a single requirement — by showing the hearers treating them that way. The sūrah often establishes a point through its opponents' speech rather than through its narrator's, and this is a clear instance. The people are not confused about what Shuʿayb is asking. They are objecting to all of it.

**"As We Please"**

The phrase is the verse's most revealing line. The claim being made is not that their practices are correct or that their measures are accurate. It is that the wealth is theirs to do with as they wish. The verse is therefore recording a specific understanding of property: that ownership confers unlimited discretion. The sūrah's answer is given in the previous verse and will be expanded in the next — that provision comes from God and that what remains after honesty is the better portion. The two positions are irreconcilable, and the verse states the objection in its purest form. A reader is being shown that the dispute is not about measurement techniques. It is about whether a person's possessions are his to dispose of without reference to anyone else.

**The Mocking Compliment**

The closing sentence praises Shuʿayb, and the commentators are clear that the praise is inverted. They note that even in calling him forbearing and of sound judgement, the people intimate that he has these qualities only in his own estimation. The word rendered *tolerant* here is the same word the sūrah used of Ibrāhīm — *ḥalīm* — and its use as mockery is a measure of how far the people are from taking him seriously. The sūrah preserves the compliment because the preservation exposes them. A person who must mock a prophet's character rather than answer his argument has run out of answers. The verse does not need to say so; the compliment does the work.

**What the Verse Reveals About Them**

Read as a portrait, the verse describes a people who have decided in advance what they will not accept. They do not dispute the evidence, question the measure, or propose an alternative. They identify the source of the demand, describe it as unreasonable, and compliment the man making it. The sūrah has shown the same pattern throughout its narratives, and its consistency is the point: the rejection is never really about the argument. The verse therefore belongs to the sūrah's larger claim, stated in its opening verses, that people respond to a warning according to what they have already decided about themselves. The Midianites' answer is not an argument. It is a statement of position, delivered politely enough to sound like one.

****The Verse and the Sūrah's Other Mockeries****

The sūrah has recorded mockery in every narrative, and comparing the instances shows a progression. Nūḥ's notables called him a man gone astray. ʿĀd dismissed Hūd as one possessed. Thamūd's elite said they would not follow the lowest among them. Lūṭ's people made no argument at all. Midian's mockery is the most sophisticated of the set: it takes the form of a question, includes a compliment, and identifies the source of the demand rather than the demand itself. The verse is therefore showing the most developed form of refusal the sūrah has encountered, and the development makes it more dangerous rather than less. A mockery that sounds reasonable is harder for a listener to see through, which is why the sūrah preserves it in full. What the verse records, then, is a closed mind announcing itself politely — and the politeness is what makes the record useful.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:88

> He said, “O my people! Consider if I stand on a clear proof from my Lord and He has blessed me with a good provision from Him. I do not want to do what I am forbidding you from. I only intend reform to the best of my ability. My success comes only through Allah. In Him I trust and to Him I turn

**Expanded Commentary**

**Four Statements in One Speech**

The reply contains four distinct claims, and their arrangement is worth tracing. First: I stand on a clear proof from God. Second: I do not intend to do what I forbid. Third: I intend only reform, so far as I am able. Fourth: my success is from God, and to Him I trust and turn. The sequence moves from authority to integrity to intention to dependence. Each statement answers a different possible objection, and none of them answers the mockery directly. Shuʿayb does not respond to the insult. He states his position in four clauses and leaves the insult unaddressed, which is itself a response. The sūrah has shown the same restraint in Hūd, who answered mockery with an oath and a challenge rather than with a counter-insult.

**"A Clear Proof From My Lord"**

The commentators identify the clear proof as the miracles given to prophets to establish their veracity, and they note the parallel with the same phrase in al-Aʿrāf (7:85). The verse's use of the phrase is conditional in form — *consider if I stand on* — and the condition is rhetorical rather than doubtful. Shuʿayb is not wondering whether he has a proof; he is asking his hearers to consider the case in which he does. The formulation is significant because it shifts the burden. The people have dismissed the message by mocking its source. The verse asks them to examine the source rather than the messenger's character, and the asking is done in the form of an invitation rather than an assertion.

**"I Do Not Want to Do What I Forbid You"**

The second statement addresses integrity, and it answers the most damaging charge available against anyone who preaches. A man who prohibits what he practises privately has no standing to prohibit it. Shuʿayb removes the charge before it is made. The verse's wording is careful: he does not claim to be sinless or to have never erred. He states that he does not *intend* to do what he forbids. The sūrah's precision here is characteristic. A claim of perfection would be both unverifiable and theologically wrong; a claim about intention is one a person can make honestly and be held to. The verse therefore gives the hearers something testable, and the test is his conduct over time rather than a statement about his past.

**"Reform to the Best of My Ability"**

The third statement defines the scope of the mission, and the definition is deliberately limited. Shuʿayb does not claim to be able to fix his society. He intends reform *so far as he is able*, and the qualifier is not modesty but accuracy. The sūrah has established the limit twice already in this chapter: no soul is charged beyond its capacity, and a messenger is not a keeper over his people. The verse applies the limit to the messenger himself. His intention is real and its reach is finite, and the finitude is stated in the same sentence as the intention. The effect is to separate the sincerity of the effort from the success of the outcome, which is exactly the distinction the following clause will make explicit.

**"My Success Comes Only Through Allah"**

The fourth statement is the verse's most quoted line, and the commentators note that it has become a standard expression in Muslim usage, conveying that whatever a person does, it is ultimately up to God whether it succeeds and carries blessing. The phrase's placement is significant: it follows immediately on the statement of limited ability. A man who intends reform so far as he is able, and who then says that success is not his to produce, has described the prophetic office completely. The sūrah's own doctrinal section made the same point about provision — God gives to whomever He wills — and the verse applies it to the outcome of preaching. The messenger supplies the effort; the result is not in his gift.

**"In Him I Trust and to Him I Turn"**

The final clause combines two movements, and the commentators note that the same words are taught to this Prophet in 42:10. Trust is reliance on God for what one cannot control. Turning is the movement of return described throughout the sūrah as *tawba* — seeking forgiveness and coming back. The pairing describes a person who has both surrendered the outcome and kept the relation. The verse's decision to end here is significant. Having stated his proof, his integrity, his limited intention and his dependence, Shuʿayb closes with the two dispositions that hold the rest together. The sūrah has been recommending exactly these two things since its eleventh verse, and here they appear in the mouth of a prophet rather than in the narrator's instruction.

****What the Speech Does Not Contain****

The reply is notable for what it leaves out. Shuʿayb does not answer the mockery, does not defend his character against the compliment, and does not repeat the commands about measure and weight. He has already made the economic case and does not make it again. The omission is instructive. A messenger who has stated his position and been mocked for it does not gain anything by restating it; he gains something by describing where he stands. The sūrah shows the same economy elsewhere, and its consistency suggests a deliberate teaching about speech. Once an argument has been given and refused, further repetition hardens the refusal. What remains useful is a statement of the speaker's own ground, and the verse supplies exactly that in four clauses.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:89

> O my people! Do not let your opposition to me lead you to a fate similar to that of the people of Noah, or Hûd, or Ṣâliḥ. And the people of Lot are not far from you

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Warning Framed as a Caution About Anger**

The verse does not begin by threatening. It begins by identifying a mechanism: your opposition to me may carry you into the same fate. The commentators gloss it directly — let not your enmity toward me cause you to commit sin, so that you are afflicted as previous generations were. The framing is significant. The people are not being warned about their measures at this point; they are being warned about their feelings toward the man who mentioned them. The sūrah is describing a real danger, which is that resentment of a critic becomes its own motive and pulls a person past decisions he would not otherwise make. The verse treats the danger as diagnosable and names it before it operates.

**Four Peoples Named in One Sentence**

Shuʿayb names four destroyed communities, and the naming is a summary of the sūrah itself. The people of Nūḥ were destroyed by the flood narrated in verses 25 to 48. The people of Hūd by the wind in verses 50 to 60. The people of Ṣāliḥ by the blast in verses 61 to 68. The people of Lūṭ by the overturning in verses 77 to 83. The verse therefore functions as a table of contents for the narrative section, delivered in the mouth of a prophet rather than by the narrator. The effect is to show that the prophets knew each other's histories. Shuʿayb is not citing scripture; he is citing events, and he cites them to people who could not have read them anywhere.

**"The People of Lūṭ Are Not Far From You"**

The final clause is the verse's sharpest line, and the commentators explain its force: Lūṭ's community was the most recent to perish and the closest to Midian in time. The phrase recalls the closing clause of verse 83, where the stones were said to be not far from the wrongdoers. The sūrah is using the same device twice within ten verses — proximity as threat — and the second use is more specific. The first was about stones; this one is about time. A people who think of the destroyed nations as ancient history are told that the most recent case is close enough to remember. The verse therefore attacks the assumption that makes history safe to ignore.

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Own Summary**

Read structurally, the verse does work the sūrah could have done in the narrator's voice and chose not to. The four names gather the entire narrative section into a single sentence spoken by a character. The choice is significant because it keeps the argument inside the story. A narrator's summary would be a comment on the narratives; a prophet's citation of them is a use of them. The sūrah's method throughout is to let its material argue through the people in it, and this verse is the clearest instance. The reader receives the summary at the moment a prophet needs it, for the purpose a prophet needs it for, and the summary carries the weight of an argument rather than of a recapitulation.

**Why the Warning Names Peoples, Not Punishments**

The verse says a fate *similar to* theirs, and does not describe what happened to any of them. The omission is deliberate. Shuʿayb's hearers do not need the details; they need the identification. A warning that describes the flood, the wind and the blast would be a lecture. A warning that names four communities and leaves their ends unstated requires the hearer to supply them, and supplying them is what makes the warning land. The sūrah has already narrated all four in this chapter, so a reader has the details. The original listeners had them from the same oral tradition the sūrah draws on. The verse's economy therefore depends on shared knowledge, and the dependence is a measure of how familiar these accounts already were.

**The Verse and the Danger of Resentment**

The verse makes a claim about human behaviour that is worth stating plainly. It says that opposition to a person can become a reason for action independent of the matter in dispute. The Midianites' grievance is not with the theology; it is with the man who told them their trade was corrupt. The verse identifies that grievance as the operative danger, and the identification is supported by the Qurʾān elsewhere, which warns against letting resentment of a people push one into injustice (5:2, 8). The sūrah is therefore giving a warning about a mechanism rather than about a punishment. The punishment follows the mechanism, and the mechanism is what a person can still interrupt.

****What the Verse Asks of Its Hearers****

The verse makes a demand that is easier to state than to meet. It asks a people to separate their feelings about a man from their conduct in a matter he has raised. The two are naturally fused: a person who dislikes the messenger finds the message harder to accept, and the difficulty feels like evidence against the message. The verse identifies the fusion and asks for it to be undone. The sūrah does not suggest this is easy, and it does not offer a technique. It states the danger, names the four consequences of ignoring it, and stops. The demand is therefore placed squarely on the hearer, and the naming of four destroyed communities is the verse's way of showing what the demand is worth. The verse therefore asks for something hard and offers no shortcut, which is why it names four nations in the same sentence.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:90

> So seek your Lord’s forgiveness and turn to Him in repentance. Surely my Lord is Most Merciful, All-Loving.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Two Commands as Hūd's**

The verse repeats, word for word, the command Hūd gave his people thirty-eight verses earlier: seek forgiveness from your Lord, then turn to Him. The commentators note the identity, and the repetition is not economy. It establishes that the requirement is the same in every case, whatever the specific sin. ʿĀd were proud builders; Midian are cheating merchants; the command addressed to both is identical. The sūrah is therefore stating that repentance is not tailored to the offence. There is no special repentance for commercial fraud and another for arrogance. There is one movement — ask, then return — and it applies to whatever the case may be. The verse's placement at the end of Shuʿayb's speech gives the claim its final position in the cycle.

**"Most Merciful, All-Loving"**

The speech closes on two divine names, and the second is the distinctive one. *Raḥīm* — merciful — has appeared throughout the chapter. *Wadūd* — loving, affectionate, the one who loves and is loved — appears here for the first time in the sūrah. The commentators take the name to denote God's love for His servants and the love He inspires in them. Its use at this point is significant. The verse is not closing with a name of power or justice; it is closing with a name of affection. A people who have just been told that four nations were destroyed receive, as the final word of the prophet's address, a name that describes God's love. The choice is not sentimental. It is the ground on which the command to return rests.

**Why the Name Matters Here**

The verse is addressed to a people who have mocked their prophet and threatened to stone him. The natural closing for such a speech would be a warning, and the warning has already been given in the previous verse. What follows it is a name of love, and the sequence is deliberate. The sūrah is showing that the offer of return does not depend on the hearers' deserving it. A person who has just insulted a messenger is still being told that his Lord is loving. The verse therefore makes a claim about the order of things: the love is not a response to repentance but the reason repentance is possible. The sūrah's doctrinal section made the same claim in different words, when it stated that God's giving is not distributed according to merit.

**Why the Verse Closes the Speech**

The placement of this verse at the end of Shuʿayb's address is significant. He has argued from economics, stated his proof, defined his limits, warned with four examples, and named a mechanism of resentment. The final move is not an argument at all. It is an invitation grounded in a divine attribute. The sūrah's method throughout is to end a prophet's case with something that does not depend on the hearer's assent. Hūd closed with a challenge; Ṣāliḥ with a test; Shuʿayb closes with a name. The variation shows that the closing move is chosen for the audience. A people who trade need to be told that what they are being offered is better than what they have, and the verse tells them in the strongest available terms.

**The Verse and the Sūrah's Opening Command**

The sūrah opened with a command to worship and then seek forgiveness, and it will close with a command to be steadfast and pray. This verse sits between them as the third statement of the same requirement. The recurrence is the sūrah's structural signature: the opening doctrine, the narratives' repeated demand, and the closing instruction all say the same thing. The verse therefore confirms that the narrative section has not wandered from the chapter's subject. A reader who has followed the six stories might conclude that they are illustrations. The verse states that they are repetitions of a single command, and the command is the one the chapter began with.

**What the Pairing of Names Does**

The two names together describe a relation rather than an attribute. Mercy concerns what God withholds from a person — the punishment his acts deserve. Love concerns what God gives beyond that — nearness, acceptance, affection. The verse pronounces both over a people who have earned neither, and the pronouncement is what makes the command intelligible. A person told to return needs to know that returning will be received. The verse supplies the knowledge in two words. The sūrah's treatment of divine names throughout has been functional rather than descriptive: each name is placed where it does specific work in an argument, and this pairing does the work of making an impossible invitation credible.

****Why the Invitation Comes After the Warning****

The order of the two verses is the sūrah's argument in miniature. The warning comes first: four peoples destroyed, a mechanism of resentment identified, a fate named. The invitation comes second: seek forgiveness, turn, my Lord is merciful and loving. The order is not accidental. An invitation issued before a warning would be received as reassurance and would cost the hearer nothing. An invitation issued after a warning is received by a person who has just been shown what is at stake, and it therefore means something. The sūrah's arrangement across the whole chapter follows the same sequence — the threat establishes the situation, the offer makes it survivable — and here the sequence is compressed into two adjacent verses. The verse therefore ends a prophet's case the way the sūrah ends its own: not with a demand but with a description of God.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:91

> They threatened, “O Shu’aib! We do not comprehend much of what you say, and surely we see you powerless among us. Were it not for your clan, we would have certainly stoned you, for you are nothing to us.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We Do Not Comprehend Much of What You Say"**

The reply opens with a claim of incomprehension, and the commentators take it as derision rather than as an admission. The people are not saying the speech was difficult. They are saying it is not worth understanding. The distinction matters, because a genuine failure to understand invites explanation and a claimed one ends the conversation. The sūrah preserves the phrase because it exposes the actual ground of the rejection. No argument has been answered; the hearing itself has been declined. The verse therefore records a move that is common and difficult to counter: a person who will not engage cannot be persuaded, and the sūrah shows Shuʿayb facing exactly this. His previous speech contained a proof, an integrity claim and an invitation. None of them is addressed.

**"We See You Powerless Among Us"**

The second statement assesses Shuʿayb's social position, and the commentators gloss the word as lowly. The assessment is accurate by the standards being applied. Shuʿayb has no followers of consequence, no armed support, no standing that would make him dangerous. The verse records the people stating this to his face, and the stating is the point. A person who must be told he is weak is being told that his weakness has been calculated and found decisive. The sūrah has described the same condition for Lūṭ two verses earlier, where the prophet wished for strength he did not have. The difference is that Lūṭ said it about himself and Midian says it about Shuʿayb. The verse therefore shows the same fact functioning as a weapon in the mouth of the people.

**"Were It Not for Your Clan"**

The third statement is the verse's most informative line, and the commentators explain the tribal context directly. In ancient Arabian society a man was protected by his clan, and since Shuʿayb's clan was part of Midian's religious community, they could not harm him without breaking with their own. The verse is therefore recording the only reason the prophet is alive. His protection is not divine intervention, not eloquence, not fear of God. It is a social structure that has nothing to do with his message and would equally protect a man saying nothing at all. The sūrah's willingness to state this is remarkable. It does not attribute the protection to God's care; it attributes it to tribal custom, and lets the reader see the arrangement for what it is.

**"You Are Nothing to Us"**

The final clause completes the assessment, and the commentators read it as *you have no power over us* or *you are of no account among us*. Either way the claim is that Shuʿayb does not register. The verse is therefore recording a total dismissal: the speech is not understood, the speaker is weak, his survival is accidental, and his standing is nil. The sūrah preserves all four claims in the people's own words, and the preservation is what makes the next verse possible. Shuʿayb's answer will address the third claim specifically, because the third is the one that reveals what the people actually fear. The verse is therefore not merely an insult. It is a confession, and the following verse will show that Shuʿayb heard it as one.

**The Verse as a Statement of Tribal Reality**

Read sociologically, the verse describes a society in which protection is collective and belief is individual. Shuʿayb's clan shields him; his clan does not follow him. The two facts together define his position precisely: safe and alone. The sūrah has described the same position for Lūṭ, whose household contained no believer but one, and for Nūḥ, whose followers were few. The recurrence is the sūrah's account of what a messenger actually occupies — a space between his people's protection and his people's rejection. The verse states the arrangement without commenting on it, and the absence of comment is significant. The sūrah does not treat the tribal protection as a mercy or as a humiliation. It records it as a fact, and the fact is what the next verse turns into an argument.

**What the Verse Sets Up**

The people have said three things, and one of them is a mistake. The claims that Shuʿayb is weak and of no account are descriptions of his social position, which he does not dispute. The claim that they spare him only for his clan is a statement about what actually restrains them, and it is the claim the next verse takes up. The sūrah's arrangement is precise: the insult is allowed to stand, and the confession is answered. A reader watching the exchange can see the difference between what the people say about Shuʿayb and what they say about themselves. The first is abuse; the second is information. The verse provides both, and the following verse uses only the second.

****The Verse and the Sūrah's Account of Power****

The verse measures a prophet by the standards of tribal society and finds him wanting, and the sūrah preserves the measurement without correcting it at this point. The correction comes in the next verse, and it does not dispute the measurement. It asks whether the standards being applied are the right ones. The verse therefore sets up the sūrah's central contrast: what counts as strength in a society and what counts as strength before God. ʿĀd were strong and destroyed; Thamūd carved homes in rock and were found dead in them; Midian's people assess Shuʿayb as powerless and are about to be found lifeless in their homes. The verse supplies the assessment that the following verses will overturn.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:92

> He said, “O my people! Do you have more regard for my clan than for Allah, turning your back on Him entirely? Surely my Lord is Fully Aware of what you do

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question That Reverses the Comparison**

Shuʿayb's reply is a single question, and it takes the people's own statement as its material. They said they spare him for his clan. He asks whether his clan is stronger with them than God. The reversal is exact: they have revealed what actually restrains them, and he turns the revelation into a comparison they cannot defend. The verse does not dispute the fact of the clan's protection. It asks what the fact means. A person who refrains from killing a prophet out of regard for a tribe, while feeling no restraint from God, has disclosed his actual hierarchy. The verse names the hierarchy in the form of a question, and the question is unanswerable because the answer is obvious and damning.

**"Turning Your Back on Him"**

The phrase describes the people's relation to God, and the commentators gloss it as casting God behind their backs without fear of Him. The image is physical: a person who puts something behind him has stopped attending to it. The verse is therefore describing not disbelief in God's existence but disregard of His claims. The distinction is central to the sūrah's diagnosis throughout. The peoples it narrates do not argue that God does not exist; they act as though His requirements do not reach their conduct. The phrase captures that condition in three words, and its placement immediately after the question makes the connection explicit. The people who respect a clan more than God have not rejected Him in argument. They have turned away from Him in practice.

**"My Lord Encompasses What You Do"**

The closing statement shifts from the question to a declaration, and the declaration concerns knowledge. The commentators note the same formula elsewhere in the sūrah: God is aware of what people do, and the awareness is stated at the point where people have just acted as though it were absent. The verse's placement of the statement is significant. It follows a question the people cannot answer, and it supplies the reason the question matters. A hierarchy that puts a clan above God is not a private arrangement; it is being recorded. The sūrah has made this point throughout, and here it comes at the moment when the people have stated their hierarchy aloud. The verse therefore tells them that the statement has been heard by the One it concerns.

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Answer to Tribalism**

The verse makes a claim that would have been directly provocative to its original audience. It says that the tribal protection a man enjoys is worth less than the regard he owes God, and that treating it otherwise is a turning away. The commentators' explanation of the tribal context makes the claim sharper: the clan that protected Shuʿayb was part of the same religious community that rejected him. The protection was therefore not a sign of the clan's faith. It was custom. The verse asks the people to notice that they are governed by custom in a matter where God's claim is direct, and the asking is the sūrah's confrontation with the strongest social force in its audience's world.

**What the Verse Leaves Unanswered**

Shuʿayb does not respond to the threats, the dismissal or the claim that he is of no account. He answers only the confession. The omission is a deliberate choice about what deserves reply. An insult is not an argument and does not require one. A statement about what restrains a people is information about their actual beliefs, and information about beliefs can be addressed. The sūrah shows the same selectivity elsewhere: Hūd ignored the charge of foolishness and answered the demand for a sign. The verse is therefore also a lesson in argument. A person who answers every line of an abusive speech has accepted the terms of the abuse. A person who answers only the revealing line has kept the exchange where it matters.

**Why the Verse Is Short**

The verse contains one question and one statement, and its brevity contrasts with the length of Shuʿayb's previous speech. The contrast is instructive. The long speech contained proof, integrity, intention and dependence, and none of it was engaged. The short reply takes a single fact the people volunteered and asks what it means. The sūrah's arrangement suggests that the shorter form is the more effective one at this stage of an exchange. Once a people have stopped listening to arguments, further argument does not help; a question about their own stated position may. The verse is therefore the sūrah's model of how to speak to a hearer who has closed, and the model is to use what he has said rather than what he should hear.

****The Verse as a Question About Priorities****

Stripped of its context, the verse asks a question that applies well beyond Midian. What does a person actually defer to? Not what he says he defers to, but what actually stops him from doing a thing. The people's answer was a clan. The verse asks them to notice that the answer has been given, and to compare it with the answer they would give if asked directly. The sūrah's method here is diagnostic rather than accusatory. It does not tell the people they are idolaters or disbelievers. It asks them to look at what restrains them and to say whether that is God. The question is unanswerable in the affirmative, and the unanswerability is the verse's whole argument. The verse therefore turns an insult into evidence, and the evidence is about the people rather than about the prophet they were abusing.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:93

> O my people! Persist in your ways, for I ˹too˺ will persist in mine. You will soon come to know who will be visited by a humiliating torment and is a liar! And watch! I too am watching with you!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Command to Continue**

The verse opens with an instruction that appears, on its face, to be the opposite of a command. Shuʿayb tells his people to act according to their position — to keep doing what they are doing. The commentators note that the same words are taught to this Prophet in other places (6:135; 39:39) and that they serve as both warning and threat. The form is not permission. It is the language of a person who has finished arguing and is stating that both sides will now continue on their current courses. The verse therefore marks the end of the dialogue. Nothing further is offered, nothing further is asked. The sūrah has shown this moment in every narrative, and here it is stated in the most explicit terms available.

**"Who Will Be Visited by a Humiliating Torment"**

The second clause states the two questions the outcome will settle, and the first concerns humiliation. The word describes a punishment that disgraces as well as harms. The sūrah has used the same vocabulary in its thirty-ninth verse, where the punishment that disgraces was said to be coming, and the earlier use establishes that this is not a new threat but the sūrah's standing one. The verse's choice of a humiliating punishment rather than merely a painful one is significant for this audience specifically. Midian's people have been assessing status throughout the exchange — Shuʿayb is weak, lowly, of no account. The verse answers in their own terms: the outcome will settle who is disgraced.

**"And Who Is a Liar"**

The second question is the more personal of the two. The people have called Shuʿayb a man of forbearance and sound judgement in mockery, and the commentators note that their earlier taunts included calling him bewitched and a liar (26:185–186). The verse takes up the charge without repeating it and states that the outcome will determine which side lied. The formulation is economical. Shuʿayb does not defend himself or produce further evidence. He refers the matter to an event both sides expect. The sūrah's method here is consistent with its treatment of the whole cycle: the truth of a messenger's claim is not established by argument alone but by the arrival of what he said would arrive.

**"Watch — I Too Am Watching"**

The final instruction is shared, and the sharing is the verse's most striking feature. Shuʿayb does not tell his people to watch while he waits. He says watch, and adds that he is watching with them. The commentators note that the same instruction is given to this Prophet in several places (9:52; 10:20, 102; 32:30). The phrase places both parties in the same posture, looking at the same expected event, and the placement removes any suggestion that the messenger holds a private advantage. The sūrah has used the same device with Ṣāliḥ, who told his people to enjoy themselves for three days. Here the waiting is indefinite and mutual, and the mutuality is what makes the verse a challenge rather than a prediction.

**The Verse and Its Parallel Instructions**

The commentators' note that this Prophet receives similar instructions connects the verse to the sūrah's Mekkan situation. Shuʿayb's words are not only historical. They are a model of what a messenger says when his people have closed, and the model is given to this Prophet elsewhere in the Qurʾān. The verse therefore functions on two levels at once. Inside the narrative it closes a dialogue; addressed to the listeners it describes their own position. The sūrah has used this double function throughout, most explicitly in verse 49 and in the closing clause of verse 83. The verse belongs to that pattern, and its placement at the end of the final narrative gives the pattern its last and clearest instance.

**What the Verse Ends**

Eleven verses of dialogue have now concluded, and the verse marks the boundary. Everything before it is speech; everything after it is event. The sūrah's arrangement across all six narratives has been the same — argument, refusal, closing statement, outcome — and this verse is the closing statement in its most developed form. A reader who has followed the cycle can recognise the shape and knows what follows. The verse's function is therefore partly structural: it tells the reader that the exchange is over and that the next verse will not contain speech. The shift is abrupt, and the abruptness is the sūrah's way of marking the difference between what people say and what happens.

****Why the Verse Does Not Argue****

The verse abandons argument, and the abandonment is the sūrah's point rather than a failure. Eleven verses of exchange have produced a proof, an integrity claim, a statement of limits, four historical examples, a diagnosis of resentment and an invitation. None of them was engaged. The verse therefore stops producing material and states the two positions as they now stand. The sūrah has shown this transition in every narrative, and its consistency makes it a rule: there is a point in an exchange after which more speech does not help, and a messenger who recognises the point has done his work. The verse is the recognition, and the mutual watching it proposes is what remains when persuasion has been exhausted. The verse therefore closes the dialogue by declining to continue it, and the refusal is the last thing a messenger in this sūrah is shown saying. The dialogue is over, and both sides are now waiting on the same event.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:94

> When Our command came, We saved Shu’aib and those who believed with him by a mercy from Us. And the ˹mighty˺ blast overtook the wrongdoers, so they fell lifeless in their homes

**Expanded Commentary**

**"When Our Command Came"**

The verse opens with the sūrah's fixed formula, and this is its fifth and final use in the narrative cycle. The command came to Nūḥ's people through the oven, to ʿĀd through the wind, to Thamūd through the blast, to Lūṭ's towns through the overturning, and now to Midian. The repetition is the sūrah's structural signature: whatever the punishment's form, its timing is described identically. The commentators note that the wording here is identical to the wording used for Thamūd, and the identity is deliberate. A reader who has followed the cycle hears the formula and knows that the dialogue has ended and the decree has arrived. The verse needs no transition because the formula is the transition.

**Saved "By a Mercy From Us"**

The rescue is attributed to mercy, and the attribution is the verse's central claim. The commentators note the identical wording in the Ṣāliḥ narrative, where the believers were saved by a mercy and spared the disgrace of that Day. The phrase establishes that deliverance is not a reward calculated from merit. It is a mercy — something given, not earned. The sūrah has made the same claim throughout, most explicitly in its doctrinal section, where God's giving was described as distributed according to His will rather than according to deserving. The verse applies the claim to a rescue, and the application is significant: the people saved are not described as having earned survival. They are described as having received it.

**The Blast**
