The punishment is the same one that destroyed Thamūd, and the commentators link the two explicitly. The word denotes a mighty cry or blast, and its recurrence across two narratives separated by centuries is part of the sūrah's argument. The peoples differ in their sins — Thamūd in ingratitude and violence, Midian in commercial fraud — and the punishment is the same. The sūrah is therefore not matching punishment to crime in a proportional way. It is showing that the outcome of settled refusal is a settled thing, whatever the specific refusal was. The verse's use of the same instrument twice within thirty verses makes the point without commentary.

**"Morning Found Them Lifeless in Their Homes"**

The final clause is word-identical to the clause describing Thamūd's end, and the identity is the verse's most remarkable feature. The same phrase — morning found them lying lifeless in their homes — describes two different peoples destroyed in different centuries for different sins. The sūrah has repeated the sentence deliberately, and the repetition does specific work. It tells the reader that the end of a destroyed nation has a fixed appearance: a morning, a place of residence, and bodies that did not move. The verse is therefore not narrating an event so much as identifying a pattern, and the pattern is what the following verse will name.

**The Identical Wording With Thamūd**

The commentators point to the correspondence directly, noting that the coming of the command, the role of mercy in salvation, the blast seizing the wrongdoers, and the morning finding them lifeless are all described in identical terms in the two accounts. The correspondence is the sūrah's method of making an argument through repetition rather than through statement. A reader who notices the identity draws the conclusion himself: these are not six separate events but one event occurring six times. The sūrah could have stated this in the narrator's voice and chose not to. The choice is characteristic. The chapter's doctrinal section states its claims explicitly; the narrative section demonstrates them by recurrence.

**Why the Sūrah Repeats Itself**

The repetition serves the chapter's purpose, which is to make its Mekkan audience recognise a pattern they are inside. A single narrative can be dismissed as a story about a distant people. Six narratives with identical structure, identical formulae and identical endings cannot be dismissed that way, because the identity is the point. The verse is the fifth instance of the formula and the second of the exact sentence, and by the time a reader reaches it the pattern is unmistakable. The sūrah has therefore built its argument cumulatively rather than deductively. It does not prove that settled refusal ends in destruction; it shows the same ending arriving five times, in the same words.

****What the Verse Omits About the Rescue****

The rescue is described in seven words and the punishment in eleven. The sūrah does not say how many believed, where they went, what they took with them, or how they were informed. The commentators supply none of this either. The omission is characteristic of the sūrah's treatment of deliverance throughout: the saved are named as a group and their fate is stated, while the destroyed are described at length. The asymmetry is not carelessness. It reflects the sūrah's priorities, which are with the exchange that preceded the outcome rather than with the outcome's logistics. A reader is given what he needs to draw the lesson and nothing that would distract him from it.

****The Verse and the Sūrah's Fifth Destruction****

This is the fifth destruction the sūrah has narrated, and the verse's use of the standing formula marks it as such. The reader who has followed the cycle has now seen the formula five times and can predict what follows it. The predictability is the sūrah's achievement rather than its weakness. By the fifth instance the narrative has become a demonstration: a pattern is being shown to recur, and each recurrence strengthens the inference that the pattern is not accidental. The verse therefore does less narrative work than the first destruction did and more argumentative work. It is evidence in a case the sūrah has been building since verse 25.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:95

> as if they had never lived there. So away with Midian as it was with Thamûd

**Expanded Commentary**

**"As Though They Had Never Lived There"**

The phrase completes the sentence begun in the previous verse, and it is word-identical to the phrase describing Thamūd's end. The commentators note the correspondence, and the identity is the verse's first claim. A town destroyed is not merely empty; it is described as though its inhabitants had never been in it. The sūrah has used the same formulation twice now, and the doubling establishes it as the sūrah's standard description of a destroyed nation's aftermath. The phrase is not about physical erasure. The homes are standing — the previous verse says the bodies were found in them. What is erased is the fact of having lived there. The verse is therefore describing a loss more complete than death, and describing it in the same words both times.

**"Away With Midian, As It Was With Thamūd"**

The refrain returns for the third and final time, and this instance differs from the previous two. ʿĀd in verse 60 and Thamūd in verse 68 were each dismissed with the same formula. Midian is dismissed with the formula plus a comparison. The sūrah has not previously linked two destroyed peoples explicitly; here it does, and the commentators give the reason: their fates were so similar. The comparison is the sūrah's own conclusion, delivered in the narrator's voice for the first time in the cycle. A reader who has noticed the identical wording in the previous verse is being told that the noticing was correct, and the telling is the sūrah's only direct comment on its narrative method.

**The Comparison Named Explicitly**

The verse names Thamūd rather than describing them, and the naming assumes the reader remembers. Thamūd were destroyed thirty verses earlier, in an account that used the same formula for the command, the same word for the blast, and the same sentence for the morning. The verse's comparison is therefore not an analogy drawn between two unlike cases. It is an identification of two accounts the sūrah has deliberately made alike. The effect on a reader is to convert the narratives from a series into a set. Six stories become one pattern with six instances, and the pattern is what the sūrah's remaining verses will draw consequences from.

**The Last Use of the Refrain**

The refrain's final appearance is worth marking. It began with ʿĀd in verse 60, repeated for Thamūd in verse 68, and closes here with Midian. Three peoples, one formula, one conclusion. Across the cycle it has done one job: to state that distance from God's mercy is the substance of the punishment. The verse's addition of the comparison gives the refrain its cumulative force.  The sūrah does not explain the formula or defend it. It repeats it until the repetition is the argument, and then stops. The stopping is significant: after this verse the narratives end, and the sūrah turns to what the narratives were for.

**What the Cycle Has Established**

Five destructions have now been narrated in full, and the verse closes the sequence. What the cycle has established is not that God destroys nations — the sūrah's audience already believed that. It has established that the destructions follow an identical sequence, that the sequence begins with a messenger's warning and a people's settled refusal, and that the outcome is described in the same words every time. The verse's comparison makes the identity explicit, and the explicitness is the cycle's conclusion. A reader is left holding a pattern rather than a list, and the pattern is what the sūrah will apply to its audience in the verses that follow.

**The Verse as the Close of the Narratives**

Structurally this verse ends the longest section of the sūrah. Sixty verses of narrative close with a refrain and a comparison, and the following verses shift register entirely. The sūrah's arrangement is deliberate: the material is presented as story, the story is shown to repeat, and the repetition is then applied. The verse is the hinge, and its brevity is appropriate to a hinge. Two clauses, one comparison, no elaboration. The sūrah has spent sixty verses earning the right to be this short. A reader who has followed the accounts does not need the pattern explained; he needs it named, and the verse names it.

****What the Comparison Asks the Reader to Do****

The verse's comparison is an instruction disguised as a statement. By saying that Midian's end was like Thamūd's, the sūrah asks the reader to hold two accounts together and notice what they share. The accounts are thirty verses apart, and holding them together requires an act of attention the sūrah has been training throughout. The comparison is therefore not a summary for a reader who has lost the thread. It is a reward for one who has kept it, and it converts the reader's noticing into the sūrah's conclusion. The method is characteristic: the chapter states its doctrines explicitly at the start and then requires the reader to derive them again from the narratives.

****Why the Verse Is the Cycle's Conclusion****

The cycle could have ended with the previous verse, which already contained the formula and the rescue. This verse adds the erasure and the comparison, and both additions are conclusions rather than narrative. The erasure generalises the outcome: it is not only that they died, but that their having lived there was undone. The comparison generalises the case: it is not only that Midian ended, but that Midian ended as Thamūd ended. Together the two clauses move the account from event to pattern, and the pattern is what the sūrah needs in order to turn to its audience. The verse is therefore a hinge written as a curse, and its brevity is the brevity of something that has been earned.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:96

> Indeed, We sent Moses with Our signs and compelling proof

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sixth Messenger, Differently Told**

The final narrative opens without the formula the previous five used. There is no *and to X We sent their brother Y*, no address to a people, no demand for exclusive worship. The verse is a single clause, and it introduces Mūsā rather than a community. The change of form signals a change of function. The five previous narratives were complete accounts with dialogue, refusal and destruction. This one is a summary, and the sūrah's reason for the compression becomes clear in the verses that follow: the Mūsā account is not here to add a sixth story but to close the cycle and hand over to the concluding section. The verse therefore reads as an introduction to a conclusion rather than as the start of a narrative.

**"Our Signs"**

The plural is significant. Mūsā is described as coming with signs rather than with a sign, and the commentators identify them as the miracles he performed, referred to elsewhere as clear proofs (7:105; 29:39). The sūrah has used the same phrase for the other messengers — Hūd and Ṣāliḥ both spoke of a clear proof from their Lord — and its application here in the plural marks the scale of Mūsā's case. The verse does not enumerate the signs or describe any of them, and the omission is deliberate. A reader familiar with the accounts elsewhere in the Qurʾān supplies the list; a reader who is not still receives the essential fact, which is that the messenger came equipped. The sūrah's economy here is a measure of how familiar the Mūsā narrative already was to its audience.

**"A Compelling Proof"**

The second term is stronger than the first, and the commentators note that some take it to refer specifically to Mūsā's staff. The word denotes authority — something that establishes a claim and cannot be argued away. The verse pairs a plural with a singular: signs, which can be witnessed, and an authority, which must be conceded. The pairing describes the two ways a prophet's case is made. A sign is shown; an authority is established. The sūrah has described the same combination throughout its narratives, where the messengers both performed and argued. The verse compresses both into three words and moves on, which tells the reader that the evidence is not the issue. The issue, as the next verse states, is what was done about it.

**Why the Mūsā Account Is Short**

The sūrah devotes four verses to Mūsā where it gave eleven to Shuʿayb and twenty-four to Nūḥ. The compression is not a judgement on Mūsā's importance — he is the most frequently narrated prophet in the Qurʾān. It is a decision about what this sūrah needs from him. The cycle has established its pattern five times, and a sixth full account would repeat what has already been demonstrated. What the Mūsā account adds is different: a case in which the destroyed party had a ruler, and in which the followers' allegiance to that ruler is named as the operative fact. The verse therefore introduces the account at exactly the length its function requires, and the function is stated in the following three verses.

**The Verse as a Bridge to the Coda**

Structurally this verse opens the sūrah's transition. The narratives end at verse 99 and the concluding section begins at verse 100, where the accounts are gathered and described as news of the towns. The Mūsā account sits between the two, and its brevity is what allows it to serve as a bridge. A full narrative would have required its own dialogue and outcome and would have delayed the transition. A single verse would not have carried the point about Pharaoh's command. Four verses is the length that makes the bridge work, and the verse's opening clause is the first plank of it. A reader moving from the destroyed towns to the sūrah's final instructions passes through this account.

**What the Verse Assumes the Reader Knows**

The verse names Mūsā without introduction, mentions signs without describing them, and refers to a proof without specifying it. Every element assumes prior knowledge, and the assumption is safe. The Mūsā narrative is told at length in al-Aʿrāf, in Ṭāhā, in al-Shuʿarāʾ and in al-Qaṣaṣ, and the sūrah of Hūd was revealed after al-Aʿrāf. The verse therefore functions as a reference rather than as a narration, and the reference does specific work. It tells the reader that the pattern established by the five previous accounts is not limited to the Arabian prophets. The greatest of the messengers faced the same structure: signs given, authority established, command refused. The verse asserts the identity by not needing to demonstrate it.

****The Verse and the Sūrah's Economy at the End****

The compression of this account is worth a final word. The sūrah had every reason to expand it: Mūsā is the most frequently narrated prophet in the Qurʾān, and the confrontation with Pharaoh is told at length in four other sūrahs. The decision to give it four verses is therefore a decision about this chapter's needs rather than about Mūsā's standing. The cycle has demonstrated its pattern five times, and a sixth demonstration would add nothing. What the Mūsā account adds is a ruler, a following, and an outcome that extends past this world. The verse introduces exactly those elements and no others, and the precision of the introduction is a measure of how clearly the sūrah knows what it still needs to say before it can close. The verse therefore does as much as the account requires and no more, and the restraint is what allows the chapter to close on time.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:97

> to Pharaoh and his chiefs, but they followed the command of Pharaoh, and Pharaoh’s command was not well guided

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Audiences Named**

The verse identifies the recipients of the mission, and the identification is precise. Mūsā was sent to Pharaoh and his chiefs — not to Egypt, not to the people generally, but to a ruler and the group around him. The sūrah has used the same description elsewhere for the elite who answer a prophet's call with mockery, and the recurrence is meaningful. In every previous narrative the decisive refusal came from the notables, and here the sūrah names them from the outset. The verse is therefore locating responsibility where the sūrah has consistently located it: not in a population but in the few who decide for it. The choice of audience determines everything that follows, and the verse states the choice before stating the outcome.

**"They Followed the Command of Pharaoh"**

The clause is the verse's central claim, and its wording is exact. The people are not described as believing Pharaoh, agreeing with him, or being deceived by him. They are described as following his command. The sūrah is making a claim about obedience rather than about conviction. A person who follows a command has not necessarily been persuaded; he has complied. The distinction is what makes the verse an accusation rather than an explanation. The sūrah has described the same dynamic in ʿĀd, whose notables dismissed Hūd, and in Thamūd, whose elite called Ṣāliḥ a liar. Here the dynamic is stated in its purest form: a command was issued, and it was followed. The verse does not soften the following into confusion or ignorance.

**"Pharaoh's Command Was Not Well Guided"**

The judgement on the command is given in four words, and the sūrah does not argue for it. The commentators note that the phrase means the command lacked guidance — it did not lead anywhere sound. The verse's economy is significant. A reader might expect a description of what Pharaoh commanded, or a refutation of it. What is given is a verdict. The sūrah has earned the right to be this brief: five narratives have just demonstrated what happens when a people follow a direction that is not guided, and the demonstration supplies the argument the verdict omits. The verse therefore states a conclusion and relies on the reader's memory of the previous sixty verses to supply the reasoning.

**The Verse's Judgement in Four Words**

The phrase describing Pharaoh's command is worth examining for what it does not say. It does not call him a liar, a tyrant or a disbeliever, though all three are established elsewhere. It says his command was not guided, which is a statement about direction rather than about character. The choice is deliberate and it sharpens the accusation against the followers. A person who follows a liar can claim he was deceived. A person who follows an unguided command has followed something that gave no indication of leading anywhere, and the following is therefore his own. The verse is therefore constructing its case carefully, and the construction places the weight where the sūrah wants it.

**Why the Verse Blames the Followers**

The verse's grammar assigns the action to the followers and the deficiency to the command, and the assignment is the point. Pharaoh's command is described, not condemned at length; the following is stated as an act the people performed. The sūrah has made the same distribution throughout its narratives, where the destroyed are always described as having done something rather than as having been acted upon. It follows that consistent with the chapter's larger claim, stated explicitly five verses later, that God did not wrong them but they wronged themselves. The claim is prepared here in a single clause: the people followed, and what they followed was unsound. Both halves of the sentence are theirs.

**The Verse and the Sūrah's Account of Leadership**

Read as a statement about authority, the verse makes a claim that would have been directly provocative. It says that a ruler's command does not carry moral weight by virtue of being his, and that following it does not transfer responsibility to him. The sūrah's original audience lived under precisely such an authority, and the tribal structure described in the Shuʿayb narrative — where a clan's protection governed a man's safety — was the same structure that governed religious adherence. The effect is that not only about Egypt. It is about the assumption that a person's allegiance is settled by his group's decision, and the sūrah's refusal of that assumption is what makes the following verses about Pharaoh's fate relevant to anyone who has ever followed a leader.

****The Verse and the Sūrah's Mekkan Audience****

The verse's claim about following a command would have been heard in a specific social setting. The audience lived in a tribal structure in which a man's religious adherence was largely settled by his group, and the structure is described explicitly in the Shuʿayb narrative — a clan protected its members and expected their conformity. The verse confronts that structure by naming the following as an act the followers performed. The commentators' note that the same description is used for the elite elsewhere in the Qurʾān strengthens the point: the sūrah consistently locates the decisive refusal in the few who decide for the many, and consistently holds the many responsible for accepting the decision. This means that not describing Egypt. It is describing the mechanism by which any society refuses a messenger, and the audience recognised the mechanism. The result is that the sūrah's sharpest statement about obedience, and it is made in the plainest available words. The verse therefore locates the decisive refusal in the people who followed rather than in the man who commanded, and the locating is the chapter's sharpest statement about obedience.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:98

> He will be before his people on the Day of Judgment and will lead them into the Fire. What an evil place to be led into

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Leader in Front**

The verse takes the image the previous verse established and extends it past death. Pharaoh went before his people in this world, issuing commands they followed; the verse says he will go before them on the Day of Judgement as well. The position is identical and the destination has changed. The sūrah is making a claim about the continuity of leadership: the man who led a people somewhere in this life does not step aside when the life ends. The verse's wording is precise — he will be *before* them, in front, in the position of one who is followed. The reader is being shown that the relation does not dissolve at death, and that the followers' excuse of having been led will not be available to them, because the leading continues.

**"He Will Lead Them Into the Fire"**

The second clause states the destination, and the verb is the same one used for guiding or conducting a group. The commentators connect the image to the general principle that every people will be called on that Day together with the one they followed. The verse is therefore not describing a punishment inflicted on Pharaoh alone; it is describing him performing the same function he performed in the world, with a different end point. The sūrah's use of the leadership vocabulary is exact and its effect is severe. Pharaoh is not being dragged into the Fire; he is leading people into it. The verse therefore completes the accusation of the previous verse by showing that the following has consequences for the followers, not only for the one who gave the command.

**The Image of the Watering Place**

The word used for the place they are led to is one ordinarily associated with bringing animals to water, and the commentators note that the association intensifies the description. A watering place is where a thirsty creature is taken to be relieved. The verse applies the word to the Fire, and the application is a reversal: the group is led, as a herd is led, to the one place that will not relieve anything. The sūrah uses concrete imagery of this kind sparingly, and its use here is effective because the image is familiar. A reader knows what it is to be led to water and knows what the leading promises. The verse takes that knowledge and inverts it in four words.

**"What an Evil Place to Be Led Into"**

The exclamation closes the verse, and its form is worth noting. It is not a statement about Pharaoh or about the people; it is a statement about the place. The sūrah has used the same exclamatory form elsewhere for a gift that is not a gift, and the form marks a judgement delivered as an observation. The effect is to move the reader from the narrative to an assessment. The verse does not tell the reader what to feel about Pharaoh's fate; it comments on the destination, and the comment invites the reader to supply the rest. The sūrah's method throughout has been to state facts and let the judgement emerge. Here the judgement is stated, but as an exclamation rather than as an argument, which leaves the reasoning to the reader.

**The Verse and Every Day of Gathering**

The verse locates the scene on the Day of Judgement, and the location matters. The previous narratives ended with destruction in this world — a flood, a wind, a blast, an overturning. This one ends with a scene in the next. The sūrah is therefore adding a dimension the earlier accounts did not supply, and the addition prepares the section that follows, which describes the Day at length. The verse is the hinge between the two: it takes a story about a destroyed ruler and shows that his destruction was not the end of his case. A reader who has followed five narratives ending in this-world punishment is told that a sixth has a further stage, and the further stage is what verses 103 to 108 will describe.

**What the Verse Adds to the Cycle**

The Mūsā account contributes one thing the other five did not: a named leader whose followers are held responsible for following him. ʿĀd's notables are described collectively; Thamūd's elite are a group; Midian's chiefs are not distinguished. Pharaoh is an individual, and the verse makes him a continuing agent after death. The addition gives the cycle its sharpest case against the excuse of obedience. A person who says he did what his leader told him is told that the leader is still in front, still leading, and that the destination is now visible to both. The verse therefore closes the narrative section with the strongest statement of individual responsibility the sūrah makes.

****Why the Leadership Image Is the Verse's Choice****

The sūrah could have described Pharaoh's punishment directly — drowning, the Fire, the curse. It chooses instead an image of leadership continuing past death, and the choice is the verse's argument. A reader who has followed the previous verse knows that the people's defence would be that they were led. The verse removes the defence by showing the leading intact on the Day. The image therefore does more work than a description of torment would, because it addresses the excuse rather than the suffering. The sūrah has used the same method throughout, where the punishment is described in terms that answer the specific claim the punished people made. Pharaoh's people claimed to have followed; the verse shows them still following.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:99

> They were followed by a curse in this ˹life˺ and ˹will receive another˺ on the Day of Judgment. What an evil gift to receive

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Curse in This Life**

The verse describes a pursuit, and the word used is the one the sūrah used for the peoples of ʿĀd and Thamūd: they were followed by a curse in this world, as they will be on the Day. The commentators take the curse here either generally or specifically as the drowning in the sea. Either reading preserves the verse's structure, which is a curse in two instalments. The first belongs to this life and the sūrah has already narrated it — Pharaoh and his chiefs were destroyed. The verse therefore does not describe a new punishment; it names what has already happened as a curse, and the naming is significant. A military defeat or a drowning is an event. A curse is a relation, and the verse converts the event into one.

**And Another on the Day**

The second instalment is reserved, and the reservation is the verse's distinctive claim. The previous narratives ended with destruction and did not describe a sequel; the Lūṭ and Midian accounts close with the erasure of the towns. This verse states that the punishment has a further portion held for the Day of Judgement. The sūrah has been building toward this throughout, and the building becomes explicit in the verses that follow, which describe the gathering, the two groups, and the two abodes. The verse is therefore the point at which the sūrah's horizon extends past this world. A reader who has followed five narratives ending in destruction is told that destruction is not the whole account, and the telling prepares the final section.

**"What an Evil Gift"**

The exclamation names the curse as a gift, and the naming is the verse's sharpest device. The word translated here as gift is one ordinarily used for something bestowed, and its application to a curse is deliberate irony. The sūrah uses the same form two verses earlier for the place the people are led to, and the repetition establishes a pattern: the vocabulary of bestowal applied to punishment. The effect is to invert the expectation attached to the word. A gift is given by someone who intends good; this gift is given by the consequence of a person's own acts. The verse therefore makes a claim about the shape of the punishment — it arrives as something bestowed, and the bestowing is what the person's conduct produced.

**The Verse's Irony**

The irony is not decorative. It makes a specific point about how punishment appears to the one who receives it. A person destroyed in this life experiences the event as something that happens to him. The verse describes the same event as something given, and the description shifts the agency. The sūrah has made the same shift explicitly in the previous verses, where the claim was that God did not wrong the destroyed peoples but they wronged themselves. The ironic use of the word *gift* enacts that claim in a single word. What arrives as a curse was sent by nobody; it was earned, and the earning is what makes it a gift rather than an assault. The verse therefore completes the cycle's argument in the vocabulary of bestowal.

**The Last Destruction Narrated**

This is the final verse of the sūrah's narrative section, and its position gives it a summarising function. Six accounts have been told, and the last one differs from the others in two ways: it concerns a ruler and his chiefs rather than a town, and it extends past this world. The verse's two instalments gather both differences. The curse in this life matches the pattern of the previous five; the curse on the Day exceeds it. The sūrah is therefore closing its narrative section by opening a new horizon, and the opening is what the following verses will fill. A reader arrives at verse 100 having been told that the accounts are not finished, and the following section explains what remains.

**Why the Mūsā Account Ends Here**

The account could have continued — the Qurʾān narrates the confrontation, the magicians, the plagues and the crossing at length elsewhere. The sūrah stops after four verses, and the stopping is a decision about function. What the account was needed for has been delivered: a leader who led, followers who followed, and a punishment in two instalments. The sūrah does not need the rest, and including it would have delayed the transition to the concluding section. The verse therefore marks the point where narration gives way to instruction, and the following verses make the shift explicit by gathering the accounts and describing them as news. The Mūsā account is the last thing the reader is shown before the sūrah turns to address him directly.

****The Two Instalments as the Sūrah's Widest Claim****

The verse's structure — a curse here and a curse there — makes the largest claim the sūrah makes about punishment. Every previous account ended inside history. This one states that the account is not finished when the history ends. The claim widens the chapter's horizon from the observable to the reserved, and the widening is what allows the following verses to describe the Day at length. A reader who has followed five destructions ending in flood, wind, blast and stones has been reading about events he could in principle verify. The verse tells him that a further portion exists which he cannot verify, and the telling prepares him for a section that will describe what cannot be seen. The verse therefore widens the chapter's horizon at the exact point where the narratives end, and the widening is what the following section fills.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:100

> These are accounts, We relate to you ˹O Prophet˺, of the ˹destroyed˺ cities. Some are still standing ˹barren˺, while others have been mowed down

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Narratives Gathered**

The verse steps back from the stories and describes them as a set. The sūrah has just finished six accounts spanning seven hundred verses of narrative time, and this verse gathers them into a single reference: accounts of towns. The shift in register is marked and deliberate. The reader has been inside the dialogues, hearing the messengers and their opponents; now he is placed outside them, looking at the collection. The verse is therefore the sūrah's own summary of its longest section, and the summary is addressed to the Prophet rather than to the audience. The address matters: the accounts are being characterised as something given to him, which connects this verse to the chapter's later statement that the stories were related to make his heart firm.

**Why the Verse Addresses the Prophet**

The sūrah could have summarised its narratives in the narrator's voice without naming a recipient. It names one, and the naming does two things. First, it identifies the accounts as revelation — news brought to a man who had not read them anywhere. Second, it locates the accounts' first function in the Prophet's own situation rather than in the audience's instruction. The sūrah's fortieth-ninth verse made the same point about the unseen accounts, and the recurrence establishes a principle: the stories are given to sustain a messenger before they are given to warn a people. The verse therefore corrects a reading that treats the narratives as mere illustration. They are sustenance first, and instruction follows.

**"Some Are Still Standing"**

The first category describes towns whose structures remain. The commentators note that the phrase denotes places still visible but emptied of inhabitants — walls standing, fields untended, homes without residents. The description is archaeological in a precise sense: it describes what a traveller on the Arabian trade routes would actually have seen. The sūrah's willingness to make a claim about visible remains is significant. It is not describing a hidden or unverifiable past; it is pointing at ruins its audience could visit. The verse therefore grounds the narratives in observable fact, and the grounding is what makes the following verses' warning available. A people whose destroyed towns are still standing cannot treat the accounts as legend.

**"Others Have Been Mowed Down"**

The second category uses an agricultural image, and the image is the verse's most striking word. The commentators take it to describe towns cut off completely, as a crop is cut at the stalk. The contrast with the first category is exact: some remains are visible, others are gone without trace. The sūrah is therefore describing two kinds of erasure, and the difference between them is not explained. Why some towns left ruins and others left nothing is not stated, and the omission leaves the reader with a range. The verse's effect is to establish that destruction in this sūrah is total in either form — a standing ruin testifies to what was removed, and an erased site removes even the testimony.

**What the Two Categories Do Together**

The pairing makes a claim about evidence. One category supplies visible proof; the other supplies the absence of proof. A reader might expect the sūrah to rely on the first and ignore the second. It presents both, and the presentation is honest about the limits of what can be shown. Some destroyed towns can be visited; others cannot be located. The sūrah does not claim that all its accounts are archaeologically verifiable, and it does not need to. The verse is therefore establishing the ground on which the following verses will argue: not that every ruin can be found, but that the pattern is documented in cases that can be and in cases that cannot. The combination is stronger than either half alone.

**The Verse as the Section's Title**

Structurally this verse functions as a heading for what preceded and an opening for what follows. It closes the narratives by naming them, and it opens the concluding section by stating what they are. The sūrah's remaining twenty-three verses are instruction, warning and description of the Day, and all of them depend on the accounts having been established. The verse is therefore the hinge of the chapter, and its brevity is appropriate. Two sentences: these are accounts of towns; some remain, some are gone. A reader who has followed the narratives needs nothing more. The verse supplies the frame and leaves the content to the sixty verses behind it and the twenty-three ahead.

****What the Verse Assumes About Its Audience****

The verse assumes that its audience knows the accounts, and the assumption is safe. The destroyed towns were on the trade routes the Makkans travelled, and the Qurʾān elsewhere invites its audience to pass by the remains and look. The verse's two categories therefore address people with direct experience of at least the first. A man who has walked past a ruined settlement and wondered about it is being told what happened there and why. The sūrah's willingness to make this assumption is what allows it to be brief. It does not need to argue that the towns existed or that they were destroyed; both are matters its audience could check. What the verse supplies is the interpretation, and the interpretation is what the following verses will develop. The verse therefore supplies both the evidence and its limit, and the honesty about the limit is what makes the evidence usable. The verse therefore supplies the evidence and its limit in a single sentence, and the pairing is what makes both usable.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:101

> We did not wrong them, rather they wronged themselves. The gods they invoked beside Allah were of no help at all when the command of your Lord came, and only contributed to their ruin

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Denial and an Assignment**

The verse opens with two clauses that do opposite work. The first denies that God wronged the destroyed peoples; the second assigns the wrong to them. The pairing is the sūrah's clearest statement of a claim it has been building since its opening verses. The denial is not defensive. It is a precise statement about the order of events: warning came first, refusal followed, and the outcome belonged to the refusal. The sūrah has narrated that order six times, and the verse states it as a principle. A reader who has followed the accounts has seen the sequence in operation; the verse tells him what the sequence means. The assignment is therefore not a verdict imposed from outside but a description of what the narratives showed.

**Why the Denial Is Necessary**

The claim that God did not wrong them answers a question the narratives inevitably raise. A reader who watches six nations destroyed may reasonably ask whether the destruction was proportionate, or whether the peoples had any chance. The verse answers both by reference to the sequence: they were warned, they had messengers, and they refused. The sūrah's insistence on this point is consistent with its treatment throughout, where God's action is never described as arbitrary. The verse therefore defends not God's power but God's justice, and the defence is made by pointing at the record. The narratives are the evidence, and the verse's denial is the conclusion drawn from them. A reader who disputes the denial must dispute the accounts.

**"Their Gods Were of No Help at All"**

The second half of the verse turns to the objects of worship, and the statement is absolute. The commentators note that these gods could not benefit their worshippers in this world, and that the case would be stronger still in the Hereafter. The verse's wording is emphatic: not a little help, no help at all. The sūrah is making a claim about the function of false gods at the moment they are most needed. A god who cannot help when the command arrives has not been shown to be weak; he has been shown not to be a god. The verse therefore uses the destructions as a test of divinity, and the test's result is stated in four words. The sūrah does not argue the point philosophically. It points at the record.

**"And Only Contributed to Their Ruin"**

The final clause goes further than the denial of help. The gods did not merely fail to prevent the ruin; they added to it. The commentators read this as the loss incurred by worshipping them — the time, the wealth, the allegiance, the refusal of the messengers' warning. The verse is therefore making a claim about the cost of false worship that is stronger than uselessness. A useless thing wastes nothing. A harmful thing costs something. The sūrah's formulation makes the second claim, and the claim is what gives the verse its force. A reader is being told that the worship of these gods was not a neutral error corrected by their destruction. It was an active contribution to the destruction, and the contribution is named.

**The Verse and the Sūrah's Doctrine of Responsibility**

The verse states the chapter's central claim about human agency in its plainest form. The sūrah has asserted it repeatedly — that God gives to whomever He wills, that no soul is charged beyond its capacity, that a person's outcome follows his conduct. Here the claim is compressed into two clauses and applied to six nations at once. The compression is significant because it removes any room for the excuse the previous verses discussed: that people followed a leader, or a custom, or a god. The verse assigns the wrong to the peoples themselves, collectively, and the assignment is what the following verse will generalise into a statement about how God deals with societies.

**What the Verse Leaves Unexplained**

The verse does not say why the gods contributed to ruin rather than merely failing to prevent it, and the omission is characteristic. The sūrah states consequences and leaves the mechanism to the reader. A person who worships something that cannot help him has spent his allegiance on nothing, and the spending is the mechanism. The verse does not spell this out because the narratives have shown it: the notables' allegiance to their customs, Thamūd's attachment to their carved homes, Midian's confidence in their trade. Each of these was a form of reliance that cost the people their response to the messenger. The verse names the cost, and the accounts supply the detail.

****The Verse as the Narratives' Verdict****

Six accounts have been told, and this verse is the verdict on all six. Its two claims — God did not wrong them, their gods did not help them — answer the two questions a reader is most likely to ask. The first question is about justice: was this deserved? The second is about the alternatives: did their gods not save them? The verse answers both in a single sentence and does not elaborate. The economy is earned by the narratives, which have supplied the evidence for both answers. A reader who has followed the accounts does not need the evidence repeated; he needs the conclusion stated. The verse states it, and the stating is what converts sixty verses of story into a case. The verse therefore settles the two questions the narratives raise, and settles them in the vocabulary the narratives themselves supplied.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:102

> Such is the ˹crushing˺ grip of your Lord when He seizes the societies entrenched in wrongdoing. Indeed, His grip is ˹terribly˺ painful and severe

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Six Cases to One Rule**

The verse converts the narratives into a general statement, and the conversion is the sūrah's method made explicit. What preceded were six accounts of particular peoples in particular places. This verse says: such is the grip. The demonstrative gathers all six into a single pattern and states it as a rule. The sūrah has been careful not to make this move earlier; it let the accounts accumulate and the reader draw the inference. The verse now confirms the inference in the narrator's voice, and the confirmation is what turns a series of stories into an argument. A reader who resisted the pattern through sixty verses is now told that the pattern is the point.

**"When He Seizes the Societies"**

The word for societies is significant. The verse does not say towns, peoples or nations; it uses a term denoting a settled community in its wrongful state. The commentators note the condition attached — God seizes them while they are doing wrong — and the condition is the verse's most important qualification. The grip is not arbitrary and not indiscriminate. It falls on a community in a specific condition, and the condition is stated. The sūrah elsewhere makes the same qualification explicit, when it states that God would not destroy towns unjustly while their people were reforming. The verse therefore describes a rule with a stated trigger, and the trigger is what makes the rule something a person can act on.

**Why the Qualification Matters**

The qualification answers the most serious objection to the narratives. A reader might conclude that destruction is a matter of timing — that God destroys nations when He decides to, and that the decision is inscrutable. The verse refuses that conclusion by naming the condition. A society entrenched in wrongdoing is seized; the same society reforming is not. The sūrah's later verse states the point directly, and this verse prepares it. The effect is to place the outcome within human reach. A community's fate is not fixed by decree but determined by its condition, and the condition can change. The verse is therefore not a threat so much as a statement of terms, and the terms are published.

**"His Grip Is Painful, Severe"**

The closing description uses two adjectives, and the commentators take them as intensifying one another. The grip is not merely strong; it is painful and severe. The sūrah's willingness to describe its own subject this bluntly is worth noting. It does not soften the description or frame it as a last resort. The accounts have shown what the grip looks like — a flood, a wind that leaves nothing standing, a blast that leaves bodies in their homes, an overturning with a rain of stones. The verse's two adjectives summarise all four without repeating any of them. The summary is therefore earned by the narratives rather than asserted, and a reader who has followed them knows what the adjectives refer to.

**The Verse as the Narratives' Conclusion**

Structurally this verse closes the sūrah's longest section, and it closes it with a rule rather than with a story. The choice is significant. A narrative section might end with its most dramatic account or with a moral. The sūrah ends with a statement about how God deals with societies, which is the thing the narratives were demonstrating. The verse therefore functions as the section's thesis, and its position immediately after the gathering verse makes the structure clear: the accounts are named, the responsibility is assigned, the rule is stated. Three verses do the work that sixty verses of narrative prepared, and the compression is possible only because the preparation was thorough.

**What the Verse Asks the Reader to Do**

The verse is addressed to a person living in a society. It states a rule about societies and their condition, and the rule includes a trigger that can be observed and altered. The verse does not tell the reader what to do; it tells him what the rule is and leaves the application to him. The sūrah's following verses will supply the instructions — be steadfast, do not incline toward the wrongdoers, establish prayer, be patient. This verse supplies the reason those instructions matter. A community's condition is what determines its outcome, and the condition is made of the conduct of the people in it. The verse therefore makes the reader's conduct a matter of public consequence rather than private concern.

****The Verse and the Sūrah's Doctrine of Terms****

The rule stated here has a condition, and the condition connects the verse to the sūrah's treatment of timing throughout. Every destruction in the narratives came at a stated term — the oven, the three days, the morning. None came early, and none came while the people were reforming. The verse's rule is therefore not a general threat but a description of a method already demonstrated six times. A reader who has followed the accounts knows that the method includes both a trigger and a delay, and that the delay is real. The verse gathers those features into a single statement, and the statement is what allows the chapter to move from narrative to instruction. The rule is known; what remains is what to do inside it.

****What the Verse Means for a Reader Inside a Society****

The verse addresses a person who is part of a society rather than an individual acting alone. The grip falls on societies, and the condition that averts it is a social condition. The verse is therefore making the reader's conduct a matter of collective consequence, and the collective dimension is what the following verses will develop into instructions. A person who reads this verse as a warning to himself alone has missed its scope. The sūrah is describing how communities are dealt with, and the community includes him. The claim is uncomfortable and it is stated plainly, and the plainness is what prepares the reader for the commands that follow four verses later.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:103

> Surely in this is a sign for those who fear the torment of the Hereafter. That is a Day for which humanity will be gathered and a Day ˹that will be˺ witnessed ˹by all˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sign for a Specific Reader**

The verse states that the accounts contain a sign, and it identifies who can read it. The sign is not available to everyone; it is available to those who fear the punishment of the Hereafter. The qualification is the verse's central claim. A reader who does not fear that punishment will look at six destroyed nations and see six historical events. A reader who fears it will see six warnings addressed to him. The verse is therefore making a statement about interpretation: the narratives do not carry their meaning on their surface, and the meaning is unlocked by a disposition rather than by analysis. The sūrah has shown the same dynamic throughout, where the notables saw the messengers and saw nothing.

**Why Fear Is the Condition**

The verse names fear rather than knowledge, intelligence or scholarship, and the choice is instructive. Knowledge of the accounts does not produce the sign; the sūrah's original audience knew them. What produces the sign is the recognition that the accounts might apply to the reader. That recognition is a form of fear, and it is what the verse requires. The sūrah has described the opposite condition repeatedly: a people who hear the warning and treat it as a story about someone else. The verse identifies the alternative and names it precisely. A reader is not asked to believe more or to know more. He is asked to be afraid in a specific way — afraid that the pattern includes him.

**"A Day for Which Humanity Will Be Gathered"**

The verse's second statement shifts from the narratives to the Day, and the shift is the sūrah's turn toward its final section. The commentators connect the gathering to the Day of Judgement, where all humanity is assembled to be judged, and they note the same description elsewhere in the Qurʾān (42:7; 64:9). The verse's wording is comprehensive: humanity, not a nation or a people. The sūrah has spent eighty verses on particular communities and their particular ends; here it states that all of them, and everyone else, will be gathered. The shift in scale is significant. The destructions were local and historical; the gathering is universal and future. The verse therefore extends the narratives' reach beyond history.

**"A Day That Will Be Witnessed"**

The second description of the Day adds a dimension the first does not contain. A gathering is an event; a witnessed gathering is an event with an audience. The commentators take the phrase to mean that all human beings from the beginning of creation to its end will be present, standing before God when He calls them to account. The verse is therefore describing a Day that is public in the fullest sense — not merely universal in its participants but observed by all of them. The sūrah's use of this description prepares the following verses, which describe the two groups and their abodes. A reader is being told that the outcome will not be private, and the publicity is part of what the verse means by witnessed.

**How the Two Descriptions Work Together**

The gathering and the witnessing are distinct, and their combination is deliberate. A person gathered is present; a person witnessed is seen. The verse requires both, and the requirement makes a claim about the Day's character. It is not a private reckoning in which each soul is assessed alone. It is a public assembly in which every soul is present and every other soul sees. The sūrah has described the same publicity in its earlier verses, where the record is kept and the deeds are known. The verse extends that description to its fullest form. A reader is being told that the outcome of his conduct will be known not only to God but to everyone assembled, and the knowledge is part of the reckoning.

**The Verse as the Section's Opening**

Structurally this verse opens the sūrah's final movement. The narratives have closed, the rule has been stated, and now the horizon extends to the Day. The verse makes the extension by connecting the two: the accounts are a sign, and the sign points at the gathering. The connection is what allows the sūrah to move from history to eschatology without a break. A reader who accepts the accounts as signs has already accepted that the pattern continues; the verse tells him where it continues to. The following verses will describe the Day's features, and the description will be intelligible only to a reader who has taken the accounts as warnings addressed to him.

****Why the Sign Requires Fear****

The verse's condition deserves a final word. A sign is normally thought of as evidence available to anyone who looks. The verse describes something different: a sign available only to a person in a particular state. The state is fear of the punishment of the Hereafter, and the fear is not a mood but a settled recognition that the accounts might apply to the reader. The sūrah has shown the opposite condition throughout its narratives — notables who saw the messengers and their proofs and saw nothing. The verse identifies what those people lacked, and the identification is not that they lacked intelligence or information. They lacked the fear that makes evidence visible. The verse therefore makes a claim about interpretation that the whole chapter supports. The verse therefore supplies the disposition the accounts require, and names it as fear rather than as knowledge. The verse therefore names the disposition that unlocks the accounts, and the naming is what allows the chapter to move from history to the Day without a break.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:104

> We only delay it for a fixed term

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shortest Verse in the Chapter**

The verse is four words in the translation and shorter in the original, and its brevity is deliberate. It follows a verse describing the gathering of all humanity and precedes a verse describing the Day's silence and the division into two groups. Between those two descriptions the sūrah inserts a single statement about timing. The placement gives the verse a function its length would not suggest: it answers the question the previous verse raises. A reader told that a Day is coming asks when, and the verse answers that the delay is bounded. The sūrah does not elaborate, does not give a duration, and does not explain the reason for the delay. It states that the delay has a limit and stops.

**"We Only Delay It"**

The verb is significant. The verse does not say the Day is distant or that it has been postponed indefinitely. It says it is being delayed, which implies an intention behind the delay and a point at which the delay ends. The commentators note that the fixed term means the time of the Day is already known to God. The verse is therefore making two claims at once: the Day is not imminent, and it is not indefinite. The combination answers two opposite errors. A person who thinks the Day will never come is told it is delayed rather than cancelled. A person who thinks it might come at any moment is told there is a term. The verse's economy allows both corrections in a single clause.

**Why the Delay Is Mentioned Here**

The verse's position is instructive. It comes immediately after the statement that the accounts are a sign for those who fear the punishment, and immediately before the description of the Day's silence. The placement makes the delay part of the warning rather than a qualification of it. A reader might take the delay as a reason to relax; the verse's position prevents that reading by surrounding it with descriptions of what is delayed. The sūrah has used the same technique throughout, where a statement of mercy is placed next to a statement of punishment so that neither can be isolated. The verse belongs to that pattern, and its brevity makes the surrounding verses more prominent rather than less.

**The Fixed Term as a Theological Claim**

The phrase makes a claim about divine action that the sūrah has asserted elsewhere. God's decisions have terms; the terms are set; the setting is not arbitrary. The sūrah has described the same structure in the narratives, where each destruction came at a specified time — the oven, the three days, the morning. The verse extends the structure to the Day itself. A reader who has followed the accounts knows that the terms were kept in every case, and the knowledge applies here. The verse is therefore not offering a reassurance about timing; it is offering a guarantee about reliability. The term is fixed, and the sūrah's record of fixed terms being met is the evidence that this one will be met.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

The verse does not state the term, and the omission is as significant as the statement. A reader is told that the delay is bounded without being told the bound. The sūrah's refusal to specify is consistent with its treatment of the unseen throughout: it reports what is needed for conduct and withholds what would serve only curiosity. A person who knew the term would calculate rather than prepare. A person who knows the term is fixed but unknown prepares. The verse therefore supplies exactly the information that makes a difference to conduct and withholds the information that would not. The economy is characteristic of the sūrah's method, and it is at its most concentrated here.

**The Verse and the Reader's Situation**

The verse addresses a person living inside the delay. He is not in the Day; he is in the period the Day is being held back for. The verse's function is to describe that period accurately: it is a delay, it is bounded, and its bound is set by someone other than the person living in it. The sūrah's following verses will describe what happens when the delay ends, and the description will be addressed to a person who is currently in it. The verse therefore establishes the reader's position before describing what lies beyond it. A reader who understands that he is in a delay rather than in an indefinite present reads the following verses differently, and the difference is what the verse is for.

****The Verse and the Sūrah's Fixed Terms****

The claim that the delay has a fixed term connects this verse to every destruction the chapter has narrated. In each case a term was stated and met: the oven burst when the command came, Thamūd was given three days, Lūṭ's people were told the morning was their appointment. The sūrah's record is therefore a record of terms kept. The verse applies that record to the Day, and the application is what makes the claim credible. A reader who has followed five accounts in which a stated term arrived exactly as stated has no reason to think the final term will behave differently. The verse is therefore not asking for faith in an unsupported claim. It is asking the reader to extend a pattern he has already verified five times. The verse therefore gives exactly as much as conduct requires, and the restraint is what makes it useful rather than merely interesting. The verse therefore states a bound without naming it, and the unnamed bound is what keeps the reader preparing rather than calculating.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:105

> When that Day arrives, no one will dare speak except with His permission. Some of them will be miserable, others joyful

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Day's First Feature: Silence**

The verse describes the Day's arrival, and the first thing it records is the absence of speech. The sūrah has just spent eighty verses on dialogue — messengers arguing, notables mocking, prophets pleading — and the Day it now describes begins with none of it. The silence is not incidental. It is the first feature named, and the naming makes a claim about the Day's character. Every argument the sūrah has narrated was conducted in speech; the Day on which those arguments are settled will not be. The verse therefore establishes a contrast that gives the narratives their weight. What could be said then cannot be said then, and the reader is being told which of the two moments he is currently in.

**"Except With His Permission"**

The qualification is the verse's central statement, and it establishes whose the Day is. Speech is not prohibited absolutely; it is permitted selectively. The commentators connect the claim to two other passages: that on the Day the Spirit and the angels will stand in rows and none will speak except one whom the Most Merciful permits and who speaks what is right (78:38), and that voices will be humbled so that nothing is heard but a murmur (20:108). The verse's wording is the most general of the three, and the generality is what makes it a statement about authority rather than about noise. The Day belongs to God in the sense that nothing is said in it without His leave.

**What the Silence Establishes**

The silence makes a claim about the preceding arguments. Every exchange the sūrah has narrated involved a person asserting something about himself — that he is generous, that his forefathers were right, that his clan protects him, that he has no need of what is offered. The Day described here removes the possibility of assertion. The verse is therefore not describing a quiet event; it is describing the end of self-presentation. A reader who has followed the notables' speeches through sixty verses understands what the silence costs them. The verse's two clauses together make the point: nobody speaks, and the assembled are divided. The division happens without anyone's input, and the silence is what makes it final.

**The Division Stated in Two Words**

The second clause divides the assembled into two groups, and the sūrah names them with single adjectives. The commentators note that these terms — the wretched and the felicitous — are among the most commonly used designations for the two groups in Islamic texts. The verse's choice of adjectives rather than nouns is worth noting. A noun would describe what a person is; an adjective describes how he fares. The sūrah is therefore not classifying people by identity but by condition, and the condition is the outcome of the conduct the narratives have described. The verse's economy is complete: two groups, two adjectives, no further specification. The following three verses will fill in what the adjectives denote.

**Why the Division Comes Before the Description**

The verse's order is instructive. It states the division before describing either group, and the order makes the division the primary fact. A reader is told that there will be two outcomes before being told what they are, and the telling establishes that the outcomes are exhaustive. There is no third group, no intermediate category, no one left unassigned. The sūrah's willingness to state this plainly is characteristic of its final section, which is more direct than the narratives. The verse therefore prepares the reader for what follows by establishing the structure: two groups, two abodes, two durations. The description of each will be given in the following verses, and the structure is what makes the description intelligible.

**The Verse and the Reader's Position**

The verse describes a scene the reader is not yet in, and the description's function is to place him. He is currently in a period when speech is possible, when arguments can be made, and when the assignment has not been made. The verse tells him that this period ends and that when it ends the assignment will be fixed. The sūrah's method throughout has been to make the reader aware of which side of a boundary he is standing on, and this verse makes the boundary explicit. A person who can speak is a person whose case is not closed. The verse is therefore not a description of the Day so much as a statement about the present, made by describing what the present is not.

****The Verse as the Day's First Description****

This is the first verse in which the sūrah describes the Day's content rather than its timing, and the description begins with what is absent. The choice is significant. A reader expecting a description of judgement, weighing, or reckoning is given silence and a division. The sūrah is therefore establishing the Day's character before its procedures, and the character is what the following verses will fill in. A person who knows that nobody speaks and that the assembled are divided into two groups has been given the framework. The details of the two abodes follow, and the framework is what makes them intelligible. The verse is therefore doing preparatory work, and the work is complete in two clauses. The verse therefore describes the Day by what it removes, and the removal is what gives the division its finality. The verse therefore opens the description of the Day with an absence, and the absence is what makes everything that follows final. The verse therefore describes the Day first by what it takes away, and the taking away is what makes everything after it decisive rather than debatable.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:106

> As for those bound for misery, they will be in the Fire, where they will be sighing and gasping

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Group Named**

The verse takes up the first of the two adjectives the previous verse supplied, and it does so in the order the sūrah has chosen. The miserable are described before the joyful, and the ordering is consistent with the narratives, where destruction has preceded rescue in every account. The sūrah's method throughout has been to state the punishment first and the deliverance second, and the pattern holds here. The verse is therefore not introducing a new topic; it is filling in a structure the previous verse established. A reader who has followed the chapter's shape recognises the movement, and the recognition tells him what to expect: a description of the Fire, then a description of the Garden, then a conclusion drawn from both.

**"In the Fire"**

The verse states the location without elaboration, and the economy is significant. The sūrah has described the punishments of this world in concrete detail — a flood, a wind, a blast, an overturning with stones. Here it names the abode in two words. The commentators note that the description that follows concerns the state of its inhabitants rather than the architecture of the place. The choice is deliberate. The sūrah is not attempting to describe the Fire; it is describing what it is like to be in it. The verse therefore shifts from the physical to the experiential, and the shift is what distinguishes this section from the narratives. What happened in this world was narrated as event; what happens here is narrated as condition.

**"Sighing and Gasping"**

The two words describe breathing, and the commentators explain them as heavy breathing, unceasing weeping, and a state of extreme pain, regret and incurable grief. The choice of respiratory vocabulary is precise. A person sighing and gasping is in physical distress of a specific kind — not wounded, not dying, but unable to breathe normally. The sūrah's use of this description rather than of a description of flames or chains is instructive. It locates the suffering in the body's most involuntary function. A reader cannot imagine away his own breathing, and the description therefore reaches him more directly than a description of external torment would. The verse's two words do more work than a paragraph of imagery.

**The Verse's Restraint**

The verse is short, and its shortness is a choice. The sūrah could have described the Fire at length; the Qurʾān does so elsewhere. Here it gives a location and two verbs. The restraint is consistent with the sūrah's treatment of the unseen throughout: it reports what is needed and declines to elaborate. A reader is told where the group is and what it is doing, and nothing more. The omission leaves the description open in a way that a detailed account would not. A person supplied with specifics can dispute them or dismiss them as exaggeration. A person told that a group is sighing and gasping has nothing to dispute, because nothing has been claimed beyond the state itself.

**What the Description Does Not Say**

The verse does not say why the group is there. The narratives have supplied the reasons — refusal of the messengers, allegiance to custom, cheating in trade, following an unguided command — and the verse does not repeat them. The omission is deliberate and it makes a claim. The assignment has been made and the reasons are behind it; the Day described in the previous verse is not a place where cases are reopened. The sūrah has already established that no one speaks except by permission, and the silence covers the explanation as much as the defence. The verse therefore describes an outcome whose grounds have been given elsewhere, and the reader is expected to supply them from the eighty verses he has just read.

**Why the Verse Comes Before Its Counterpart**

The ordering of the two descriptions is worth holding. The miserable are described first, in a single verse; the joyful are described second, in a verse that adds the phrase about an unfailing gift. The asymmetry is not accidental. The sūrah's method throughout has been to state the warning fully and then the mercy, and the sequence gives the mercy its final position. A reader who encounters the two descriptions in this order holds the second one last, and the sūrah's remaining verses will build on that position. The verse is therefore not only a description of the Fire; it is the first half of a pair whose second half carries the chapter's final word.

****Why the Description Is Experiential****

The verse's choice to describe breathing rather than flames deserves a final word. The sūrah's narratives described this-world punishments in physical detail, and a reader might expect the same treatment here. The verse declines it, and the decline is instructive. A description of external torment can be argued with, dismissed as exaggeration, or held at a distance. A description of sighing and gasping cannot, because every reader knows what his own breathing is. The verse therefore reaches the reader through something he cannot externalise, and the reaching is what makes the description effective. The sūrah's method here is the same one it used throughout: state what is needed for the reader to act, and leave what would serve only his imagination. The verse therefore describes the Fire through the body rather than through the scenery, and the choice is what makes it reach the reader. The verse therefore locates the suffering in breath, which no reader can put at a distance, and the locating is the description's whole method. The verse therefore describes the first group in the fewest words that carry the state, and the fewness is what makes the counterpart verse's addition stand out.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:107

> staying there forever, as long as the heavens and the earth will endure, except what your Lord wills. Surely your Lord does what He intends

**Expanded Commentary**

**"As Long as the Heavens and the Earth Endure"**

The phrase measures the duration, and the commentators note a reading that takes it beyond ordinary time. Al-Bayḍāwī argues that the period described is neither serial time nor time as it exists in the physical realm, but refers to the heavens and earth as the basic setting of the afterlife, citing the statement that on that Day the earth will be changed into other than the earth, and the heavens too (14:48). The reading matters because it takes the phrase out of the category of a long period and places it in the category of a permanent condition. The sūrah's wording supports the reading: the measure is not a number of years but the endurance of the frame in which the state exists.

**The Exception Clause**

The phrase *except what your Lord wills* qualifies the duration, and the qualification is the verse's most discussed feature. The commentators differ over its scope, and the difference is substantive rather than merely technical. One reading takes it as a statement of God's unrestricted power — the duration is as stated, and the exception preserves the principle that nothing binds Him. Another takes it as indicating that the Fire's duration is not identical with the Garden's. The sūrah does not settle the question, and the unsettled clause is characteristic of its treatment of the unseen. A reader is given a duration and a qualification, and the relation between them is left to the theological discussion the verse has generated rather than resolved in the text.

**"Your Lord Does What He Intends"**

The closing statement resolves the exception in the direction of divine freedom, and the commentators note its function. The verse does not specify what God wills; it states that He does what He wills. The formulation places the matter beyond human determination without leaving it arbitrary, because the same sūrah has stated repeatedly that God does not wrong anyone. The verse is therefore asserting freedom within a character already established. A reader who has followed the chapter knows that the God described here is the one who sent messengers, gave warnings, and did not destroy towns while their people were reforming. The statement of unrestricted will is made about that God, and the context is what keeps it from becoming a licence.

**The Verse and the Debate About the Fire's End**

The commentators record that Ibn Taymiyyah read the wording of this verse as indicating that the Fire itself will one day cease to exist, though not the Garden, and that the same position has been held by other Islamic authorities, particularly among the Sufis. The position rests on the exception clause and on the asymmetry between this verse and the next, where the Garden's giving is described as unfailing. The sūrah does not state the position, and the report should be held as a reading rather than as a claim of the text. What the verse supports directly is that the duration is expressed with a qualification here and without a corresponding qualification of the gift in the following verse. The difference is real; the inference drawn from it is a matter of interpretation.

**What the Verse Establishes About Duration**

Setting the debate aside, the verse makes one claim plainly: the state of the miserable group is not temporary in any ordinary sense. The measure given is the endurance of the heavens and the earth, which is the longest measure available in the sūrah's vocabulary. The verse is therefore not describing a punishment with a term. It is describing a condition coextensive with the frame of existence itself. The sūrah's willingness to state this plainly is consistent with its treatment throughout, where the outcomes are described without softening. A reader is given the duration as the sūrah states it, and the exception clause is given alongside it, and the two are not reconciled in the text.

**Why the Exception Appears in Both Verses**

The same clause appears in the following verse about the Garden, and its presence in both is significant. If the clause distinguished the two abodes, it would appear in one. Its appearance in both establishes that it functions as a general reservation of divine freedom rather than as a differentiating marker. The verse's closing statement supports this reading: your Lord does what He intends. The sūrah is therefore making a claim about the relation between stated outcomes and divine will that applies to both groups equally. A reader is told that the descriptions are true and that they are not constraints on God, and the two claims are held together by the exception appearing twice.

****What the Verse Requires of a Reader****

The verse asks a reader to hold two claims that are not easily held together. The duration is stated in the longest terms available, and it is qualified by an exception whose scope is not defined. The sūrah does not reconcile them, and the unreconciled pairing is what the verse requires. A reader who resolves the tension by discarding the exception has lost the claim about divine freedom. A reader who resolves it by treating the duration as uncertain has lost the warning. The verse holds both, and the holding is the sūrah's characteristic method with the unseen: state what is revealed, mark what is not, and leave the relation between them to the reader's honesty rather than to a formula. The verse therefore leaves the reader with a duration stated and a freedom preserved, and asks him to hold both.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:108

> And as for those destined to joy, they will be in Paradise, staying there forever, as long as the heavens and the earth will endure, except what your Lord wills—a ˹generous˺ giving, without end

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Group Named**

The verse takes up the second adjective from two verses earlier, and its structure mirrors the previous account. The joyful are in the Garden; the duration is stated in the same words; the exception clause is repeated. The mirroring is exact enough to make the differences stand out, and the sūrah has arranged it that way. A reader comparing the two verses finds the same frame with one addition, and the addition is the verse's whole point. The sūrah's method throughout has been to establish a pattern and then mark the variation, and here the variation is a single phrase at the end. The mirroring is therefore not repetition for its own sake; it is the technique by which the final phrase is given its weight.
