It is a statement made by someone who knows how the previous five courses ended, addressed to people who have heard the accounts. The verse's power lies entirely in the unstated half, and the unstated half is the chapter.

****Why the Chapter Ends Its Argument Here****

The verse is the last substantive statement the chapter makes to its opponents, and the decision to stop is deliberate. A hundred and twenty verses of narrative, warning, description and command have preceded it, and nothing further is added. The sūrah has shown the same transition in the Shuʿayb narrative, where the messenger abandoned argument and stated the two positions. It follows that teaching a reader when to stop arguing, and the teaching is as much a part of the chapter's instruction as the commands. A person who has made his case and been refused has done what he was sent to do, and the remaining task is patience.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:122

> And wait! Surely we ˹too˺ are waiting.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shortest Statement in the Chapter's Close**

The verse is two clauses, and it completes the message the previous verse began. The commentators note that these statements function as warnings and threats against the Makkan idolaters. The verse's brevity is not a diminishment; it is the point. After a hundred and twenty-one verses, the sūrah's final word to its opponents is an instruction to wait and a statement that the believers are waiting too. The economy is what gives the verse its force. A longer statement would be an argument, and the chapter has finished arguing. A statement this short is not an argument; it is a posture, and the posture is what the verse communicates.

**A Shared Waiting**

The verse's most distinctive feature is the word *too*. The opponents are told to wait, and the believers state that they are also waiting. The sūrah has used the same formula in the Shuʿayb narrative, where the prophet told his people to watch and added that he was watching with them. The shared posture removes any suggestion that one side holds an advantage. Both are waiting for the same event, and neither knows its time — the chapter has stated that the Day is delayed for a fixed term and that the term is known only to God. The verse is therefore placing both parties in an identical relation to the future, and the identity is what makes the statement a challenge rather than a threat.

**Why Waiting Is the Final Posture**

The verse identifies waiting as the appropriate stance for a person who has stated his position and cannot alter the outcome's timing. The sūrah has established the elements: the Day is delayed for a fixed term, the term is known to God, the outcome is settled, and the payment will be full. Given those elements, waiting is not passivity. It is the only accurate response. A person who continues to argue after the argument has been refused is not strengthening his case. A person who waits is acknowledging what the chapter has stated. The verse is therefore identifying a posture that follows from the chapter's claims rather than adding a new one, and the identification is the sūrah's final instruction about conduct.

**The Verse and the Sūrah's Account of Time**

The verse's placement completes the chapter's treatment of time. The narratives showed destructions arriving at fixed terms — the oven, the three days, the morning. The doctrinal section stated that the Day is delayed for a fixed term. The verse states that both parties are waiting inside that term. The three elements together describe the reader's situation precisely: he is in a period whose length is set, whose end is certain, and whose content is his own conduct. The verse is therefore not a statement about the opponents so much as a description of the interval the reader occupies. The opponents are addressed; the reader is located.

**What the Verse Does Not Claim**

The verse does not say that the believers' waiting will be rewarded, and it does not say that the opponents' waiting will end badly. Both claims have been made elsewhere in the chapter, and the verse does not repeat them. The omission is significant because it keeps the verse from being a prediction. It is a statement of posture, and the posture is the same for both parties. The sūrah's restraint here is characteristic: it states what is being done and leaves the consequence to the material already given. A reader who wants the consequence has it in the previous hundred verses. A reader who wants the posture has it here, in four words.

**Why the Verse Ends the Message**

The message to the opponents closes here, and the closing is deliberate. The sūrah does not add a final warning, a last appeal, or a summary of the charges. It ends the message with a statement of shared waiting, and then turns to the Prophet and the believers for the chapter's last verse. The arrangement tells the reader what the sūrah regards as complete. The case has been made, the positions have been stated, and nothing further is owed to those who have refused. What follows from this is that the end of the argument, and the ending is not a withdrawal. It is the recognition that the argument has reached the point the chapter described in the Shuʿayb narrative, where further speech does not help.

****What Waiting Requires of the One Who Waits****

The verse names a posture without describing it, and the chapter supplies the description. Waiting here is not idleness. The commands given four verses earlier — steadfastness, non-inclination, prayer, patience — are what a person does while waiting. The verse therefore presupposes them, and the presupposition is what keeps the posture from being passive. A person who is steadfast, who does not incline, who prays at the appointed hours and is patient, is waiting in the sense the verse means. A person who is doing none of these is not waiting; he is simply passing time. The verse's brevity is possible because the chapter has already specified the content of the wait.

****The Verse and the Chapter's Final Movement****

The verse closes the message to the opponents, and the closing is followed by a single verse addressed to the Prophet and the believers. The arrangement is the chapter's final structure: the case is stated, the positions are declared, the waiting is shared, and then the chapter turns inward for its last statement. The turn is significant. The sūrah's last word is not to those who refused but to those who accepted, and the word is about worship, trust, and the assurance that nothing is overlooked. The consequence is that the hinge of that turn, and its four words are what allow the chapter to end where it does.

## Sūrah Hūd 11:123

> To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the knowledge of what is hidden in the heavens and the earth. And to Him all matters are returned. So worship Him and put your trust in Him. And your Lord is never unaware of what you do

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chapter's Last Verse and Its Shape**

The verse contains four statements, and their arrangement is the sūrah's final method. First: the unseen belongs to God. Second: all matters return to Him. Third: worship Him and trust Him. Fourth: your Lord is not unaware of what you do. The sequence moves from knowledge to return to command to assurance. The sūrah has used this shape throughout — a statement about God, a statement about the outcome, a command, and a closing ground. The verse is therefore not a summary appended to the chapter. It is the chapter's characteristic structure at its most compressed, and the compression is possible because the hundred and twenty-two verses before it have supplied the material.

**"To Allah Belongs the Knowledge of the Unseen"**

The opening statement establishes a limit, and the limit is the sūrah's final word on what it has withheld. The chapter has described the unseen throughout — the Day, the two abodes, the fixed term — and has consistently declined to specify what cannot be known. This verse states the reason for the restraint. The unseen belongs to God, and the belonging is exclusive. The sūrah is therefore not being coy about details it could supply. It is stating that the details are not available to anyone but God, and that the withholding is a fact about knowledge rather than a choice about presentation. The verse closes the chapter's treatment of the unseen by naming its owner.

**"To Him All Matters Are Returned"**

The second statement concerns the outcome, and the word for returned is the one the sūrah used in its fourth verse, where humanity's return to God was stated as the ground of the warning. The recurrence brackets the chapter: the fourth verse announced the return and the last verse confirms it. The commentators note that the phrase summarises the sūrah's message to the Prophet and, by extension, to all believers. The verse's placement of the return before the command is significant. A person told to worship and trust needs to know where matters end, and the verse supplies that before supplying the instruction. The order is the sūrah's standard sequence, and its use in the final verse makes the verse a complete statement rather than an exhortation.

**"So Worship Him and Put Your Trust in Him"**

The command has two parts, and the commentators note that the call to trust is echoed throughout the Qurʾān — that God loves those who trust (3:159), and that believers are to trust in God (5:23). The pairing is significant. Worship is the outward act; trust is the inward reliance. The sūrah has commanded both separately earlier — the opening verse commanded worship, and the Shuʿayb narrative closed with a prophet saying *in Him I trust and to Him I turn*. The final verse combines them in a single instruction, and the combination is the chapter's requirement in its shortest form. A reader is given the whole demand in six words, and the six words are the same demand the chapter has been stating since its beginning.

**What Trust Means Here**

The commentators record a definition of trust from the Sufi tradition: trust is present when the heart is at rest because of the Lord's guarantee. The definition is worth holding because it connects the command to the chapter's material. A person who has read a hundred verses of destroyed nations, a fixed term, and a full payment has been given the guarantee. The verse asks him to rest in it. The sūrah's method throughout has been to supply the ground before issuing the command, and here the ground has been supplied across the whole chapter. The command to trust is therefore not a demand for a feeling the reader has not been given reasons for. It is a request that he accept what the chapter has shown.

**"Your Lord Is Never Unaware of What You Do"**

The closing statement returns to the formula the sūrah has used repeatedly, and its final position is significant. The commentators note the same assurance elsewhere, and the verse places it last. The statement does two things at once. It assures the believer that his conduct is seen and will not be lost, which is the ground of the patience commanded four verses earlier. And it warns the opponent that nothing has been overlooked, which is the ground of the payment declared in verse 111. The verse addresses both audiences in a single clause, and the addressing is the chapter's final act. A reader is left with the knowledge that whatever he has done in the interval is known, and the knowledge is what the chapter has been building toward.

**Why the Chapter Ends Here**

The verse closes the sūrah without a narrative, a warning or a story. It ends with a statement about God, a command, and an assurance. The choice is the chapter's final method: the material is accumulated through narrative and doctrine, and the ending is the requirement in its plainest form. A reader who has followed the chapter arrives at this verse holding the destroyed nations, the fixed term, the two abodes, and the four commands. The verse does not add to any of it. It states where knowledge lies, where matters return, what is required, and that nothing is missed. The chapter ends as it began — with God, with a command, and with the assurance that the return is real.
